## Prologue Nantes, 1789 / 序幕 南特，1789年
===Original===



===Chinese===

序幕 南特，1789年

### I / 第一节

### I /

### I /

### I / 
===Original===

“Tyrant! tyrant! tyrant!”
It was Pierre who spoke, his voice was hardly raised above a murmur, but there was such an intensity of passion expressed in his face, in the fingers of his hand which closed slowly and convulsively as if they were clutching the throat of a struggling viper, there was so much hate in those muttered words, so much power, such compelling and awesome determination that an ominous silence fell upon the village lads and the men who sat with him in the low narrow room of the auberge des Trois Vertus.
Even the man in the tattered coat and threadbare breeches, who﻿—perched upon the centre table﻿—had been haranguing the company on the subject of the Rights of Man, paused in his peroration and looked down on Pierre half afraid of that fierce flame of passionate hate which his own words had helped to kindle.
The silence, however, had only lasted a few moments, the next Pierre was on his feet, and a cry like that of a bull in a slaughterhouse escaped his throat.
“In the name of God!” he shouted, “let us cease all that senseless talking. Haven’t we planned enough and talked enough to satisfy our puling consciences? The time has come to strike, *mes amis*, to strike I say, to strike at those cursed aristocrats, who have made us what we are﻿—ignorant, wretched, downtrodden﻿—senseless clods to work our fingers to the bone, our bodies till they break so that they may wallow in their pleasures and their luxuries! Strike, I say!” he reiterated while his eyes glowed and his breath came and went through his throat with a hissing sound. “Strike! as the men and women struck in Paris on that great day in July. To them the Bastille stood for tyranny, and they struck at it as they would at the head of a tyrant﻿—and the tyrant cowered, cringed, made terms﻿—he was frightened at the wrath of the people! That is what happened in Paris! That is what must happen in Nantes. The château of the duc de Kernogan is our Bastille! Let us strike at it tonight, and if the arrogant aristocrat resists, we’ll raze his house to the ground. The hour, the day, the darkness are all propitious. The arrangements hold good. The neighbours are ready. Strike, I say!”

===Chinese===

“暴君！暴君！暴君！”
说话的是皮埃尔（Pierre）。他的声音虽然压得极低，近乎喃喃自语，但那张脸上所流露出的炽烈激情，以及他那缓缓而痉挛般收紧的手指——仿佛正死死掐住一条垂死挣扎的毒蛇咽喉——还有那低语声中饱含的浓烈仇恨、强大力量与令人敬畏的决绝，使得“三德”客栈（auberge des Trois Vertus）低矮狭窄的屋子里，同坐的一众乡村青年与汉子们陷入了一片不祥的死寂。
甚至连那个身穿破烂上衣、磨损马裤，高踞在正中桌子上向众人高谈阔论“人权”的男子，也在演讲的高潮处戛然而止，低头看着皮埃尔，对自己这番话所煽动起来的狂暴仇恨烈焰感到一丝畏惧。
然而沉默只持续了片刻，下一瞬皮埃尔便霍然起身，喉咙里迸发出一声犹如屠宰场公牛般的狂吼。
“奉上帝之名！”他厉声大喊，“别再废话连篇了！难道我们密谋得还不够、说得还不够抚慰我们懦弱的良心吗？动手的时刻到了，朋友们（mes amis），我说动手！向那些该死的贵族佬（aristos）动手！是他们把我们糟践成今天这副模样——愚昧、凄惨、受尽践踏——成了任由驱使的木石，累断了手指、折断了脊梁，只为了让他们在享乐与奢靡中纵情声色！动手，我说！”他双目圆睁，呼吸在喉间发出嘶嘶的风声，再次吼道，“动手！就像巴黎（Paris）的男男女女在七月那个伟大的日子里动手一样！在他们眼中，巴士底狱就是暴政的化身，他们砸向它，就像砍下暴君的头颅——暴君退缩了，屈服了，妥协了——他在人民的雷霆之怒面前吓破了胆！这便是在巴黎发生的事！这也正是必须在南特（Nantes）重演的事！德·凯尔诺冈公爵（M. le duc de Kernogan）的城堡就是我们的巴士底狱！今晚我们就去砸烂它！如果那个傲慢的贵族佬胆敢反抗，我们就把他的宅邸夷为平地！良辰、吉日、黑夜，一切皆已齐备！部署分毫不差！乡邻们早已严阵以待！动手，我说！”

===Original===

He brought his hard fist crashing down upon the table, so that mugs and bottles rattled: his enthusiasm had fired all his hearers: his hatred and his lust of revenge had done more in five minutes than all the tirades of the agitators sent down from Paris to instil revolutionary ideas into the slow-moving brains of village lads.
“Who will give the signal?” queried one of the older men quietly.
“I will!” came a lusty response from Pierre.
He strode to the door, and all the men jumped to their feet, ready to follow him, dragged into this hotheaded venture by the mere force of one man’s towering passion. They followed Pierre like sheep﻿—sheep that have momentarily become intoxicated﻿—sheep that have become fierce﻿—a strange sight truly﻿—and yet one that the man in the tattered coat who had done so much speechifying lately, watched with eager interest and presently related with great wealth of detail to M. de Mirabeau the champion of the people.
“It all came about through the death of a pair of pigeons,” he said.
The death of the pigeons, however, was only the spark which set all these turbulent passions ablaze. They had been smouldering for half a century, and had been ready to burst into flames for the past decade.
Antoine Melun, the wheelwright, who was to have married Louise, Pierre’s sister, had trapped a pair of pigeons in the woods of M. le duc de Kernogan. He had done it to assert his rights as a man﻿—he did not want the pigeons. Though he was a poor man, he was no poorer than hundreds of peasants for miles around: but he paid imposts and taxes until every particle of profit which he gleaned from his miserable little plot of land went into the hands of the collectors, whilst M. le duc de Kernogan paid not one sou towards the costs of the State, and he had to live on what was left of his own rye and wheat after M. le duc’s pigeons had had their fill of them.
Antoine Melun did not want to eat the pigeons which he had trapped, but he desired to let M. le duc de Kernogan know that God and Nature had never intended all the beasts and birds of the woods to be the exclusive property of one man, rather than another. So he trapped and killed two pigeons and M. le duc’s head-bailiff caught him in the act of carrying those pigeons home.
Whereupon Antoine was arrested for poaching and thieving: he was tried at Nantes under the presidency of M.

===Chinese===

他那坚硬的拳头重重砸在桌面上，杯盘瓶罐震得叮当作响。他的狂热点燃了在场的所有听众，他在五分钟内激起的仇恨与复仇渴望，胜过了巴黎派来的煽动者们为了把革命思想灌输进乡下青年迟钝脑子而发表的所有长篇大论。
“谁来发信号？”一位上了年纪的汉子轻声问道。
“我来！”皮埃尔给出了响亮的回答。
他大步向门口走去，所有人都跳起身来准备跟随他，仅凭一人排山倒海般的激情，便将他们全都卷入了这场狂热的冒险。他们像羊群一样跟在皮埃尔身后——一时喝醉了的羊群，变得凶暴的羊群——委实是一副奇景。然而，近日大出风头的那位身穿破外套的演说家却兴致勃勃地注视着这一幕，并在不久后绘声绘色地向人民的卫士德·米拉波先生（M. de Mirabeau）详加转述。
“这一切全由打死两只鸽子而起。”他说。
然而，鸽子的死不过是点燃这股汹涌怒潮的导火索。这些怒火已经在地下暗暗燃烧了半个世纪，并在过去的十年里随时准备破土喷薄。
车匠安托万·默朗（Antoine Melun）原本要迎娶皮埃尔的妹妹路易丝（Louise），他在德·凯尔诺冈公爵的林地里下套捕了两只鸽子。他这样做是为了维护自己身为人的权利——他并非真的想要那两只鸽子。虽然他家境贫寒，但比起方圆数英里内的几百个农户也并不更穷。然而，他必须缴纳繁重的苛捐杂税，直到他那块可怜的瘠地所榨出的每一分收益都落入收税官之手，而德·凯尔诺冈公爵却不必为国家开支掏一个铜板；他自己只能靠公爵的鸽子吃饱之后剩下的一点黑麦和小麦度日。
安托万·默朗并不想吃掉捕来的鸽子，但他想让德·凯尔诺冈公爵明白，上帝和大自然绝未打算让林中所有的走兽飞禽沦为某个人的私有财产，剥夺他人的份额。因此他设套捕杀了双鸽，公爵的总管雅克·拉布吕尼埃（Jacques Labrunière）当场将拎着鸽子回家的他抓了个现行。
于是安托万以偷猎和盗窃罪名被捕。他在南特受审，主审人正是德·

===Original===

le duc de Kernogan, and ten minutes ago, while the man in the tattered coat was declaiming to a number of peasant lads in the coffee-room of the auberge des Trois Vertus on the subject of their rights as men and citizens, someone brought the news that Antoine Melun had just been condemned to death and would be hanged on the morrow.
That was the spark which had fanned Pierre Adet’s hatred of the aristocrats to a veritable conflagration: the news of Antoine Melun’s fate was the bleat which rallied all those human sheep around their leader. For Pierre had naturally become their leader because his hatred of M. le duc was more tangible, more powerful than theirs. Pierre had had more education than they. His father, Jean Adet the miller, had sent him to a school in Nantes, and when Pierre came home M. le curé of Vertou took an interest in him and taught him all he knew himself﻿—which was not much﻿—in the way of philosophy and the classics. But later on Pierre took to reading the writings of M. Jean-Jacques Rousseau and soon knew the *Contrat Social* almost by heart. He had also read the articles in M. Marat’s newspaper *L’ami du Peuple*! and, like Antoine Melun, the wheelwright, he had got it into his head that it was not God, nor yet Nature who had intended one man to starve while another gorged himself on all the good things of this world.
He did not, however, speak of these matters, either to his father or to his sister or to M. le curé, but he brooded over them, and when the price of bread rose to four sous he muttered curses against M. le duc de Kernogan, and when famine prices ruled throughout the district those curses became overt threats; and by the time that the pinch of hunger was felt in Vertou Pierre’s passion of fury against the duc de Kernogan had turned to a frenzy of hate against the entire noblesse of France.
Still he said nothing to his father, nothing to his mother and sister. But his father knew. Old Jean would watch the storm-clouds which gathered on Pierre’s lowering brow; he heard the muttered curses which escaped from Pierre’s lips whilst he worked for the liege-lord whom he hated. But Jean was a wise man and knew how useless it is to put out a feeble hand in order to stem the onrush of a torrent. He knew how useless are the words of wisdom from an old man to quell the rebellious spirit of the young.
Jean was on the watch.

===Chinese===

凯尔诺冈公爵。就在十分钟前，当那个穿破外套的男子还在“三德”客栈的茶室里向一群乡下青年高谈阔论他们作为人和公民的权利时，有人传来了消息：安托万·默朗刚刚被判处死刑，次日便要执行绞刑。
这正是将皮埃尔·阿代（Pierre Adet）对贵族的仇恨彻底煽动成燎原大火的火星；安托万·默朗的悲惨下场，成了将这些迷途羔羊聚拢在领头人周围的号角。皮埃尔顺理成章地成为了他们的领袖，因为他对公爵的仇恨比旁人更加深切、更为强烈。皮埃尔受过比他们更多的教育。他的父亲，磨坊主让·阿代（Jean Adet）曾送他去南特的一所学校读书；皮埃尔回乡后，韦尔图（Vertou）的本堂神甫对他青睐有加，把自己所知的那点哲学和古典学识尽数相授——虽然那本就不多。但后来皮埃尔开始阅读让-雅克·卢梭（Jean-Jacques Rousseau）先生的著作，很快就能将《社会契约论》（Contrat Social）背得滚瓜烂熟。他还读过马拉（Marat）先生创办的《人民之友报》（L'Ami du Peuple）上的文章！如同车匠安托万·默朗一样，他也认定绝非上帝或大自然注定要让一人挨饿，而让另一人穷奢极欲享尽世间繁华。
然而，他从未对父亲、妹妹或是本堂神甫谈起这些心思，只是独自在心头反复咀嚼。当面包价格涨到四个苏时，他暗暗咒骂德·凯尔诺冈公爵；当饥荒带来的粮价暴涨席卷全郡时，那些诅咒变成了公开的威胁；而等到韦尔图也感受到了饥馑的逼迫时，皮埃尔对凯尔诺冈公爵的满腔怒火，已经演变成对整个法兰西贵族阶层的疯狂仇恨。
即便如此，他依然对父亲只字不提，对母亲和妹妹也守口如瓶。但他的父亲心里明白。老让会看着皮埃尔阴云密布的紧锁眉头；在皮埃尔为那个他痛恨的封建领主服劳役时，老让听到了儿子嘴角溢出的低沉咒骂。然而让是个明白人，深知伸出一只衰弱的手去阻挡滔滔洪流是多么徒劳。他深知老者充满智慧的规劝，根本无法抚平年轻人躁动叛逆的心魂。
让时刻警惕地关注着这一切。

===Original===

And evening after evening when the work on the farm was done, Pierre would sit in the small low room of the auberge with other lads from the village talking, talking of their wrongs, of the arrogance of the aristocrats, the sins of M. le duc and his family, the evil conduct of the King and the immorality of the Queen: and men in ragged coats and tattered breeches came in from Nantes, and even from Paris, in order to harangue these village lads and told them yet further tales of innumerable wrongs suffered by the people at the hands of the aristos, and stuffed their heads full of schemes for getting even once and for all with those men and women who fattened on the sweat of the poor and drew their luxury from the hunger and the toil of the peasantry.
Pierre sucked in these harangues through every pore: they were meat and drink to him. His hate and passions fed upon these effusions till his whole being was consumed by a maddening desire for reprisals, for vengeance﻿—for the lust of triumph over those whom he had been taught to fear.
And in the low, narrow room of the auberge the fevered heads of village lads were bent together in conclave, and the ravings and shoutings of a while ago were changed to whisperings and low murmurings behind barred doors and shuttered windows. Men exchanged cryptic greetings when they met in the village street, enigmatical signs passed between them while they worked: strangers came and went at dead of night to and from the neighbouring villages. M. le duc’s overseers saw nothing, heard nothing, guessed nothing. M. le curé saw much and old Jean Adet guessed a great deal, but they said nothing, for nothing then would have availed.
Then came the catastrophe.

===Chinese===

年复一年，日复一日，每当田间农活收工后的傍晚，皮埃尔总会与村里的其他年轻人聚在客栈矮小逼仄的房间里，谈论着，不停谈论着他们所遭受的不公、贵族的专横、公爵一家的罪孽、国王的倒行逆施以及王后的骄奢淫逸；衣衫褴褛、裤腿破烂的人从南特甚至巴黎赶来，向这些乡下小伙发表演讲，讲述人民在贵族佬手下蒙受的数不清的冤屈，并给他们的脑子里灌满了彻底清算那些吸吮穷人血汗、以农民饥寒劳苦滋养自身奢华的吸血鬼的计划。
皮埃尔的每一个毛孔都在吮吸着这些演说：它们成了他的精神食粮。他的仇恨与激情在这些激烈言辞的浇灌下滋长，直到他的整个身心都被一种疯狂的报复与复仇渴望吞噬——那是企图战胜那些他自幼被教导去惧怕之人的征服欲望。
在客栈低矮狭窄的屋内，乡下小伙子们狂热的头颅聚在一起密谋，适才的叫嚷与呐喊化作了紧锁门窗背后的耳语与低诉。人们在村中街巷相遇时互致隐晦的问候，劳作时彼此交换着神秘的暗号；在夜深人静之际，陌生人在附近的村落之间来去穿梭。公爵的管事们视而不见、充耳不闻，未曾察觉分毫。本堂神甫看得分明，老让·阿代也猜出了七八分，但他们皆缄口不言，因为此时此刻任何劝阻都已无济于事。
紧接着，灾难降临了。

### II / 第二节

### II /

### II / 
===Original===

Pierre pushed open the outer door of the auberge des Trois Vertus and stepped out under the porch. A gust of wind caught him in the face. The night, so the chronicles of the time tell us, was as dark as pitch: on ahead lay the lights of the city flickering in the gale: to the left the wide tawny ribbon of the river wound its turbulent course toward the ocean, the booming of the waters swollen by the recent melting of the snow sounded like the weird echoes of invisible cannons far away.
Without hesitation Pierre advanced. His little troop followed him in silence. They were a little sobered now that they came out into the open and that the fumes of cider and of hot, perspiring humanity no longer obscured their vision or inflamed their brain.
They knew whither Pierre was going. It had all been prearranged﻿—throughout this past summer, in the musty parlour of the auberge, behind barred doors and shuttered windows﻿—all they had to do was to follow Pierre, whom they had tacitly chosen as their leader. They walked on behind him, their hands buried in the pockets of their thin, tattered breeches, their heads bent forward against the fury of the gale.
Pierre made straight for the mill﻿—his home﻿—where his father lived and where Louise was even now crying her eyes out because Antoine Melun, her sweetheart, had been condemned to be hanged for killing two pigeons.
At the back of the mill was the dwelling house and beyond it a small farmery, for Jean Adet owned a little bit of land and would have been fairly well off if the taxes had not swallowed up all the money that he made out of the sale of his rye and his hay. Just here the ground rose sharply to a little hillock which dominated the flat valley of the Loire and commanded a fine view over the more distant villages.
Pierre skirted the mill and without looking round to see if the others followed him he struck squarely to the right up a narrow lane bordered by tall poplars, and which led upwards to the summit of the little hillock around which clustered the tumbledown barns of his father’s farmery.
The gale lashed the straight, tall stems of the poplars until they bent nearly double, and each tiny bare twig sighed and whispered as if in pain. Pierre strode on and the others followed in silence.

===Chinese===

皮埃尔推开“三德”客栈的外门，走到门廊下。一阵狂风迎面扑来。据当年的编年史记载，那一夜漆黑如墨：正前方，城里的灯火在狂风中明灭不定；左侧，泛着黄褐色浊浪的卢瓦尔河（the Loire）犹如一条宽阔的绸带，蜿蜒奔腾向大洋，初融积雪汇成的滚滚波涛发出轰鸣，仿佛远方隐形大炮传来的怪异回响。
皮埃尔毫不犹豫地向前走去。他的那一小队人马默默跟在身后。走到露天旷野之后，众人都清醒了几分，苹果酒的气息与闷热汗臭不再遮蔽双眼、烧灼大脑。
他们知道皮埃尔要去向何方。这一切早已在刚刚过去的夏天里谋划停当——在客栈发霉的客厅里，在紧闭的门窗背后——他们所要做的，就是紧跟他们心照不宣推举的领袖皮埃尔。他们跟在他身后向前迈进，双手插在单薄破烂的裤兜里，低垂着头，顶着暴虐的狂风艰难前行。
皮埃尔径直走向磨坊——那是他的家——他的父亲住在那儿，他的妹妹路易丝此刻正哭肿了双眼，因为她的意中人安托万·默朗只因打了两只鸽子就被判处绞刑。
磨坊后头是住宅，再往后是一个小农庄。让·阿代拥有几亩薄田，要不是捐税榨干了他卖黑麦和干草挣下的每一文钱，他的日子本该过得相当宽裕。地势在此陡然拔高，形成一座小丘陵，俯瞰着卢瓦尔河平缓的河谷，将更远处的村镇尽收眼底。
皮埃尔绕过磨坊，头也不回地径直向右拐进一条夹道高耸白杨的小径。小径通向小丘之巅，他父亲农庄破旧的谷仓正错落散布在那里。
狂风抽打着笔直高耸的白杨树干，几乎将它们压弯了一半，每一根光秃秃的细枝都在痛苦地叹息呜咽。皮埃尔大步前行，其他人沉默地跟随着。

===Original===

They were chilled to the bone under their scanty clothes, but they followed on with grim determination, set teeth, and anger and hate seething in their hearts.
The top of the rising ground was reached. It was pitch dark, and the men when they halted fell up against one another trying to get a foothold on the sodden ground. But Pierre seemed to have eyes like a cat. He only paused one moment to get his bearings, then﻿—still without a word﻿—he set to work. A large barn and a group of small circular straw ricks loomed like solid masses out of the darkness﻿—black, silhouetted against the black of the stormy sky. Pierre turned toward the barn: those of his comrades who were in the forefront of the small crowd saw him disappearing inside one of those solid shadowy masses that looked so ghostlike in the night.
Anon those who watched and who happened to be facing the interior of the barn saw sparks from a tinder flying in every direction: the next moment they could see Pierre himself quite clearly. He was standing in the middle of the barn and intent on lighting a roughly-fashioned torch with his tinder: soon the resin caught a spark and Pierre held the torch inclined toward the ground so that the flames could lick their way up the shaft. The flickering light cast a weird glow and deep grotesque shadows upon the face and figure of the young man. His hair, lanky and dishevelled, fell over his eyes; his mouth and jaw, illumined from below by the torch, looked unnaturally large, and showed his teeth gleaming white, like the fangs of a beast of prey. His shirt was torn open at the neck, and the sleeves of his coat were rolled up to the elbow. He seemed not to feel either the cold from without or the scorching heat of the flaming torch in his hand. But he worked deliberately and calmly, without haste or febrile movements: grim determination held his excitement in check.
At last his work was done. The men who had pressed forward, in order to watch him, fell back as he advanced, torch in hand. They knew exactly what he was going to do, they had thought it all out, planned it, spoken of it till even their unimaginative minds had visualised this coming scene with absolutely realistic perception.

===Chinese===

他们衣衫褴褛，浑身冻得冰凉刺骨，但依然怀着冷酷的决绝紧随其后，咬紧牙关，胸膛里翻腾着滔天的愤怒与仇恨。
他们登上了丘陵顶端。夜色漆黑如墨，汉子们停步时在湿漉漉的烂泥地上站立不稳，东倒西歪地撞在一处。但皮埃尔仿佛长了一双夜猫般的眼睛。他只停顿了片刻辨明方向，随后便一言不发地动起手来。一座大谷仓和几座圆形的麦草垛在黑暗中犹如凝固的暗影拔地而起——黑压压的轮廓映衬在风雨交加的夜空下。皮埃尔朝谷仓走去：走在队伍最前面的同伴看见他钻进了那些在黑夜中宛如幽灵般凝重的阴影之中。
不久，那些正对谷仓内部望去的人看到了火石击出的火星四处迸溅：下一刻，他们便清楚地看到了皮埃尔的身影。他伫立在谷仓中央，正专注地用火石引燃一支粗制火把。很快树脂吃到了火星，皮埃尔将火把斜倾向地面，好让火舌顺着木柄舔舐向上。跳跃的火光在这个年轻人的脸庞与身躯上映出了诡谲的光芒与狰狞可怖的深重暗影。他那干瘪散乱的头发垂落在眼前；火把从下方照亮了他的嘴唇与下颌，显得异乎寻常的巨大，露出了雪白的牙齿，犹如野兽的獠牙。他的衬衣敞着领口，外衣的袖子卷到了肘部。他似乎既感觉不到外界的严寒，也感受不到手中烈火的灼热。他从容冷静地忙碌着，没有丝毫仓皇或躁动：冷酷坚毅的意志压制住了胸中的亢奋。
火把终于做好了。适才挤上前去张望的人群在他举着火把走出来时纷纷后退。他们心知肚明他要做什么，他们早已反复思忖、细细谋划、口口相传，甚至连他们最缺乏想象力的头脑，也早已分毫不差地预见到了眼前这即将上演的一幕。

===Original===

And yet, now that the supreme hour had come, now that they saw Pierre﻿—torch in hand﻿—prepared to give the signal which would set ablaze the seething revolt of the countryside, their heart seemed to stop its beating within their body; they held their breath, their toil-worn hands went up to their throats as if to repress that awful choking sensation which was so like fear.
But Pierre had no such hesitations; if his breath seemed to choke him as it reached his throat, if it escaped through his set teeth with a strange whistling sound, it was because his excitement was that of a hungry beast who had sighted his prey and is ready to spring and devour. His hand did not shake, his step was firm: the gusts of wind caught the flame of his torch till the sparks flew in every direction and scorched his hair and his hands, and while the others recoiled he strode on, to the straw-rick that was nearest.
For one moment he held the torch aloft. There was triumph now in his eyes, in his whole attitude. He looked out into the darkness far away which seemed all the more impenetrable beyond the restricted circle of flickering torchlight. It seemed as if he would wrest from that inky blackness all the secrets which it hid﻿—all the enthusiasm, the excitement, the passions, the hatred which he would have liked to set ablaze as he would the straw-ricks anon.
“Are you ready, *mes amis*?” he called.
“Aye! aye!” they replied﻿—not gaily, not lustily, but calmly and under their breath.
One touch of the torch and the dry straw began to crackle; a gust of wind caught the flame and whipped it into energy; it crept up the side of the little rick like a glowing python that wraps its prey in its embrace. Another gust of wind, and the flame leapt joyously up to the pinnacle of the rick, and sent forth other tongues to lick and to lick, to enfold the straw, to devour, to consume.
But Pierre did not wait to see the consummation of his work of destruction. Already with a few rapid strides he had reached his father’s second straw-rick, and this too he set alight, and then another and another, until six blazing furnaces sent their lurid tongues of flames, twisting and twirling, writhing and hissing through the stormy night.

===Chinese===

然而，当这决定命运的时刻真正降临，当他们看见皮埃尔手擎火把、准备发出那将引爆整个乡野起义狂潮的信号时，他们的心脏仿佛在胸膛里停止了跳动；他们屏住呼吸，粗糙布满老茧的手按在喉头，仿佛要压制住那酷似恐惧的窒息感。
但皮埃尔毫无犹豫；即便气息在喉间几乎让他窒息，即便呼吸在咬紧的齿缝间发出怪异的嘶鸣，那也是因为他心中涌动着嗜血野兽窥见猎物、正欲扑杀吞噬的狂热。他的手毫不颤抖，步伐坚定不移：狂风卷动火把上的烈焰，火星四处激射，灼痛了他的头发与手背，在旁人纷纷退缩之际，他却大步流星，直奔最近的那座麦草垛。
他将火把高高举起了一瞬。他的双眸与整个身姿中此刻充溢着胜利的光芒。他凝视着远方的黑夜，在摇曳火把照亮的狭小光圈之外，夜幕显得格外深不可测。他仿佛要从这如墨的漆黑中榨出其隐藏的一切秘密——所有他渴望像点燃草垛一样彻底引爆的热血、狂喜、激情与仇恨。
“准备好了吗，朋友们？”他高声喊道。
“好了！好了！”他们答道——并不欢快，也不高昂，而是压低声音平静地应和。
火把轻轻一触，干燥的草屑便噼啪作响；一阵狂风扑来，火苗霍然窜起；它顺着小草垛的边缘蜿蜒向上，宛如一条灼热的巨蟒将猎物紧紧缠绕在怀中。又一阵狂风呼啸而过，烈焰欢腾地蹿上垛顶，喷吐出更多火舌舔舐着、包裹着麦草，贪婪地吞噬、焚毁。
但皮埃尔并未驻足欣赏这毁灭之作的终章。他已然跨着大步冲向了父亲的第二座草垛，将其点燃，紧接着是第三座、第四座……直到六座熊熊燃烧的熔炉将猩红可怖的火舌送入狂暴的夜空，在风雨之中狂舞盘旋、扭曲嘶鸣。

===Original===

Within the space of two minutes the whole summit of the hillock seemed to be ablaze, and Pierre, like a god of fire, torch in hand, seemed to preside over and command a multitude of ever-spreading flames to his will. Excitement had overmastered him now, the lust to destroy was upon him, and excitement had seized all the others too.
There was shouting and cursing, and laughter that sounded mirthless and forced, and calls to Pierre, and oaths of revenge. Memory, like an evil-intentioned witch, was riding invisibly in the darkness, and she touched each seething brain with her fever-giving wand. Every man had an outrage to remember, an injustice to recall, and strong, brown fists were shaken aloft in the direction of the château de Kernogan, whose lights glimmered feebly in the distance beyond the Loire.
“Death to the tyrant! *A la lanterne les aristos!* The people’s hour has come at last! No more starvation! No more injustice! Equality! Liberty! *A mort les aristos!*”
The shouts, the curses, the crackling flames, the howling of the wind, the soughing of the trees, made up a confusion of sounds which seemed hardly of this earth; the blazing ricks, the flickering, red light of the flames had finally transformed the little hillock behind the mill into another Brocken on whose summit witches and devils do of a truth hold their revels.
“*A moi!*” shouted Pierre again, and he threw his torch down upon the ground and once more made for the barn. The others followed him. In the barn were such weapons as these wretched, penniless peasants had managed to collect﻿—scythes, poles, axes, saws, anything that would prove useful for the destruction of the château de Kernogan and the proposed browbeating of M. le duc and his family. All the men trooped in in the wake of Pierre. The entire hillock was now a blaze of light﻿—lurid and red and flickering﻿—alternately teased and fanned and subdued by the gale, so that at times every object stood out clearly cut, every blade of grass, every stone in bold relief, and in the ruts and fissures, every tiny pool of muddy water shimmered like strings of fire-opals: whilst at others, a pall of inky darkness, smoke-laden and impenetrable would lie over the ground and erase the outline of farm-buildings and distant mill and of the pushing and struggling mass of humanity inside the barn.

===Chinese===

不过两分钟光景，整个丘陵之巅便化作一片火海，而皮埃尔手执火把，宛如火神降世，傲然统御、挥斥着无数四下蔓延的烈焰。狂热彻底征服了他，毁灭的欲望攫住了他的灵魂，而狂喜与亢奋也瞬间席卷了在场的每一个人。
人群中爆发出一阵阵呐喊与诅咒，夹杂着毫无笑意、干瘪生硬的狂笑，有人高呼皮埃尔的名字，有人咬牙切齿立下复仇誓言。记忆宛如居心叵测的女巫，在黑暗中无形游荡，用播撒狂热的魔杖轻触每一颗沸腾的头脑。每个人心中都有一桩血泪凌辱难以磨灭，有一桩深仇大恨刻骨铭心；一只只黝黑强健的拳头高高挥向卢瓦尔河彼岸远方凯尔诺冈城堡（château de Kernogan）的方向，那里的灯火正在夜色中微弱地摇曳。
“弄死暴君！把贵族佬吊上路灯杆！〔A la lanterne les aristos !〕人民的时辰终于到了！不再挨饿！不再受屈！平等！自由！处死贵族佬！〔A mort les aristos !〕”
呼喊声、咒骂声、烈火爆裂声、狂风呼啸声与树木呜咽声交织在一处，化作人间罕有的鼎沸声浪；熊熊燃烧的草垛与跳跃赤红的火光，终于将磨坊后的小丘彻底化作了另一座布罗肯峰（Brocken）——群魔乱舞、女巫彻夜狂欢的峰峦。
“跟我来！〔A moi !〕”皮埃尔再次高呼，他把火把摔在地上，转身再次冲入谷仓。其余人等亦步亦趋。谷仓里摆放着这些身无分文的贫苦农夫所能搜集到的一切武器——长柄大镰刀、长棍、利斧、锯子，凡是能用来摧毁凯尔诺冈城堡、恫吓压垮公爵及其家人的物件一应俱全。汉子们尾随皮埃尔蜂拥而入。如今整座丘陵火光冲天，血红、刺目而摇曳，被烈风时而挑逗、时而鼓荡、时而压制；一时间，草木顽石纤毫毕现，深凹车辙与泥泞水洼在强光下泛出宛如火欧泊般的温润微光；然而转瞬之间，被浓烟笼罩的漆黑帷幕又压过大地，吞没了农舍、远方磨坊，以及在谷仓中推搡拥挤的人群轮廓。

===Original===

But Pierre, heedless of light and darkness, of heat or of cold, proceeded quietly and methodically to distribute the primitive implements of warfare to this crowd of ignorant men, who were by now over ready for mischief: and with every weapon which he placed in willing hands, he found the right words for willing ears﻿—words which would kindle passion and lust of vengeance most readily where they lay dormant, or would fan them into greater vigour where they smouldered.
“For thee this scythe, Hector Lebrun,” he would say to a tall, lanky youth whose emaciated arms and bony hands were stretched with longing toward the bright piece of steel; “remember last year’s harvest, the heavy tax thou wert forced to pay, so that not one sou of profit went into thy pocket, and thy mother starved whilst M. le duc and his brood feasted and danced, and shiploads of corn were sunk in the Loire lest abundance made bread too cheap for the poor!
“For thee this pickaxe, Henri Meunier! Remember the new roof on thy hut, which thou didst build to keep the wet off thy wife’s bed, who was crippled with ague﻿—and the heavy impost levied on thee by the tax-collector for this improvement to thy miserable hovel.
“This pole for thee, Charles Blanc! Remember the beating administered to thee by the duc’s bailiff for daring to keep a tame rabbit to amuse thy children!
“Remember! Remember, *mes amis*!” he added exultantly, “remember every wrong you have endured, every injustice, every blow! remember your poverty and his wealth, your crusts of dry bread and his succulent meals, your rags and his silks and velvets, remember your starving children and ailing mother, your care-laden wife and toil-worn daughters! Forget nothing, *mes amis*, tonight, and at the gates of the château de Kernogan demand of its arrogant owner wrong for wrong and outrage for outrage.”
A deafening cry of triumph greeted this peroration, scythes and sickles and axes and poles were brandished in the air and several scores of hands were stretched out to Pierre and clasped in this newly-formed bond of vengeful fraternity.

===Chinese===

然而皮埃尔浑然不顾明暗寒暑，只是平静而有条不紊地将这些原始的战斗器械分发给这群早己按捺不住作恶冲动的愚朴农人。每当他将一件武器交到一双渴望的手中，他总能找到恰如其分的话语送入迫切的耳中——那些最容易在沉睡处引燃激情与复仇烈焰的话语，或是将暗燃火种鼓荡为冲天狂澜的字句。
“这把镰刀给你，埃克托·勒布兰，”他对一个高瘦瘦长的年轻人说道，那青年憔悴的双臂与骨瘦如柴的手正贪婪地伸向那片明晃晃的钢铁，“想想去年的收成，你被迫缴纳的沉重赋税，你兜里没有留下一分钱的利润，你的母亲忍饥挨饿，而公爵先生和他的走狗却在大摆筵席、纵情歌舞，整船整船的粮食被沉入卢瓦尔河，只为了防止丰收让穷人买到廉价的面包！
“这把十字镐给你，亨利·默尼耶！想想你为了不让身患疟疾卧床不起的妻子淋雨而给茅屋翻修的新屋顶——以及税务官就凭你修葺了破烂窝棚而向你横征暴敛的重税！
“这根长棍给你，夏尔·布朗！想想公爵的管事就因为你敢养一只驯顺的兔子逗孩子们开心而把你打得皮开肉绽！
“记住！记住，朋友们！”他狂喜地补充道，“记住你们所遭受的每一次屈辱、每一桩不公、每一记毒打！记住你们的赤贫与他的豪富，你们干瘪的面包屑与他鲜美多汁的大餐，你们的褴褛衣衫与他的绫罗绸缎；记住你们饥肠辘辘的孩子与卧病在床的母亲，你们忧心忡忡的妻子与劳碌不堪的女儿！今晚什么都不要遗忘，朋友们，到凯尔诺冈城堡的大门前，向那傲慢的主人以眼还眼、以暴易暴！”
这番激昂陈词赢得了震耳欲聋的欢呼，大镰刀、小镰刀、利斧与木棍在半空中挥舞，数十双手伸向皮埃尔，紧紧相握在这刚刚结成的复仇弟兄誓盟之中。

### III / 第三节

### III / 
===Original===

Then it was that with vigorous play of the elbows, Jean Adet, the miller, forced his way through the crowd till he stood face to face with his son.
“Unfortunate!” he cried, “what is all this? What dost thou propose to do? Whither are ye all going?”
“To Kernogan!” they all shouted in response.
“En avant, Pierre! we follow!” cried some of them impatiently.
But Jean Adet﻿—who was a powerful man despite his years﻿—had seized Pierre by the arm and dragged him to a distant corner of the barn:
“Pierre!” he said in tones of command, “I forbid thee in the name of thy duty and the obedience which thou dost owe to me and to thy mother, to move another step in this hotheaded adventure. I was on the high road, walking homewards, when that conflagration and the senseless cries of these poor lads warned me that some awful mischief was afoot. Pierre! my son! I command thee to lay that weapon down.”
But Pierre﻿—who in his normal state was a dutiful son and sincerely fond of his father﻿—shook himself free from Jean Adet’s grasp.
“Father!” he said loudly and firmly, “this is no time for interference. We are all of us men here and know our own minds. What we mean to do tonight we have thought on and planned for weeks and months. I pray you, father, let me be! I am not a child and I have work to do.”
“Not a child?” exclaimed the old man as he turned appealingly to the lads who had stood by, silent and sullen during this little scene. “Not a child? But you are all only children, my lads. You don’t know what you are doing. You don’t know what terrible consequences this mad escapade will bring upon us all, upon the whole village, aye! and the countryside. Do you suppose for one moment that the château of Kernogan will fall at the mercy of a few ignorant unarmed lads like yourselves? Why! four hundred of you would not succeed in forcing your way even as far as the courtyard of the palace. M. le duc has had wind for some time of your turbulent meetings at the auberge: he has kept an armed guard inside his castle yard for weeks past, a company of artillery with two guns hoisted upon his walls. My poor lads! you are running straight to ruin! Go home, I beg of you! Forget this night’s escapade! Nothing but misery to you and yours can result from it.”
They listened quietly, if surlily, to Jean Adet’s impassioned words. Far be it from their thoughts to flout or to mock him.

===Chinese===

就在这时，磨坊主让·阿代奋力用胳膊肘在人群中挤开一条通路，直到与儿子面对面站定。
“造孽啊！”他喊道，“这都是在干什么？你想干什么？你们大家伙儿这是要去哪儿？”
“去凯尔诺冈！”众人齐声高呼回应。
“前进，皮埃尔！我们跟着你！〔En avant, Pierre ! we follow !〕”几个人不耐烦地大叫。
但让·阿代——尽管上了年纪，却依然身板结实——一把抓住了皮埃尔的手臂，将他拽到了谷仓僻静的角落：
“皮埃尔！”他用命令的口吻喝道，“凭你对我和对你母亲应尽的本分与顺从，我命令你，不许在这场头脑发热的胡闹中再往前走一步！我刚才正走在大路上往家赶，那冲天的火光和这群可怜小伙子盲目的狂叫，就让我知道要出大祸了！皮埃尔！我的儿啊！我命令你把那家伙放下！”
但皮埃尔——平日里本是个孝顺听话、深爱父亲的儿子——猛地挣脱了让·阿代的掌控。
“父亲！”他声音洪亮而坚定地说，“现在不是插手的时候！我们在这儿的全都是男子汉，心里清清楚楚！今晚我们要干的事，已经深思熟虑、谋划了几个星期、几个月！我求您了，父亲，由我去吧！我不是小孩子，我有正事要做！”
“不是孩子？”老人失声惊呼，哀求般地转向在这场争执中阴沉肃立的小伙子们，“不是孩子？可你们全都不过是一群孩子啊，孩子们！你们根本不知道自己在干什么！你们根本不知道这趟疯狂的冒险会给我们所有人、给整个村子，甚至给整个乡野带来多么可怕的灾祸！难道你们真以为凭你们几个愚昧无知、手无寸铁的小伙子，就能把凯尔诺冈城堡踏平吗？天哪！就算你们有四百人，也休想冲进那宫殿般的城堡院子！公爵先生早就听到了你们在客栈聚众闹事的风声，他这几个星期一直在城堡大院里驻扎着武装卫队，一队炮兵把两门大炮架在城墙上！我可怜的孩子们！你们这是在自取灭亡！回家去吧，我求求你们！把今晚的胡闹忘了吧！除了给你们和家人带来灭顶之灾，什么也不会有的！”
面对让·阿代激昂肺腑的劝导，他们沉默甚至带着几分阴鸷地听着。他们绝没有嘲讽轻慢老人的心思。

===Original===

Paternal authority commanded respect even among the most rough; but they all felt that they had gone too far now to draw back: the savour of anticipated revenge had been too sweet to be forgone quite so readily, and Pierre with his vigorous personality, his glowing eloquence, his compelling power had more influence over them than the sober counsels of prudence and the wise admonitions of old Jean Adet. Not one word was spoken, but with an instinctive gesture every man grasped his weapon more firmly and then turned to Pierre, thus electing him their spokesman.
Pierre too had listened in silence to all that his father said, striving to hide the burning anxiety which was gnawing at his heart, lest his comrades allowed themselves to be persuaded by the old man’s counsels and their ardour be cooled by the wise dictates of prudence. But when Jean Adet had finished speaking, and Pierre saw each man thus grasping his weapon all the more firmly and in silence, a cry of triumph escaped his lips.
“It is all in vain, father,” he cried, “our minds are made up. A host of angels from heaven would not bar our way now to victory and to vengeance.”
“Pierre!” admonished the old man.
“It is too late, my father,” said Pierre firmly, “en avant, lads!”
“Yes! en avant! en avant!” assented some, “we have wasted too much time as it is.”
“But, unfortunate lads,” admonished the old man, “what are you going to do?﻿—a handful of you﻿—where are you going?”
“We go straight to the crossroads now, father,” said Pierre, firmly. “The firing of your ricks﻿—for which I humbly crave your pardon﻿—is the preconcerted signal which will bring the lads from all the neighbouring villages﻿—from Goulaine and les Sorinières and Doulon and Tourne-Bride to our meeting place. Never you fear! There will be more than four hundred of us and a company of paid soldiers is not like to frighten us. Eh, lads?”
“No! no! en avant!” they shouted and murmured impatiently, “there has been too much talking already and we have wasted precious time.”
“Pierre!” entreated the miller.
But no one listened to the old man now. A general movement down the hillock had already begun and Pierre, turning his back on his father, had pushed his way to the front of the crowd and was now leading the way down the slope.

===Chinese===

长辈的威严即便在最粗野的汉子心中也受人敬重；但他们都感到自己已经迈得太远，无法回头了：预期的复仇滋味太过甜美，绝非轻易能够舍弃，而皮埃尔那强悍的个性、炽热的口才与无可抗拒的统御力，对他们的影响远胜过老让·阿代审慎冷静的劝诫与明智警示。没有一个人开口答话，但凭着一种本能的举动，每个人都把手中的武器握得更紧，随后齐齐转向皮埃尔，以此公推他为代言人。
皮埃尔同样默默听完了父亲所说的一切，竭力掩饰内心啃噬般的焦灼，生怕同伴们被老人的劝告所打动，那股狂热被明智的审慎所熄灭。但当让·阿代话音落下，皮埃尔看到每个人都在沉默中将武器攥得更紧时，一声胜利的欢呼从他唇间脱口而出。
“白费口舌了，父亲！”他大喊，“我们心意已决！哪怕天上的天使大军下凡，也休想阻挡我们夺取胜利与复仇的道路！”
“皮埃尔！”老人厉声告诫。
“太迟了，我的父亲，”皮埃尔坚定地说道，“前进，弟兄们！〔en avant, lads !〕”
“对！前进！前进！〔en avant ! en avant !〕”几个人随声附和，“我们已经耽搁太久了！”
“可是，造孽的糊涂蛋啊，”老人痛心疾首，“就凭你们这几个人，到底要干什么？你们要去哪里？”
“我们现在径直去十字路口，父亲，”皮埃尔坚定地回答，“烧掉您的麦垛——为此我卑微地乞求您的原谅——是事先约定好的信号，它会把周围所有村庄的小伙子都召唤到我们的集结地，古莱讷（Goulaine）、莱索里尼埃尔（les Sorinières）、杜隆（Doulon）和图尔恩布里德（Tourne-Bride）的人都会来！您甭害怕！到时候我们会有四百多人，一队吃饷的雇佣兵休想吓倒我们！对不对，弟兄们？”
“对！不对！前进！”他们高声叫嚷，不耐烦地催促，“废话已经够多了，我们白白浪费了宝贵的时间！”
“皮埃尔！”磨坊主哀求道。
但此刻已经没人听得进老人的话了。整个人群已经开始朝山丘下移动，皮埃尔背对着父亲，挤到人群最前列，领头向山坡下走去。

===Original===

Up on the summit the fire was already burning low; only from time to time an imprisoned tongue of flame would dart out of the dying embers and leap fitfully up into the night. A dull red glow illumined the small farmery and the mill and the slowly moving mass of men along the narrow road, whilst clouds of black, dense smoke were tossed about by the gale. Pierre walked with head erect. He ceased to think of his father and he never looked back to see if the others followed him. He knew that they did: like the straw-ricks a while ago, they had become the prey of a consuming fire: the fire of their own passion which had caught them and held them and would not leave them now until their ardour was consumed in victory or defeat.

===Chinese===

山丘顶上的烈火已经渐渐转弱；只剩下一缕缕受困的火舌偶尔从渐冷的余烬中窜出，在夜空中忽明忽暗地跳跃。暗红色的微光照亮了小农舍、磨坊，以及在狭窄道路上缓缓移动的人流，而一团团浓密的黑烟则被狂风扯得四散飞扬。皮埃尔昂首挺胸大步前行。他不再去想他的父亲，也从未回头去看身后是否有人跟从。他深知他们会跟上来：正如不久前的那些草垛一样，他们早已沦为吞噬烈火的祭品——那是他们自身的激情之火，将他们牢牢擒获、捆绑，除非狂热在胜利或败亡中彻底燃尽，否则绝不会将他们放过。

### IV / 第四节

### IV / 
===Original===

M. le duc de Kernogan had just finished dinner when Jacques Labrunière, his head-bailiff, came to him with the news that a rabble crowd, composed of the peasantry of Goulaine and Vertou and the neighbouring villages, had assembled at the crossroads, there held revolutionary speeches, and was even now marching toward the castle still shouting and singing and brandishing a miscellaneous collection of weapons chiefly consisting of scythes and axes.
“The guard is under arms, I imagine,” was M. le duc’s comment on this not altogether unforeseen piece of news.
“Everything is in perfect order,” replied the head-bailiff cooly, “for the defence of M. le duc and his property﻿—and of Mademoiselle.”
M. le duc, who had been lounging in one of the big armchairs in the stately hall of Kernogan, jumped to his feet at these words: his cheeks suddenly pallid, and a look of deadly fear in his eyes.
“Mademoiselle,” he said hurriedly, “by God, Labrunière, I had forgotten﻿—momentarily﻿—”
“M. le duc?” stammered the bailiff in anxious inquiry.
“Mademoiselle de Kernogan is on her way home﻿—even now﻿—she spent the day with Mme. la Marquise d’Herbignac﻿—she was to return at about eight o’clock.﻿ ﻿… If those devils meet her carriage on the road.﻿ ﻿…”
“There is no cause for anxiety, M. le duc,” broke in Labrunière hurriedly. “I will see that half a dozen men get to horse at once and go and meet Mademoiselle and escort her home.﻿ ﻿…”
“Yes﻿ ﻿… yes﻿ ﻿… Labrunière,” murmured the duc, who seemed very much overcome with terror now that his daughter’s safety was in jeopardy, “see to it at once. Quick! quick! I shall wax crazy with anxiety.”
While Labrunière ran to make the necessary arrangements for an efficient escort for Mademoiselle de Kernogan and gave the sergeant in charge of the posse the necessary directions, M. le duc remained motionless, huddled up in the capacious armchair, his head buried in his hand, shivering in front of the huge fire which burned in the monumental hearth, himself the prey of nameless, overwhelming terror.
He knew﻿—none better﻿—the appalling hatred wherewith he and all his family and belongings were regarded by the local peasantry.

===Chinese===

德·凯尔诺冈公爵刚刚用过晚膳，他的总管雅克·拉布吕尼埃便匆匆赶来禀报：由古莱讷、韦尔图及周边各村农民组成的乌合之众已在十字路口集结，在那里发表了革命演说，此刻正高歌呐喊、挥舞着各式杂凑的武器——主要是长柄大镰刀和斧头——向城堡开拔而来。
“我想守卫已经全副武装了吧，”公爵先生对这并非完全出乎意料的消息冷冷评价道。
“一切均妥当就绪，”总管镇定地答道，“誓死保卫公爵先生及其家业——以及小姐。”
原本闲适地斜靠在凯尔诺冈庄严大厅宽大扶手椅上的公爵先生，听到最后几个字时霍然跃起：他的面颊瞬间煞白，眼中掠过极度的恐慌。
“小姐，”他急促地喘息道，“老天啊，拉布吕尼埃，我一时……给忘了……”
“公爵先生？”总管惶惑地结结巴巴问道。
“凯尔诺冈小姐正在回家的路上——就在此刻！她在德·埃尔比尼亚克侯爵夫人（Marquise d'Herbignac）那里过了一整天——本该在八点左右返回的……要是那些恶棍在路上撞见了她的马车……”
“公爵先生不必忧心，”拉布吕尼埃急忙插话，“我立刻安排六个人骑快马迎上前去，护送小姐回府……”
“快……快去，拉布吕尼埃，”公爵喃喃说道，一旦女儿的安危受到威胁，他已被恐怖彻底击垮，“立刻去办！快！快！我急得都要发疯了！”
拉布吕尼埃奔出去为凯尔诺冈小姐安排得力的护卫队，并向带队军士下达指示，而公爵先生却僵坐在宽大的扶手椅里一动不动，双手抱头，在宏伟壁炉中熊熊燃烧的烈火前瑟瑟发抖，沦为那难以言喻的巨大恐惧的俘虏。
他深知——没有人比他更清楚——当地农民对他本人、他的家族以及一切所有物怀着怎样蚀骨销魂的深仇大恨。

===Original===

Astride upon his manifold rights﻿—feudal, territorial, seignorial rights﻿—he had all his life ridden roughshod over the prejudices, the miseries, the undoubted rights of the poor people, who were little better than serfs in the possession of the high and mighty duc de Kernogan. He also knew﻿—none better﻿—that gradually, very gradually it is true, but with unerring certainty, those same downtrodden, ignorant, miserable and half-starved peasants were turning against their oppressors, that riots and outrages had occurred in many rural districts in the North and that the insidious poison of social revolution was gradually creeping toward the South and West, and had already infected the villages and small townships which were situated quite unpleasantly close to Nantes and to Kernogan.
For this reason he had kept a company of artillery at his own expense inside the precincts of his château, and with the aristocrat’s open contempt for this peasantry which it had not yet learned to fear, he had disdained to take further measures for the repression of local gatherings, and would not pay the village rabble the compliment of being afraid of them in any way.
But with his daughter Yvonne in the open roadway on the very night when an assembly of that same rabble was obviously bent on mischief, matters became very serious. Insult, outrage or worse might befall the proud aristocrat’s only child, and knowing that from these people, whom she had been taught to look upon as little better than beasts, she could expect neither mercy nor chivalry, the duc de Kernogan within his unassailable castle felt for his daughter’s safety the most abject, the most deadly fear which hath ever unnerved any man.
Labrunière a few minutes later did his best to reassure his master.
“I have ordered the men to take the best horses out of the stables, M. le duc,” he said, “and to cut across the fields toward la Gramoire so as to intercept Mademoiselle’s coach ere it reach the crossroads. I feel confident that there is no cause for alarm,” he added emphatically.
“Pray God you are right, Labrunière,” murmured the duc feebly. “Do you know how strong the rabble crowd is?”
“No, Monseigneur, not exactly.

===Chinese===

他依仗着自己名下多如牛毛的特权——封建领主权、土地权与庄园管辖权——毕生都粗暴地践踏着穷苦人民的诉求、苦难以及不容置疑的正当权益；在他这位显赫至尊的德·凯尔诺冈公爵手中，那些穷苦人与农奴无异。他也深知——同样没有人比他更清楚——那些受尽践踏、愚昧、凄惨且忍饥挨饿的农民，正在缓慢地——确实极其缓慢，但无可阻挡地——转过身来对抗压迫者；北方许多乡村地区已爆发了暴动与杀戮，社会革命的阴险毒素正逐渐向南部和西部蔓延，甚至已经感染了那些距离南特和凯尔诺冈近得令人不快的村镇。
正因如此，他自掏腰包在城堡要塞内豢养了一队炮兵；然而出于贵族对尚未学会畏惧的贱民一贯的轻蔑，他不屑于采取进一步措施来镇压地方上的小规模集会，根本不屑于向村里的暴民表露出哪怕一丁点的畏惧。
可是，就在这群暴民显然蓄意作乱的深夜，他的爱女伊冯娜（Yvonne）却身在空旷的大路上，事情顿时变得万分凶险。凌辱、暴行乃至更可怕的下场都可能降临到这位骄傲贵族的独生女头上；公爵深知，在这些从小被教育看作与禽兽无异的贱民身上，她绝不可能奢望一丝仁慈或骑士风度。高踞在固若金汤的城堡中的德·凯尔诺冈公爵，为了女儿的安危，感受到了任何男子所能体会到的最卑微、最致命的透骨恐惧。
几分钟后，拉布吕尼埃极力抚慰他的主人。
“公爵先生，我已经命人从马厩里挑出最好的骏马，”他说，“抄近路穿过田野前往拉格拉穆瓦尔，好在小姐的马车赶到十字路口之前迎上她。我敢断定，绝无理由惊慌。”他斩钉截铁地补充道。
“愿上帝保佑你是对的，拉布吕尼埃，”公爵气若游丝地喃喃自语，“你知道那群暴民究竟有多少人吗？”
“大人，小的不能确知。

===Original===

Camille the under-bailiff, who brought me the news, was riding homewards across the meadows about an hour ago when he saw a huge conflagration which seemed to come from the back of Adet’s mill: the whole sky has been lit up by a lurid light for the past hour, and I fancied myself that Adet’s straw must be on fire. But Camille pushed his horse up the rising ground which culminates at Adet’s farmery. It seems that he heard a great deal of shouting which did not seem to be accompanied by any attempt at putting out the fire. So he dismounted and led his horse round the hillock skirting Adet’s farm buildings so that he should not be seen. Under cover of darkness he heard and saw the old miller with his son Pierre engaged in distributing scythes, poles and axes to a crowd of youngsters and haranguing them wildly all the time. He also heard Pierre Adet speak of the conflagration as a preconcerted signal, and say that he and his mates would meet the lads of the neighbouring villages at the crossroads﻿ ﻿… and that four hundred of them would then march on Kernogan and pillage the castle.”
“Bah!” quoth M. le duc in a voice hoarse with execration and contempt, “a lot of oafs who will give the hangman plenty of trouble tomorrow. As for that Adet and his son, they shall suffer for this﻿ ﻿… I can promise them that.﻿ ﻿… If only Mademoiselle were home!” he added with a heartrending sigh.

===Chinese===

副管事卡米耶送来消息，大约一个小时前他骑马穿过草地回家，看见阿代的磨坊后面冲起巨大的火光。整片天空在过去这一个小时里都被照得猩红，我起初还以为是阿代家的麦草失火了。但卡米耶策马奔上阿代农庄所在的高地，据说他听到了鼎沸的呼喊声，而且根本没人去扑火。于是他跳下马，牵着马绕着小丘在阿代家农舍背后隐蔽前行，以免被人察觉。借着夜色掩护，他亲眼看到、亲耳听到老磨坊主和他的儿子皮埃尔正忙着给一大群年轻人分发长镰、长棍和利斧，而且一直在疯狂地发表演说煽动他们。他还听到皮埃尔·阿代说这大火是事先约定的信号，说他和同伙要在十字路口与邻近各村的青年汇合……然后四百人一起向凯尔诺冈进发，洗劫城堡。”
“呸！”公爵先生咬牙切齿，用充满诅咒与鄙夷的沙哑声音啐道，“一群蠢笨的乡巴佬，明天够刽子手忙活的了！至于那个阿代和他的儿子，他们休想逃过重惩……我发誓要让他们付出代价……只要小姐能平安到家就好！”他发出一声揪心的长叹，喃喃自语。

### V / 第五节
===Original===

Indeed, had M. le duc de Kernogan been gifted with second sight, the agony of mind which he was enduring would have been aggravated an hundredfold. At the very moment when the head-bailiff was doing his best to reassure his liege-lord as to the safety of Mlle. de Kernogan, her coach was speeding along from the château of Herbignac toward those same crossroads where a couple of hundred hotheaded peasant lads were planning as much mischief as their unimaginative minds could conceive.
The fury of the gale had in no way abated, and now a heavy rain was falling﻿—a drenching, sopping rain which in the space of half an hour had added five centimetres to the depth of the mud on the roads, and had in that same space of time considerably damped the enthusiasm of some of the poor lads. Three score or so had assembled from Goulaine, two score from les Sorinières, some three dozen from Doulon: they had rallied to the signal in hot haste, gathered their scythes and spades, very eager and excited, and had reached the crossroads which were much nearer to their respective villages than to Jean Adet’s farm and the mill, even while the old man was admonishing his son and the lads of Vertou on the summit of the blazing hillock. Here they had spent half an hour in cooling their heels and their tempers under the drenching rain﻿—wet to the skin﻿—fuming and fretting at the delay.
But even so﻿—damped in ardour and chilled to the marrow﻿—they were still a dangerous crowd and prudence ought to have dictated to Mademoiselle de Kernogan the wiser course of ordering her coachman Jean-Marie to head his horses back toward Herbignac the moment that the outrider reported that a mob, armed with scythes, spades and axes, held the crossroads, and that it would be dangerous for the coach to advance any further.
Already for the past few minutes the sound of loud shouting had been heard even above the tramp of the horses and the clatter of the coach. Jean-Marie had pulled up and sent one of the outriders on ahead to see what was amiss: the man returned with very unpleasant tidings﻿—in his opinion it certainly would be dangerous to go any further. The mob appeared bent on mischief: he had heard threats and curses all levelled against M. le duc de Kernogan﻿—the conflagration up at Vertou was evidently a signal which would bring along a crowd of malcontents from all the neighbouring villages.

===Chinese===

确实，倘若德·凯尔诺冈公爵具备未卜先知的本领，他此刻所经受的精神折磨定会加剧百倍。就在总管极力向领主保证凯尔诺冈小姐平安无事的同一时刻，她的马车正从埃尔比尼亚克城堡疾驰驶向同一个十字路口——在那里，两百名头脑发热的乡下青年正在谋划着他们愚拙的大脑所能构想出的最大祸事。
暴烈的狂风丝毫未见减弱，如今又下起倾盆大雨——滂沱的大雨在半小时内让道路上的泥泞深了五厘米，也在这段时间里大大浇熄了部分可怜小伙子的狂热。古莱讷集结了大约六十人，莱索里尼埃尔来了四十人，杜隆来了三十六人左右：他们闻讯后匆匆响应信号，抄起大镰刀和铁锹，满腔亢奋与激动，赶到了距离各自村庄远比阿代农庄和磨坊近得多的十字路口；而与此同时，老让·阿代还在烈焰腾空的山丘顶上规劝着儿子和韦尔图的青年。这群人在大雨倾盆下足足苦等了半个小时，浑身湿透，心焦如焚，因延误而怨声载道。
然而即便如此——尽管热血渐冷、寒气彻骨——他们依然是一群危险的暴徒。当先遣护卫禀报有一群手持大镰刀、铁锹和利斧的暴民占领了十字路口、马车前行十分危险时，明智的抉择本应让凯尔诺冈小姐立即命令车夫让-马里调转马头返回埃尔比尼亚克。
其实在过去几分钟里，即使隔着马蹄声与车轮辘辘声，也能听见嘈杂的叫喊。让-马里勒住缰绳，派了一名骑从上前打探究竟：那人带回了极其不妙的消息——在他看来，继续前行绝对凶多吉少。暴民显然蓄意作乱：他听到了针对德·凯尔诺冈公爵的所有威胁与诅咒——韦尔图山头的冲天大火显然是个信号，必将招引周边所有村庄的骚乱分子。

===Original===

He was for turning back forthwith. But Mademoiselle put her head out of the window just then and asked what was amiss. On hearing that Jean-Marie and the postilion and outriders were inclined to be afraid of a mob of peasant lads who had assembled at the crossroads, and were apparently threatening to do mischief, she chided them for their cowardice.
“Jean-Marie,” she called scornfully to the old coachman, who had been in her father’s service for close on half a century, “do you really mean to tell me that you are afraid of that rabble!”
“Why no! Mademoiselle, so please you,” replied the old man, nettled in his pride by the taunt, “but the temper of the peasantry round here has been ugly of late, and ’tis your safety I have got to guard.”
“ ’Tis my commands you have got to obey,” retorted Mademoiselle with a gay little laugh which mitigated the peremptoriness of her tone. “If my father should hear that there’s trouble on the road he will die of anxiety if I do not return: so whip up the horses, Jean-Marie. No one will dare to attack the coach.”
“But Mademoiselle﻿—” remonstrated the old man.
“Ah çà!” she broke in more impatiently, “am I to be openly disobeyed? Best join that rabble, Jean-Marie, if you have no respect for my commands.”
Thus twitted by Mademoiselle’s sharp tongue, Jean-Marie could not help but obey. He tried to peer into the distance through the veil of blinding rain which beat against his face and stung the horses to restlessness. But the light from the coach lanterns prevented his seeing clearly into the darkness beyond. Still it seemed to him that on ahead a dense and solid mass was moving toward the coach, also that the sound of shouting and of excited humanity was considerably nearer than it had been before. No doubt the mob had perceived the lights of the coach, and was even now making towards it, with what intent Jean-Marie divined all too accurately.
But he had his orders, and, though he was an old and trusted servant, disobedience these days was not even to be thought of. So he did as he was bid. He whipped up his horses, which were high-spirited and answered to the lash with a bound and a plunge forward. Mlle. de Kernogan leaned back on the cushions of the coach. She was satisfied that Jean-Marie had done as he was told, and she was not in the least afraid.
But less than five minutes later she had a rude awakening.

===Chinese===

他主张立即折返。但小姐偏在此时探出车窗，询问出了何事。在得知让-马里、驭手和随行护卫居然害怕在十字路口集结的一群似乎意图滋事的乡下小伙时，她斥责他们的怯懦。
“让-马里，”她带着轻蔑的口吻呼唤服侍了父亲近半个世纪的老车夫，“你当真要告诉我，你怕了那群下贱坯子吗？”
“哪里的话！小姐，随您怎么说，”老人被这嘲讽刺痛了尊严，答道，“可是近来这片乡下的农民脾性凶得很，老汉我得护卫您的安全哪。”
“你得服从的是我的命令，”小姐反唇相讥，发出一声清脆的笑声，冲淡了命令语气的生硬，“如果我父亲听说路上出了乱子，而我又未曾归家，他定会忧急而死：所以赶车吧，让-马里！没人敢袭击这辆马车。”
“可是小姐——”老人试图抗辩。
“怎么！”她更加不耐烦地打断道，“难道你要公然违抗我的命令吗？若是你不知遵从我的吩咐，让-马里，倒不如去投奔那伙乌合之众算了！”
被小姐这番尖刻的话一激，让-马里只得遵命。他透过打在脸上让人睁不开眼的暴风雨，极力望向远方，马儿也被风雨抽打得烦躁不安。然而马车车灯的光芒反而让他看不清前方的黑暗。尽管如此，他隐约觉得前方有一大群黑压压的人马正朝着马车涌来，人群的呐喊与鼓噪之声也比先前近了许多。毫无疑问，那伙人看见了马车的灯火，正冲上前截击，意图何在，让-马里心知肚明。
但他身负命令，尽管身为得力受信任的老仆，在当下公然违命也是绝不可想象的。于是他遵令行事。他挥鞭催马，烈马扬鬃奋蹄向前飞奔。凯尔诺冈小姐靠在马车的软垫上。见让-马里照办，她心中十分满意，丝毫不觉得有什么可怕。
然而不到五分钟之后，她便迎来了残酷的梦醒时分。

===Original===

The coach gave a terrific lurch. The horses reared and plunged, there was a deafening clamour all around: men were shouting and cursing: there was the clash of wood and iron and the cracking of whips: the tramp of horses’ hoofs in the soft ground, and the dull thud of human bodies falling in the mud, followed by loud cries of pain. There was the sudden crash of broken glass, the coach lanterns had been seized and broken: it seemed to Yvonne de Kernogan that out of the darkness faces distorted with fury were peering at her through the windowpanes. But through all the confusion, the coach kept moving on. Jean-Marie stuck to his post, as did also the postilion and the four outriders, and with whip and tongue they urged their horses to break through the crowd regardless of human lives, knocking and trampling down men and lads heedless of curses and blasphemies which were hurled on them and on the occupants of the coach, whoever they might be.
The next moment, however, the coach came to a sudden halt, and a wild cry of triumph drowned the groans of the injured and the dying.
“Kernogan! Kernogan!” was shouted from every side.
“Adet! Adet!”
“You limbs of Satan,” cried Jean-Marie, “you’ll rue this night’s work and weep tears of blood for the rest of your lives. Let me tell you that! Mademoiselle is in the coach. When M. le duc hears of this, there will be work for the hangman.﻿ ﻿…”
“Mademoiselle in the coach,” broke in a hoarse voice with a rough tone of command. “Let’s look at her.﻿ ﻿…”
“Aye! Aye! let’s have a look at Mademoiselle,” came with a volley of objurgations and curses from the crowd.
“You devils﻿—you would dare?” protested Jean-Marie.
Within the coach Yvonne de Kernogan hardly dared to breathe. She sat bolt upright, her cape held tightly round her shoulders: her eyes dilated now with excitement, if not with fear, were fixed upon the darkness beyond the windowpanes. She could see nothing, but she *felt* the presence of that hostile crowd who had succeeded in overpowering Jean-Marie and were intent on doing her harm.
But she belonged to a caste which never reckoned cowardice amongst its many faults. During these few moments when she knew that her life hung on the merest thread of chance, she neither screamed nor fainted but sat rigidly still, her heart beating in unison with the agonising seconds which went so fatefully by.

===Chinese===

马车剧烈地晃荡了一下。战马惊恐扬蹄腾空，四下爆发出一阵震耳欲聋的喧嚣：人们疯狂地呼喊与咒骂，刀斧铁器与木棍的撞击声、皮鞭的抽打声不绝于耳；马蹄在泥泞中踏溅，伴随着人体跌落烂泥的沉闷重响与撕心裂肺的惨嚎。突然传来一声玻璃破碎的巨响，车灯被扯下砸得粉碎：在伊冯娜·德·凯尔诺冈眼中，黑暗中仿佛有一张张愤怒扭曲的面孔隔着车窗玻璃死死瞪着她。然而在这片极度混乱中，马车依然在向前冲。让-马里坚守在车夫座上，驭手和四名随骑护卫也寸步不让，挥舞着皮鞭拼命驱赶马匹冲破人群，全然不顾人命，将扑上来的汉子小伙纷纷撞翻踩踏，任凭恶毒的诅咒与谩骂雨点般砸向他们与车内的贵客。
然而下一刻，马车骤然停顿，一声狂暴的胜利呐喊盖过了伤者与垂死者的痛苦呻吟。
“凯尔诺冈！凯尔诺冈！”四周到处传来怒吼。
“阿代！阿代！”
“你们这群该死的撒旦恶徒，”让-马里厉声大叫，“你们今晚的勾当定会叫你们痛悔终生，后半辈子哭出血泪来！我告诉你们！小姐就在车里！等公爵先生知道了，刽子手有的是活儿干……”
“小姐在车里，”一个沙哑而带着粗暴命令口吻的声音打断了他，“让我们瞧瞧她……”
“对！对！让我们瞧瞧公爵小姐！”人群中爆发出一阵恶毒的咒骂与喧哗。
“你们这群魔鬼——你们敢？！”让-马里怒斥。
马车内部，伊冯娜·德·凯尔诺冈几乎不敢呼吸。她挺直身躯端坐着，斗篷紧裹在肩头：她的双眸因亢奋与恐惧而睁大，紧紧盯着车窗外的黑暗。她什么也看不见，却能真切感受到那群敌对暴徒的存在——他们制服了让-马里，正图谋加害于她。
但她出身于绝无懦弱名声的高傲门阀。在这命悬一线的几秒钟里，她既没有尖叫也没有昏厥，而是僵硬静坐，心跳伴随着命运宣判般的漫长刻度剧烈搏动。

===Original===

And even now, when the carriage door was torn violently open and even through the darkness she discerned vaguely the forms of these avowed enemies close beside her, and anon felt a rough hand seize her wrist, she did not move, but said quite calmly, with hardly a tremor in her voice:
“Who are you? and what do you want?”
An outburst of harsh and ironical laughter came in response.
“Who are we, my fine lady?” said the foremost man in the crowd, he who had seized her wrist and was half in and half out of the coach at this moment, “we are the men who throughout our lives have toiled and starved whilst you and such as you travel in fine coaches and eat your fill. What we want? Why, just the spectacle of such a fine lady as you are being knocked down into the mud just as our wives and daughters are if they happen to be in the way when your coach is passing. Isn’t that it, *mes amis*?”
“Aye! aye!” they replied, shouting lustily. “Into the mud with the fine lady. Out with her, Adet. Let’s have a look at Mademoiselle how she will look with her face in the mud. Out with her, quick!”
But the man who was still half in and half out of the coach, and who had hold of Mademoiselle’s wrist did not obey his mates immediately. He drew her nearer to him and suddenly threw his rough, begrimed arms round her, and with one hand pulled back her hood, then placing two fingers under her chin, he jerked it up till her face was level with his own.
Yvonne de Kernogan was certainly no coward, but at the loathsome contact of this infuriated and vengeful creature, she was overcome with such a hideous sense of fear that for the moment consciousness almost left her: not completely alas! for though she could not distinguish his face she could feel his hot breath upon her cheeks, she could smell the nauseating odour of his damp clothes, and she could hear his hoarse mutterings as for the space of a few seconds he held her thus close to him in an embrace which to her was far more awesome than that of death.

===Chinese===

甚至在此刻，车门被猛地暴力拉开，透过昏暗的夜色，她隐约辨认出身旁近在咫尺的宿敌身影，并随之感到一只粗糙的大手狠命攥住了她的手腕，她依旧纹丝不动，用几乎听不出颤抖的极平静口吻说道：
“你们是谁？想干什么？”
回应她的是一阵刺耳而嘲讽的狂笑。
“我们是谁，高贵的小姐？”走在人群最前头的那个人说，正是他抓住了她的手腕，半个身子探进车厢，“我们是那些一辈子面朝黄土忍饥挨饿的人，而你们这种人却乘着华丽马车锦衣玉食！我们要什么？哈，无非是想看看像您这样娇贵的小姐被一脚踢进烂泥里，就像我们的妻子女儿在你们马车驶过时被撵进泥沟一样！大伙儿说是不是，朋友们？”
“没错！没错！”他们扯着嗓门哄应，“把这娇贵的小姐拽进烂泥里！拉她出来，阿代！让我们瞧瞧公爵小姐趴在泥地里是个什么模样！快拉出来！”
但那个半截身子探在车门内的汉子紧攥着小姐的手腕，并未立刻顺应同伴的叫嚣。他把她猛拽到自己跟前，一把扯落了她的兜帽，托起她的下颌逼她正视自己。
伊冯娜·德·凯尔诺冈决非软弱之辈，但面对眼前这个满腔仇怨的暴徒，一阵可怖的恐慌瞬间袭来，令她几乎失去神智：然而并未完全昏厥！虽然看不清他的面孔，却能感受到他粗重灼热的呼吸，闻到他潮湿衣衫上的腐浊气息，听到他压低的沙哑诅咒，在这短短几秒内，那迫近的威胁比死亡本身更令人胆寒。

===Original===

“And just to punish you, my fine lady,” he said in a whisper which sent a shudder of horror right through her, “to punish you for what you are, the brood of tyrants, proud, disdainful, a budding tyrant yourself, to punish you for every misery my mother and sister have had to endure, for every luxury which you have enjoyed, I will kiss you on the lips and the cheeks and just between your white throat and chin and never as long as you live if you die this night or live to be an hundred will you be able to wash off those kisses showered upon you by one who hates and loathes you﻿—a miserable peasant whom you despise and who in your sight is lower far than your dogs.”
Yvonne, with eyes closed, hardly breathed, but through the veil of semiconsciousness which mercifully wrapped her senses, she could still hear those awful words, and feel the pollution of those loathsome kisses with which﻿—true to his threat﻿—this creature﻿—half man, wholly devil, whom she could not see, but whom she hated and feared as she would Satan himself﻿—now covered her face and throat.
After that she remembered nothing more. Consciousness mercifully forsook her altogether. When she recovered her senses, she was within the precincts of the castle: a confused murmur of voices reached her ears, and her father’s arms were round her. Gradually she distinguished what was being said: she gathered the threads of the story which Jean-Marie and the postilion and outriders were hastily unravelling in response to M. le duc’s commands.
These men of course knew nothing of the poignant little drama which had been enacted inside the coach. All they knew was that they had been surrounded by a rough crowd﻿—a hundred or so strong﻿—who brandished scythes and spades, that they had made valiant efforts to break through the crowd by whipping up their horses, but that suddenly some of those devils more plucky than the others seized the horses by their bits and rendered poor Jean-Marie quite helpless. He thought then that all would be up with the lot of them and was thinking of scrambling down from his box in order to protect Mademoiselle with his body, and the pistols which he had in the boot, when happily for everyone concerned, he heard in the distance﻿—above the clatter which that abominable rabble was making, the hurried tramp of horses.

===Chinese===

“为了惩罚你，高贵的小姐，”他低声耳语，那声音令她浑身战栗，“惩罚你们这群专横暴虐的贵族血脉，惩罚你自幼养成的傲慢与残忍；为了报复我母亲和妹妹所受的每一重苦难，报复你享用过的每一份奢靡，我要当面狠狠羞辱你，在你脸上留下耻辱的印记！无论你今晚死去还是活到百岁，你一生都洗刷不掉这份来自一个痛恨你之人的羞辱——来自一个被你鄙夷、视若草芥的下贱农夫的严惩！”
伊冯娜双目紧闭，气若游丝，但在半昏迷的混沌意识中，她依然听到了那些可怖的字句，经受了这狂徒如约降下的残酷羞辱与玷污——这个她未曾看清面容、却视同恶魔般惧怕与痛恨的暴徒。
此后发生的事情她再无知觉，昏迷慈悲地带走了她所有的感知。当她重获神智时，人已置身城堡之内：嘈杂的人声传入耳际，父亲正将她紧紧搂在怀里。渐渐地，她听清了周遭的言语，拼凑出让-马里、驭手和护卫遵照公爵命令仓促交代的来龙去脉。
这些仆从自然对车厢内发生的惊险一幕一无所知。他们只知道自己被上百名挥舞镰刀利斧的暴民围攻，他们虽鞭马冲撞，却有几个胆大包天的恶徒死死拽住马嚼子，让老让-马里束手无策。正当他以为大势已去、打算跳下车座用血肉之躯和车厢里的手枪护卫小姐时，天遂人愿，在暴民的鼎沸喧嚣之上，他听到了远方疾驰而来的急促马蹄声。

===Original===

At once he jumped to the conclusion that these could be none other than a company of soldiers sent by M. le duc. This spurred him to a fresh effort, and gave him a new idea. To Carmail the postilion who had a pistol in his holster he gave the peremptory order to fire a shot into the air or into the crowd, Jean-Marie cared not which. This Carmail did, and at once the horses, already maddened by the crowd, plunged and reared wildly, shaking themselves free. Jean-Marie, however, had them well in hand, and from far away there came the cries of encouragement from the advancing horsemen who were bearing down on them full tilt. The next moment there was a general melee. Jean-Marie saw nothing save his horses’ heads, but the outriders declared that men were trampled down like flies all around, while others vanished into the night.
What happened after that none of the men knew or cared. Jean-Marie galloped his horses all the way to the castle and never drew rein until the precincts were reached.

===Chinese===

让-马里立刻断定这必定是公爵先生派来的援兵骑兵。这令他精神大振，灵机一动，严令腰别手枪的驭手卡尔迈立即朝天或朝人群开枪鸣警。卡尔迈依令开枪，本就受惊发狂的骏马猛地腾跃挣脱了束缚。让-马里稳稳勒住缰绳，而远方风驰电掣赶来的骑兵也发出了鼓舞人心的呼喝。下一瞬间双方混战成一团。让-马里眼里只有马头，只顾策马突围，而随骑护卫事后声称暴民四下如落蝇般被踏翻踩碎，其余人等则遁入夜色之中。
此后究竟如何，众人既不知晓也不关心。让-马里一路纵马狂奔回城堡，直到进入要塞内院才勒住缰绳。

### VI / 第六节
===Original===

Had M. de Kernogan had his way and a free hand to mete out retributive justice in the proportion that he desired, there is no doubt that the hangman of Nantes would have been kept exceedingly busy. As it was a number of arrests were effected the following day﻿—half the manhood of the countryside was implicated in the aborted *Jacquerie* and the city prison was not large enough to hold it all.
A court of justice presided over by M. le duc, and composed of half a dozen men who were directly or indirectly in his employ, pronounced summary sentences on the rioters which were to have been carried out as soon as the necessary arrangements for such wholesale executions could be made. Nantes was turned into a city of wailing; peasant-women﻿—mothers, sisters, daughters, wives of the condemned, trooped from their villages into the city, loudly calling on M. le duc for mercy, besieging the improvised courthouse, the prison gates, the town residence of M. le duc, the palace of the bishop: they pushed their way into the courtyards and the very corridors of those buildings﻿—flunkeys could not cope with them﻿—they fought with fists and elbows for the right to make a direct appeal to the liege-lord who had power of life and death over their men.
The municipality of Nantes held aloof from this distressful state of things, and the town councillors, the city functionaries and their families shut themselves up in their houses in order to avoid being a witness to the heartrending scenes which took place uninterruptedly round the courthouse and the prison. The mayor himself was powerless to interfere, but it is averred that he sent a secret courier to Paris to M. de Mirabeau, who was known to be a personal friend of his, with a detailed account of the *Jacquerie* and of the terrible measures of reprisal contemplated by M. le duc de Kernogan, together with an earnest request that pressure from the highest possible quarters be brought to bear upon His Grace so that he should abate something of his vengeful rigours.
Poor King Louis, who in these days was being terrorised by the National Assembly and swept off his feet by the eloquence of M. de Mirabeau, was only too ready to make concessions to the democratic spirit of the day. He also desired his noblesse to be equally ready with such concessions. He sent a personal letter to M.

===Chinese===

倘若德·凯尔诺冈公爵能如愿以偿、放手施展他所渴望的严刑报复，毫无疑问南特的刽子手定会忙得不可开交。次日，大批涉案人员遭到搜捕——半个乡村的青壮年都卷入了这场未遂的暴动，城里的监狱根本容纳不下。
由公爵亲自主持的法庭，成员由六名直接或间接受雇于他的亲信组成，草草对暴民宣判了死刑，并打算在完成大规模行刑准备后立即执行。南特沦为一座悲号之城；被判死罪者的母亲、姐妹、女儿、妻子成群结队从村落涌入城中，高声哀求公爵先生开恩，围堵在临时法庭、监狱大门、公爵府邸及主教公署外：她们硬挤进院落和建筑物的走廊，仆役根本阻拦不住，妇女们用拳头和手肘推搡争抢，只求能直接向掌握着亲人生杀大权的主子陈情请命。
南特市政当局对此惨状袖手旁观，市议员、市政官员及其家眷紧闭门户，以免目睹法庭和监狱周围接连上演的撕心裂肺惨剧。市长本人无力干预，但据说他派密使前往巴黎，向私交笃厚的德·米拉波先生递送了密信，详述了这场农民暴动的始末以及德·凯尔诺冈公爵意欲展开的血腥报复，恳切请求由最高层出面施压，迫使公爵收敛其严酷的复仇手段。
可怜的路易十六国王当时正受制于国民议会的威慑，沉醉于德·米拉波先生雄辩的口才之中，极愿向当下的民主思潮作出让步。他也期望麾下的贵族阶层同样展现这般姿态。他亲笔致信公爵先生，不仅是请求，更是命令他向这群受人蛊惑的乡下年轻人展示宽宏与仁慈——国王坚称，一次意外的法外开恩定能赢回民众的忠诚与拥戴。

===Original===

le duc, not only asking him, but commanding him, to show grace and mercy to a lot of misguided peasant lads whose loyalty and adherence﻿—he urged﻿—might be won by a gracious and unexpected act of clemency.
The King’s commands could not in the nature of things be disobeyed: the same stroke of the pen which was about to send half a hundred young countrymen to the gallows granted them M. le duc’s gracious pardon and their liberty: the only exception to this general amnesty being Pierre Adet, the son of the miller. M. le duc’s servants had deposed to seeing him pull open the door of the coach and stand for some time half in and half out of the carriage, obviously trying to terrorise Mademoiselle. Mademoiselle refused either to corroborate or to deny this statement, but she had arrived fainting at the gate of the château, and she had been very ill ever since. She had sustained a serious shock to her nerves, so the doctor hastily summoned from Paris had averred, and it was supposed that she had lost all recollection of the terrible incidents of that night.
But M. le duc was satisfied that it was Pierre Adet’s presence inside the coach which had brought about his daughter’s mysterious illness and that heartrending look of nameless horror which had dwelt in her eyes ever since. Therefore with regard to that man M. le duc remained implacable and as a concession to a father’s outraged feelings both the mayor of Nantes and the city functionaries accepted Adet’s condemnation without a murmur of dissent.
The sentence of death finally passed upon Pierre, the son of Jean Adet, miller of Vertou, could not, however, be executed, for the simple reason that Pierre had disappeared and that the most rigorous search instituted in the neighbourhood and for miles around failed to bring him to justice. One of the outriders who had been in attendance on Mademoiselle on that fateful night declared that when Jean-Marie finally whipped up his horses at the approach of the party of soldiers, Adet fell backwards from the step of the carriage and was run over by the hind wheels and instantly killed. But his body was never found among the score or so which were left lying there in the mud of the road until the women and old men came to seek their loved ones among the dead.
Pierre Adet had disappeared. But M. le duc’s vengeance had need of a prey.

===Chinese===

在当时的情势下，王命自然不容违抗：原本将把五十名乡村青年送上绞架的同一道判令，笔锋一转便成了公爵先生恩准的赦免与获释。在这场大赦之中，唯一的例外便是磨坊主的儿子皮埃尔·阿代。公爵的仆从指认看见他强行扯开车门，半截身子探入车厢，公然恐吓小姐。小姐对这一证词既未证实也未否认，但她抵达城堡大门时已然昏厥，自那以后一直缠绵病榻。仓促从巴黎召来的名医断言她遭受了极严重的神经创伤，人们认定她已将那恐怖之夜的遭遇遗忘殆尽。
然而公爵先生坚信，正是皮埃尔·阿代闯入马车，才导致爱女罹患怪病，双眸中自此流露出那难以磨灭的名状恐惧。因此，对这个人公爵表现得铁石心肠，南特市长与市政官员也顾及一位受辱父亲的情感，毫不异议地通过了对阿代的死刑判决。
然而，对韦尔图磨坊主让·阿代之子皮埃尔所下的死刑判决最终却未能执行，原因很简单：皮埃尔人间蒸发了，在当地及方圆数英里内展开的严密搜捕均未能将他捕获归案。当晚随侍小姐的一名护卫声称，当让-马里在骑兵赶到时奋力鞭马突围之际，阿代从车踏板上仰面跌落，被后轮当场碾毙。但在横陈于路面烂泥中的二十余具尸体中，当妇孺老人前来认领亲人遗体时，始终未曾找到他的尸身。
皮埃尔·阿代消失了。但公爵先生的复仇渴望必须找到猎物。

===Original===

The outrage which he was quite convinced had been perpetrated against his daughter must be punished by death﻿—if not by the death of the chief offender, then by that of the one who stood nearest to him. Thus was Jean Adet the miller dragged from his home and cast into prison. Was he not implicated himself in the riots? Camille the bailiff had seen and heard him among the insurgents on the hillock that night. At first it was stated that he would be held as hostage for the reappearance of his son. But Pierre Adet had evidently fled the countryside: he was obviously ignorant of the terrible fate which his own folly had brought upon his father. Many thought that he had gone to seek his fortune in Paris where his talents and erudition would ensure him a good place in the present mad rush for equality amongst all men. Certain it is that he did not return and that with merciless hate and vengeful relentlessness M. le duc de Kernogan had Jean Adet hanged for a supposed crime said to be committed by his son.
Jean Adet died protesting his innocence. But the outburst of indignation and revolt aroused by this crying injustice was swamped by the torrent of the revolution which, gathering force by these very acts of tyranny and of injustice, soon swept innocent and guilty alike into a vast whirlpool of blood and shame and tears.

===Chinese===

他深信女儿遭受的凌辱必须以鲜血偿还——纵使主犯未死，也必须由血缘最近之人代受。于是，磨坊主让·阿代被从家中强行拖走，投进大牢。难道他自己未曾涉足暴乱？管事卡米耶当晚亲眼看见他在山丘暴民之中。起初外界宣称将留他为人质，以待其子现身伏法。然而皮埃尔·阿代显然已潜逃异乡，对自身鲁莽给老父招致的飞来横祸一无所知。许多人猜测他已前往巴黎谋求出路，凭他的才干与学识，在那场狂热的人人平等风潮中定能谋得一席之地。无论如何，他终未归来，而德·凯尔诺冈公爵怀着刻骨仇恨与冷酷执念，终以莫须有的罪名将让·阿代送上了绞架，替子顶罪。
让·阿代在大呼冤枉中惨遭处决。然而，这桩骇人听闻的暴行所激起的人民愤恨与反抗狂潮，很快便被大革命的滔天巨浪所吞没——正是这些层出不穷的暴政与不公，聚敛起毁天灭地的伟力，转瞬间将无辜与罪愆一同卷入了血海、耻辱与泪水的无垠漩涡之中。

## Book / 第一卷
===Original===

I Bath, 1793

===Chinese===

卷一 巴斯，1793年

## I The Moor / 第一章 荒原
===Original===



===Chinese===

第一章 荒原

#### I / 第一节
===Original===

Silence. Loneliness. Desolation.
And the darkness of late afternoon in November, when the fog from the Bristol Channel has laid its pall upon moor and valley and hill: the last grey glimmer of a wintry sunset has faded in the west: earth and sky are wrapped in the gloomy veils of oncoming night. Some little way ahead a tiny light flickers feebly.
“Surely we cannot be far now.”
“A little more patience, Mounzeer. Twenty minutes and we be there.”
“Twenty minutes, *mordieu*. And I have ridden since the morning. And you tell me it was not far.”
“Not far, Mounzeer. But we be not ’orzemen either of us. We doan’t travel very fast.”
“How can I ride fast on this heavy beast? And in this *satané* mud. My horse is up to his knees in it. And I am wet﻿—ah! wet to my skin in this *sacré* fog of yours.”
The other made no reply. Indeed he seemed little inclined for conversation: his whole attention appeared to be riveted on the business of keeping in his saddle, and holding his horse’s head turned in the direction in which he wished it to go: he was riding a yard or two ahead of his companion, and it did not need any assurance on his part that he was no horseman: he sat very loosely in his saddle, his broad shoulders bent, his head thrust forward, his knees turned out, his hands clinging alternately to the reins and to the pommel with that ludicrous inconsequent gesture peculiar to those who are wholly unaccustomed to horse exercise.
His attitude, in fact, as well as the promiscuous set of clothes which he wore﻿—a labourer’s smock, a battered high hat, threadbare corduroys and fisherman’s boots﻿—at once suggested the loafer, the do-nothing who hangs round the yards of halfway houses and posting inns on the chance of earning a few coppers by an easy job which does not entail too much exertion on his part and which will not take him too far from his favourite haunts. When he spoke﻿—which was not often﻿—the soft burr in the pronunciation of the sibilants betrayed the Westcountryman.

===Chinese===

寂静。荒凉。凄清。
十一月暮色苍茫的黑夜降临了，从布里斯托尔海峡（Bristol Channel）涌来的浓雾在荒原、峡谷与丘陵上笼上一层阴郁的裹尸布：冬日落日最后那一抹灰暗的微光已在西方隐没，天地尽皆隐没在逼近黑夜的幽暗面纱之中。正前方不远处，一星微弱的灯火隐约跳动着。
“我们离那儿肯定不远了吧？”
“再耐着点儿性子，先生（Mounzeer）。还有二十分钟咱们就到了。”
“二十分钟，见鬼（mordieu）！我从大清早就开始骑马赶路，你却跟我说不远！”
“是不远，先生。可咱们俩谁都不会骑马，走得自然不快。”
“骑着这么一头笨牲口，我怎么快得起来？更别提这该死的（satané）烂泥了！我这匹马陷得快没到膝盖了！还有这见鬼的（sacré）大雾，把我浑身都浇得透湿，湿透到骨头里了！”
另一人没有作声。事实上他似乎极不愿多言，全副心思仿佛都用在稳住马鞍、以及让马头顺着他指定的方向前行上。他骑在同伴前头一两码的地方，根本无须他开腔证明，明眼人一眼就能看出他毫无骑术可言：他在鞍上坐得松松垮垮，宽厚的肩膀佝偻着，脑袋前探，两膝向外撇着，双手在缰绳与鞍桥之间轮番胡乱抓扯，带着全然不谙骑术之人特有的滑稽荒唐举止。
事实上，不仅是他的姿态，还有他身上那套杂七杂八的行头——一件粗布短罩衫、一顶破损的高顶帽、磨得发亮的灯芯绒裤子，外加一双渔夫长靴——无不暴露出他是个游手好闲之徒。这种人常年混迹于中途客栈与驿站大院，指望着碰上点儿既不费大力气、又不用离开老巢太远的轻松差事，混上几个铜板糊口。每逢他开口——这倒并不常见——吐字中略带含混的齿音便泄露了他英格兰西部乡下人的身份。

===Original===

His companion, on the other hand, was obviously a stranger: high of stature, and broadly built, his wide shoulders and large hands and feet, his square head set upon a short thick neck, all bespoke the physique of a labouring man, whilst his town-made clothes﻿—his heavy caped coat, admirably tailored, his buckskin breeches and boots of fine leather﻿—suggested, if not absolutely the gentleman, at any rate one belonging to the well-to-do classes. Though obviously not quite so inexperienced in the saddle as the other man appeared to be, he did not look very much at home in the saddle either: he held himself very rigid and upright and squared his shoulders with a visible effort at seeming at ease, like a townsman out for a constitutional on the fashionable promenade of his own city, or a cavalry subaltern but lately emerged from a riding school. He spoke English quite fluently, even colloquially at times, but with a marked Gallic accent.

===Chinese===

相反，他的同伴显然是个异乡人：身材魁梧，体格健硕，宽肩阔掌，大脚粗颈，短粗的脖颈上生着一颗方头，尽显粗重体力劳动者的骨架体魄；然而他身上那套城里裁制的高档衣衫——那件做工精良、沉重考究的斗篷大衣，那条鹿皮马裤与精良皮靴——却表明他就算称不上名门绅士，也至少属于殷实富足的阶层。虽然他在马背上不像前头那汉子那般笨拙不堪，但也绝称不上挥洒自如：他身姿僵硬挺直，故意紧绷双肩想要显得从容自然，宛如城里人在自家时尚林荫大道上散步消遣，又似初出骑兵训练学校的年轻少尉。他的英语说得颇为流利，有时甚至带着市井俚语，但带有一股浓重的高卢口音。

#### II / 第二节
===Original===

The road along which the two cavaliers were riding was unspeakably lonely and desolate﻿—an offshoot from the main Bath to Weston road. It had been quite a good secondary road once. The accounts of the county administration under date 1725 go to prove that it was completed in that year at considerable expense and with stone brought over for the purpose all the way from Draycott quarries, and for twenty years after that a coach used to ply along it between Chelwood and Redhill as well as two or three carriers, and of course there was all the traffic in connection with the Stanton markets and the Norton Fairs. But that was nigh on fifty years ago now, and somehow﻿—once the mail-coach was discontinued﻿—it had never seemed worth while to keep the road in decent repair. It had gone from bad to worse since then, and travelling on it these days either ahorse or afoot had become very unpleasant. It was full of ruts and crevasses and knee-deep in mud, as the stranger had very appositely remarked, and the stone parapet which bordered it on either side, and which had once given it such an air of solidity and of value, was broken down in very many places and threatened soon to disappear altogether.
The country round was as lonely and desolate as the road. And that sense of desolation seemed to pervade the very atmosphere right through the darkness which had descended on upland and valley and hill. Though nothing now could be seen through the gloom and the mist, the senses were conscious that even in broad daylight there would be nothing to see. Loneliness dwelt in the air as well as upon the moor. There were no homesteads for miles around, no cattle grazing, no pastures, no hedges, nothing﻿—just arid wasteland with here and there a group of stunted trees or an isolated yew, and tracts of rough, coarse grass not nearly good enough for cattle to eat.
There are vast stretches of upland equally desolate in many parts of Europe﻿—notably in Northern Spain﻿—but in England, where they are rare, they seem to gain an additional air of loneliness through the very life which pulsates in their vicinity. This bit of Somersetshire was one of them in this year of grace 1793.

===Chinese===

两位骑者所走的道路无比凄凉幽僻——它是从巴斯（Bath）通往韦斯顿主干道上分出的一条岔路。这条路当年也曾是一条优良的二级公路。郡政府1725年的账册足资证明，此路于该年耗费巨资筑成，所用石料皆特意从德雷科特采石场一路运来；此后二十年间，切尔伍德与红山之间的邮车常年在此穿梭，另有两三家货运车行营运，更不用说与斯坦顿集市及诺顿市集相关的繁忙交通了。然而那已是将近五十年前的旧事了，不知为何——一旦邮政马车停运——似乎就再无人觉得有必要维持道路的修缮了。自那以后，路况每况愈下，如今无论骑马还是步行，在这条路上赶路都变得痛苦不堪。路上坑洼遍布、沟壑纵横，积水泥泞深及膝盖，正如那异乡客恰如其分的抱怨；而道路两旁曾经彰显坚实与体面的石砌矮墙，如今也已有多处坍塌，眼看就要荡然无存了。
四周的旷野与这条道路一样荒凉落寞。那种荒芜之感似乎穿透了笼罩高地、河谷与山峦的浓重夜色，弥漫在整片空气之中。尽管此刻透过昏暗与浓雾目力难及，但感官却清晰地知晓，即便在光天化日之下也绝无景物可观。荒凉不仅盘踞在大地上，更萦绕在空气里。方圆数英里内不见村舍农庄，没有牛羊吃草，没有牧场，没有树篱，空无一物——只有贫瘠的荒原，偶尔点缀着一丛矮小的黑莓灌木，或是几株在贫瘠石缝间艰难求存的自生野岑树苗。
在正前方极远处，巴斯城的灯火在薄暮浓雾中隐隐闪烁；而在更远处的西南方，布里斯托尔（Bristol）城隐没在铅灰色的沉重云层之下；但在这片荒凉的旷野上，四下唯有无边黑暗与死寂。在1793年这个寒冽的十一月里，除了这两位骑着疲惫劣马、在泥泞破碎的古道上跌撞前行的骑者之外，荒原上再无任何生灵的踪迹。

===Original===

Despite the proximity of Bath and its fashionable life, its gaieties and vitality, distant only a little over twenty miles, and of Bristol distant less than thirty, it had remained wild and forlorn, almost savage in its grim isolation, primitive in the grandeur of its solitude.

===Chinese===

尽管紧邻着巴斯城及其衣香鬓影的时尚人群、欢歌笑语、华丽舞会与奢靡盛景，这里却是一片粗粝凄凉的萨默塞特郡原野，正如亚瑟王在卡米洛特设朝理政的远古岁月一样蛮荒未开、苍凉寥落。

#### III / 第三节
===Original===

The road at the point now reached by the travellers begins to slope in a gentle gradient down to the level of the Chew, a couple of miles further on: it was midway down this slope that the only sign of living humanity could be perceived in that tiny light which glimmered persistently. The air itself under its mantle of fog had become very still, only the water of some tiny moorland stream murmured feebly in its stony bed ere it lost its entity in the bosom of the river far away.
“Five more minutes and we be at th’ Bottom Inn,” quoth the man who was ahead in response to another impatient ejaculation from his companion.
“If we don’t break our necks meanwhile in this confounded darkness,” retorted the other, for his horse had just stumbled and the inexperienced rider had been very nearly pitched over into the mud.
“I be as anxious to arrive as you are, Mounzeer,” observed the countryman laconically.
“I thought you knew the way,” muttered the stranger.
“ ’Ave I not brought you safely through the darkness?” retorted the other; “you was pretty well ztranded at Chelwood, Mounzeer, or I be much mistaken. Who else would ’ave brought you out ’ere at this time o’ night, I’d like to know﻿—and in this weather too? You wanted to get to th’ Bottom Inn and didn’t know ’ow to zet about it: none o’ the gaffers up to Chelwood ’peared eager to ’elp you when I come along. Well, I’ve brought you to th’ Bottom Inn and﻿ ﻿… Whoa! Whoa! my beauty! Whoa, confound you! Whoa!”
And for the next moment or two the whole of his attention had perforce to be concentrated on the business of sticking to his saddle whilst he brought his fagged-out, ill-conditioned nag to a standstill.
The little glimmer of light had suddenly revealed itself in the shape of a lantern hung inside the wooden porch of a small house which had loomed out of the darkness and the fog. It stood at an angle of the road where a narrow lane had its beginnings ere it plunged into the moor beyond and was swallowed up by the all-enveloping gloom. The house was small and ugly; square like a box and built of grey stone, its front flush with the road, its rear flanked by several small outbuildings.

===Chinese===

在两位骑者此刻抵达的地段，道路开始陡峭地下探，伸向一片低洼的小洼地。洼地里耸立着一丛盘根错节、落尽树叶的柳树，预示着近旁有沟渠或水塘。道路两侧的地势隆起为密布杂草的微凸小丘；小径本身的烂泥深及马膝，马匹接连打滑失蹄，在暗藏的乱石上磕绊踉跄，黑臭的黏泥溅了骑手一身。
前方的灯火如今明显迫近了：它似乎来自洼地边缘一座孤零零小屋的底层窗户。
“就是那地方吗？”那异乡客又问了一遍。
“正是，先生。人称‘谷底客栈’（Bottom Inn）。再过一分钟咱们就到了。”
“哼！我这辈子就没走过这么该死的（sacré）烂路，也绝不想再见到这鬼地方第二次！”
“若非有事在身，旁人是极少打这儿过的，”向导平淡地答道。
“有事！见鬼的上帝（Nom de Dieu）！谁会在这鬼地方有什么正经事？”
“嗐！各人有各人的营生呗，”那人含糊其辞地应道。
“依我看，这儿倒是个剪径强盗与杀手出没的绝佳去处，”异乡客冷冷讥诮道。
“哟！咱们这一带可没有这号人。至少没有成气候的。拦路的野汉子倒偶尔有，不过只要旁人不招惹他们，他们也绝不伤人性命。”
“管他有没有强盗，”另一人啐道，“要是五分钟内赶不到，我非冻死在马鞍上不可！”
“您本可以坐驿站马车的，先生，”那人平静地反驳道。
“走这种破道，我怎么坐得起大车？”
“大车是走不通。但您本可以步行啊。”
“步行！”法国人狠狠咒骂了一声，“在这该死的泥沼里走上十英里？还在这种漆黑不见五指的夜里？”
“好歹驿车能把您送到离谷底客栈三英里的地方，剩下的路您走过来就是了。”
“那我宁可被绞死，”异乡人咕哝道。
“我这不是平平安安把您从黑夜里领过来了嘛？”对方反驳道，“您在切尔伍德那会儿可真是走投无路了，先生，除非我老汉看走了眼。除了我，这大半夜的还有谁肯把您引到这儿来——又赶上这种鬼天气？您吵着要去谷底客栈却摸不着门道，我过去的时候，切尔伍德镇上的老汉们可没一个乐意搭理您。得，我把您领到谷底客栈了，而且……驭！驭！好伙计！驭！该死的你给我停下！驭！”
在接下来的片刻工夫里，他的全副精力被迫用来稳住鞍座，费力勒停他那匹精疲力竭、脾性顽劣的瘦马。
那点微弱的灯光突然显出了真容——那是悬挂在一座从浓雾与夜色中显现出来的小屋外木门廊里的一盏提灯。小屋坐落在道路的转角处，一条狭窄的小巷在此延伸而出，随即便没入远处的荒原，被吞噬在无边的晦暗之中。房子低矮丑陋，宛如一只四方盒子，由灰石砌成，前脸紧贴着道路，后方倚靠着几间零碎的附属杂屋。

===Original===

Above the porch hung a plain signboard bearing the legend: “The Bottom Inn” in white letters upon a black ground: to right and left of the porch there was a window with closed shutters, and on the floor above two more windows﻿—also shuttered﻿—completed the architectural features of the Bottom Inn.
It was uncompromisingly ugly and uninviting, for beyond the faint glimmer of the lantern only one or two narrow streaks of light filtrated through the chinks of the shutters.

===Chinese===

门廊上方挂着一块朴素无华的招牌，黑底白字书写着：“谷底客栈”（The Bottom Inn）。门廊左右各有一扇闭紧百叶窗的窗户，而楼上同样闭着百叶窗的两扇窗户，便构成了谷底客栈全部的建筑特征。
这地方显得极度粗陋冷漠，令人望而却步，除了提灯散发出的微弱光晕外，唯有一两道纤细的光线从百叶窗的缝隙中透出来。

#### IV / 第四节
===Original===

The travellers, after some difference of opinion with their respective horses, contrived to pull up and to dismount without any untoward accident. The stranger looked about him, peering into the darkness. The place indeed appeared dismal and inhospitable enough: its solitary aspect suggested footpads and the abode of cutthroats. The silence of the moor, the pall of mist and gloom that hung over upland and valley sent a shiver through his spine.
“You are sure this is the place?” he queried.
“Can’t ye zee the zign?” retorted the other gruffly.
“Can you hold the horses while I go in?”
“I doan’t know as ’ow I can, Mounzeer. I’ve never ’eld two ’orzes all at once. Suppose they was to start kickin’ or thought o’ runnin’ away?”
“Running away, you fool!” muttered the stranger, whose temper had evidently suffered grievously during the weary, cold journey from Chelwood. “I’ll break your *satané* head if anything happens to the beasts. How can I get back to Bath save the way I came? Do you think I want to spend the night in this Godforsaken hole?”
Without waiting to hear any further protests from the lout, he turned into the porch and with his riding whip gave three consecutive raps against the door of the inn, followed by two more. The next moment there was the sound of a rattling of bolts and chains, the door was cautiously opened and a timid voice queried:
“Is it Mounzeer?”
“*Pardieu!* Who else?” growled the stranger. “Open the door, woman. I am perished with cold.”
With an unceremonious kick he pushed the door further open and strode in. A woman was standing in the dimly lighted passage. As the stranger walked in she bobbed him a respectful curtsey.
“It is all right, Mounzeer,” she said; “the Captain’s in the coffee-room. He came over from Bristol early this afternoon.”
“No one else here, I hope,” he queried curtly.
“No one, zir. It ain’t their hour not yet. You’ll ’ave the ’ouse to yourself till after midnight. After that there’ll be a bustle, I reckon. Two shiploads come into Watchet last night﻿—brandy and cloth, Mounzeer, so the Captain says, and worth a mint o’ money. The pack ’orzes will be through yere in the small hours.”
“That’s all right, then. Send me in a bite and a mug of hot ale.”
“I’ll see to it, Mounzeer.”
“And stay﻿—have you some sort of stabling where the man can put the two horses up for an hour’s rest?”
“Aye, aye, zir.”

===Chinese===

两位旅人好不容易与各自的坐骑达成妥协，未生意外便勒马翻身落地。那异乡客环顾四周，眯起双眼打量着夜色。这地方着实阴森荒凉得紧，孤立无援的景象活脱脱一副盗贼出没、亡命之徒藏垢的巢穴。荒原的死寂，笼罩在丘陵与河谷之上的浓雾与阴霾，激得他背脊一阵发凉。
“你当真肯定就是这儿吗？”他质问道。
“您瞧不见那块招牌么？”另一人没好气地顶撞道。
“我先进去，你能替我牵着马吗？”
“这我可不敢打包票，先生。我老汉从没一口气牵过两匹马。要是它们尥起蹶子或者撒欢跑了呢？”
“跑了，你个蠢才！”异乡客恶狠狠地骂道，在从切尔伍德颠簸受冻而来的艰难旅途之后，他的脾气显然败坏到了极点，“要是这牲口出了半点差错，我非把你的狗头打烂不可！除了原路折返，我还怎么回巴斯？难不成你以为我想在这见鬼的破窟窿里过夜吗？”
不等那乡巴佬继续抗议，他便一头扎进门廊，用马鞭在客栈门板上连续敲击了三下，随后又敲了下两记。片刻之后，门内传来了门闩与锁链拉动的金属声响，门被小心翼翼地推开了一条缝，一个怯生生的声音问道：
“是法国先生（Mounzeer）吗？”
“老天哪（Pardieu）！还能是谁？”异乡客粗鲁地咆哮道，“快开门，妇人！我都要冻僵了！”
他不客气地飞起一脚将门踹开，大步跨了进去。昏暗逼仄的过道里站着一个妇人。那异乡客走进来时，她恭敬地屈膝行礼。
“一切妥当，先生，”她说，“船长就在茶室里。他今天午后一早就从布里斯托尔赶过来了。”
“我希望这儿没别的人吧？”他冷淡地问。
“没旁人，老爷。还不到他们碰头的时候呢。子夜以前这屋子全归您享用。不过过了半夜，我估摸着就得热闹起来了。昨夜有两船私货运抵沃切特港——运的全是白兰地和上等细呢，先生，船长说是值大钱的宝贝。运货的驮马队下半夜打这儿打尖路过。”
“那就好。给我弄口热吃的，再来一壶烫麦芽酒。”
“这就去办，先生。”
“且慢——你们有马厩能让外头那汉子把两匹马牵进去歇息一个小时吗？”
“有的，有的，老爷。”

===Original===

“Very well then, see to that too: and see that the horses get a feed and a drink and give the man something to eat.”
