“回过神来吧，马丹-罗热老兄，”他冷硬地说道，“我这人不记仇：只要顺着我的毛摸，我就是一条温顺的好狗。可要是谁敢拽我的尾巴，或是踩了我的爪子，嘿！我自然要龇牙咆哮。要是再犯一次……我就直接下嘴咬了……给我牢牢记住这一点；现在咱们言归正传，虽说我承认，我对你那帮凯尔诺冈家的余孽已经有点厌烦了。”
大人物说话间，马丹-罗热勉强重新稳住了心神。他只觉喉咙干渴难耐，手心湿热冒汗：恰似一个人摸黑在崎岖路上跌跌撞撞前行，却突然在一道深不见底的万丈深渊边缘险险刹住脚步，差一点点就摔得粉身碎骨。

===Original===

With a few harsh words, with a monstrous insult Carrier had made him feel the gigantic power which could hurl any man from the heights of self-assurance and of ambition to the lowest depths of degradation: he had shown him the glint of steel upon the guillotine.
He had been hit as with a sledgehammer﻿—the blow hurt terribly, for it had knocked all his self-esteem into nothingness and pulverised his self-conceit. It had in one moment turned him into a humble and cringing sycophant.
“I had no mind,” he began tentatively, “to give offence. My thoughts were bent on the Kernogans. They are a fine haul for us both, citizen Carrier, and I worked hard and long to obtain their confidence over in England and to induce them to come with me to Nantes.”
“No one denies that you have done well,” retorted Carrier gruffly and not yet wholly pacified. “If the haul had not been worth having you would have received no help from me.”
“I have shown my gratitude for your help, citizen Carrier. I would show it again﻿ ﻿… more substantially if you desire.﻿ ﻿…”
He spoke slowly and quite deferentially but the suggestion was obvious. Carrier looked up into his face: the light of measureless cupidity﻿—the cupidity of the coarse-grained, enriched peasant﻿—glittered in his pale eyes. It was by a great effort of will that he succeeded in concealing his eagerness beneath his habitual air of lofty condescension:
“Eh? What?” he queried airily.
“If another five thousand francs is of any use to you﻿ ﻿…”
“You seem passing rich, citizen Martin-Roget,” sneered Carrier.
“I have slaved and saved for four years. What I have amassed I will sacrifice for the completion of my revenge.”
“Well!” rejoined Carrier with an expressive wave of the hand, “it certainly is not good for a pure-minded republican to own too much wealth. Have we not fought,” he continued with a grandiloquent gesture, “for equality of fortune as well as of privileges﻿ ﻿…”
A sardonic laugh from young Lalouët broke in on the proconsul’s eloquent effusion.
Carrier swore as was his wont, but after a second or two he began again more quietly:
“I will accept a further six thousand francs from you, citizen Martin-Roget, in the name of the Republic and all her needs. The Republic of France is up in arms against the entire world. She hath need of men, of arms, of﻿ ﻿…”
“Oh! cut that,” interposed young Lalouët roughly.

===Chinese===

几句尖酸刻薄的厉语、一通丧心病狂的羞辱，卡里耶便让他深切领教到了那种能把任何人从自负与野心的顶峰瞬间打入万劫不复深渊的滔天权势：他向他亮出了断头台上寒光凛冽的钢刀。
他仿佛遭到了一记巨锤重击——这记重创痛彻心扉，因为它把他所有的自尊击得粉碎，将他的虚荣心碾为了齑粉。顷刻间，他沦为了一副卑躬屈膝、奴颜婢膝的谄媚奴相。
“我绝无……冒犯之意，”他小心翼翼地试探着开口道，“我只是满心牵挂着凯尔诺冈一家。对咱们双方而言，他们可都是不可多得的巨大收获，卡里耶公民；为了在英格兰博取他们的信任，诱骗他们随我前来南特，我可是耗费了无数心血。”
“没人能否认你办得漂亮，”卡里耶没好气地反驳道，火气尚未完全平息，“若不是这趟买卖油水丰厚，你休想从我这儿得到半点助力。”
“我对你的鼎力相助深表感激，卡里耶公民。若你愿意，我随时可以再次聊表寸心……拿出更实际的诚意……”
他语调放缓，极尽恭顺，但话里的暗示再直白不过。卡里耶抬眼注视着他的面庞：那双惨白的眼眸中闪烁着毫无节制的贪婪光芒——那是一个粗鄙暴发农夫典型的贪婪。他凭借极大的克制力，才在惯常那种居高临下的傲慢神态下掩饰住内心的急切：
“嗯？什么诚意？”他装模作样地轻描淡写问道。
“要是再奉上五千法郎能派上用场的话……”
“看来你挺阔绰嘛，马丹-罗热公民，”卡里耶讥笑道。
“这四年来我当牛做马、省吃俭用。只要能报仇雪恨，我积攒下的所有积蓄都在所不惜。”
“好嘛！”卡里耶意味深长地一挥手附和道，“对于一个心地纯洁的共和派公民而言，身外之财太多确实并非好事。难道我们不曾为了财富的平等以及特权的废除而并肩奋战吗……”
年轻拉卢埃发出一声冷嘲热讽的嗤笑，硬生生打断了总督的高谈阔论。
卡里耶习惯性地咒骂了一句，不过过了一两秒，他又放低了声调继续说道：
“以共和国及其一切紧迫需求的名义，马丹-罗热公民，我就收下你这六千法郎吧。法兰西共和国如今正武装起来对抗整个反法同盟。她需要人马，需要武器，需要——”
“行了！少扯那套吧，”年轻的拉卢埃粗鲁地插话道。

===Original===

But the over-vain, high and mighty despot who was ready to lash out with unbridled fury against the slightest show of disrespect on the part of any other man, only laughed at the boy’s impudence.
“Curse you, you young viper,” he said with that rude familiarity which he seemed to reserve for the boy, “you presume too much on my forbearance. These children you know, citizen.﻿ ﻿… Name of a dog!” he added roughly, “we are wasting time! What was I saying﻿ ﻿… ?”
“That you would take six thousand francs,” replied Martin-Roget curtly, “in return for further help in the matter of the Kernogans.”
“Why, yes!” rejoined Carrier blandly, “I was forgetting. But I’ll show you what a good dog I am. I’ll help you with those Kernogans﻿ ﻿… but you mistook my words, citizen: ’tis ten thousand francs you must pour into the coffers of the Republic, for her servants will have to be placed at the disposal of your private schemes of vengeance.”
“Ten thousand francs is a large sum,” said Martin-Roget. “Let me hear what you will do for me for that.”
He had regained something of his former complacency. The man who buys﻿—be it goods, consciences or services﻿—is always for the moment master of the man who sells. Carrier, despite his dictatorial ways, felt this disadvantage, no doubt, for his tone was more bland, his manner less curt. Only young Jacques Lalouët stood by﻿—like a snarling terrier﻿—still arrogant and still disdainful﻿—the master of the situation﻿—seeing that neither schemes of vengeance nor those of corruption had ruffled his self-assurance. He remained beside the door, ready to pounce on either of the two intruders if they showed the slightest sign of forgetting the majesty of the great proconsul.

===Chinese===

然而，这个狂妄自大、唯我独尊的暴君平日里哪怕旁人稍有不敬便会暴跳如雷，面对少年的放肆无礼，却仅仅一笑置之。
“该死的，你这小毒蛇，”他用那种似乎专为少年保留的粗鲁亲昵口吻说道，“你仗着我的纵容越来越放肆了。这些毛头孩子，你懂的，公民……见鬼！”他又粗声粗气地骂了一句，“咱们净耽误时间了！刚才我说到哪儿了……？”
“说到你愿意收下六千法郎，”马丹-罗热言简意赅地答道，“以此作为在凯尔诺冈一家事上继续提供助力的报酬。”
“哈，可不是嘛！”卡里耶温和地附和道，“我差点给忘了。但我会让你瞧瞧我是条多么忠诚的好狗。在凯尔诺冈那帮人身上我自会助你一臂之力……不过你刚才听岔了我的意思，公民：你得向共和国的金库充纳一万法郎才行，毕竟公家的差役都得听凭调遣，为你个人的复仇计划效力。”
“一万法郎可不是个小数目，”马丹-罗热说道，“且让我听听，拿了这笔钱你能为我办些什么。”
他又重拾了几分先前的底气与自得。无论购买的是货物、良心还是劳务，买方在那一刻总归凌驾于卖方之上。卡里耶尽管作风专横独裁，无疑也感受到了处于下风的劣势，因为他的语调变得愈发温和，态度也不再那般蛮横。唯有年轻的雅克·拉卢埃侍立在侧——犹如一只龇牙低吼的猎犬——依旧狂妄傲慢、不可一世，仿佛他才是全场真正的主宰，毕竟无论是复仇的密谋还是肮脏的贿赂，都丝毫未能动摇他的自信从容。他依旧伫立在门旁，随时准备在眼前这两位不速之客胆敢有半点轻慢大特使威严的苗头时猛扑上去。

#### VI / 第六节
===Original===

“I told you just now, citizen Martin-Roget,” resumed Carrier after a brief pause, “and I suppose you knew it already, that I am surrounded with spies.”
“Spies, citizen?” murmured Martin-Roget, somewhat taken aback by this sudden irrelevance. “I didn’t know﻿ ﻿… I imagine﻿ ﻿… Anyone in your position﻿ ﻿…”
“That’s just it,” broke in Carrier roughly. “My position is envied by those who are less competent, less patriotic than I am. Nantes is swarming with spies. Mine enemies in Paris are working against me. They want to undermine the confidence which the National Convention reposes in her accredited representative.”
“Preposterous,” ejaculated young Lalouët solemnly.
“Well!” rejoined Carrier with a savage oath, “you would have thought that the Convention would be only too thankful to get a strong man at the head of affairs in this hotbed of treason and of rebellion. You would have thought that it was no one’s affair to interfere with the manner in which I administer the powers that have been given me. I command in Nantes, what? Yet some busybodies up in Paris, some fools, seem to think that we are going too fast in Nantes. They have become weaklings over there since Marat has gone. It seems that they have heard rumours of our flat-bottomed barges and of our fine Republican marriages: apparently they disapprove of both. They don’t realise that we have to purge an entire city of every kind of rabble﻿—traitors as well as criminals. They don’t understand my aspirations, my ideals,” he added loftily and with a wide, sweeping gesture of his arm, “which is to make Nantes a model city, to free her from the taint of crime and of treachery, and.﻿ ﻿…”
An impatient exclamation from young Lalouët once again broke in on Carrier’s rhetoric, and Martin-Roget was able to slip in the query which had been hovering on his lips:
“And is this relevant, citizen Carrier,” he asked, “to the subject which we have been discussing?”
“It is,” replied Carrier drily, “as you will see in a moment. Learn then, that it has been my purpose for some time to silence mine enemies by sending to the National Convention a tangible reply to all the accusations which have been levelled against me.

“我方才对你说过，马丹-罗热公民，”卡里耶稍作停顿后继续说道，“想必你早就心知肚明，我如今正被密探重重包围。”

“荒谬绝伦，”年轻的拉卢埃一本正经地附和道。

年轻拉卢埃一声不耐烦的冷哼再次打断了卡里耶的高调演说，马丹-罗热这才得以插话，问出盘桓在嘴边已久的疑问：
“敢问卡里耶公民，这与我们方才商讨的正题有何相干？”
“大有相干，”卡里耶干硬地答道，“你马上就明白了。实不相瞒，一段时间以来，我一直筹划着给国民公会送去一份确凿有力的答卷，以此堵住向我泼来的所有污水，彻底封住敌人的嘴。

===Original===

It is my purpose to explain to the Assembly my reasons for mine actions in Nantes, my Drownages, my Republican marriages, all the coercive measures which I have been forced to take in order to purge the city from all that is undesirable.”
“And think you, citizen Carrier,” queried Martin-Roget without the slightest trace of a sneer, “that up in Paris they will understand your explanations?”
“Yes! they will﻿—they must when they realise that everything that I have done has been necessitated by the exigencies of public safety.”
“They will be slow to realise that,” mused the other. “The National Convention today is not what the Constitutional Assembly was in ’92. It has become soft and sentimental. Many there are who will disapprove of your doings.﻿ ﻿… Robespierre talks loftily of the dignity of the Republic﻿ ﻿… her impartial justice.﻿ ﻿… The Girondins﻿ ﻿…”
Carrier interposed with a coarse imprecation. He suddenly leaned forward, sprawling right across the desk. A shaft of light from between the damask curtains caught the end of his nose and the tip of his protruding chin, distorting his face and making it seem grotesque as well as hideous in the dim light. He appeared excited and inflated with vanity. He always gloried in the atrocities which he committed, and though he professed to look with contempt on every one of his colleagues, he was always glad of an opportunity to display his inventive powers before them, and to obtain their fulsome eulogy.
“I know well enough what they talk about in Paris,” he said, “but I have an answer﻿—a substantial, definite answer for all their rubbish. Dignity of the Republic? Bah! Impartial justice? ’Tis force, strength, Spartan vigour that we want﻿ ﻿… and I’ll show them.﻿ ﻿… Listen to my plan, citizen Martin-Roget, and see how it will work in with yours. My idea is to collect together all the most disreputable and notorious evildoers of this city﻿ ﻿… there are plenty in the entrepôt at the present moment, and there are plenty more still at large in the streets of Nantes﻿—thieves, malefactors, forgers of State bonds, assassins and women of evil fame﻿ ﻿… and to send them in a batch to Paris to appear before the Committee of Public Safety, whilst I will send to my colleagues there a letter couched in terms of gentle reproach: ‘See!’ I shall say, ‘what I have to contend with in Nantes. See!

“我打算向国民公会阐明我在南特采取各项举措的缘由：我的沉溺处决、我的‘共和国婚礼’，以及为了肃清城内所有败类而不得不采取的一切强制手段。”
“卡里耶公民，你真觉得，”马丹-罗热不带半点讥讽地反问道，“巴黎那些人能体谅你的这番苦心开释？”
“是的！他们会的——一旦他们明白我所做的一切皆是出于公众安全的迫切需要，他们就不得不认同。”
“他们恐怕没那么快想通，”对方沉思道，“如今的国民公会早已不复92年制宪议会的气魄。它变得软弱多愁善感。许多人会对你的作为横加非议……罗伯斯庇尔口口声声高谈共和国的尊严……她那公正无私的法度……吉伦特派……”
卡里耶粗野地咒骂了一声打断了他。他突然向前探出身子，整个人趴在书桌上。从锦缎窗帘缝隙透过来的一束光线正好打在他的鼻尖和突出的下巴尖上，扭曲了他的面部轮廓，使他在昏暗的光线下显得既怪诞又狰狞。他神情亢奋，虚荣心极度膨胀。他一向以自己犯下的残暴罪行自鸣得意，尽管他口头上自称蔑视身边的每一个同僚，但只要有机会向他们展示自己的发明才干并博取谄媚的赞誉，他向来乐此不疲。
“巴黎那些人在嚼什么舌根我一清二楚，”他说道，“但对于他们的那些陈词滥调，我自有一份扎实确凿的铁证来回敬。共和国的尊严？呸！公正无私的法度？我们需要的是雷霆万钧的铁腕、力量与斯巴达式的刚毅魄力……我要给他们好好瞧瞧……听听我的盘算吧，马丹-罗热公民，看它能如何与你的谋划不谋而合。我的构想是，把全城最臭名昭著、劣迹斑斑的恶棍通统纠集起来……如今保税仓库里关着大把这样的人，南特大街上逍遥法外的也多的是——窃贼、凶徒、伪造公债者、行凶者以及残花败柳……把他们打成一个批次押送进巴黎，送交救国委员会发落；同时，我会给那里的同僚们附上一封措辞委婉温和的责难信：‘瞧瞧吧！’信里我会这样写，‘我在南特不得不周旋应付的究竟是些什么货色。瞧瞧吧！

===Original===

the moral pestilence that infests the city. These evildoers are but a few among the hundreds and thousands of whom I am vainly trying to purge this city which you have entrusted to my care!’ They won’t know how to deal with the rabble,” he continued with his harsh strident laugh. “They may send them to the guillotine wholesale or deport them to Cayenne, and they will have to give them some semblance of a trial in any case. But they will have to admit that my severe measures are justified, and in future, I imagine, they will leave me more severely alone.”
“If as you say,” urged Martin-Roget, “the National Convention give your crowd a trial, you will have to produce some witnesses.”
“So I will,” retorted Carrier cynically. “So I will. Have I not said that I will round up all the most noted evildoers in the town? There are plenty of them I assure you. Lately, my Company Marat have not greatly troubled about them. After Savenay there was such a crowd of rebels to deal with, there was no room in our prisons for malefactors as well. But we can easily lay our hands on a couple of hundred or so, and members of the municipality or of the district council, or tradespeople of substance in the city will only be too glad to be rid of them, and will testify against those that were actually caught red-handed. Not one but has suffered from the pestilential rabble that has infested the streets at night, and lately I have been pestered with complaints of all these night-birds﻿—men and women and﻿ ﻿…”
Suddenly he paused. He had caught Martin-Roget’s feverish gaze fixed excitedly upon him. Whereupon he leaned back in his chair, threw his head back and broke into loud and immoderate laughter.
“By the devil and all his myrmidons, citizen!” he said, as soon as he had recovered his breath, “meseems you have tumbled to my meaning as a pig into a heap of garbage. Is not ten thousand francs far too small a sum to pay for such a perfect realisation of all your dreams? We’ll send the Kernogan girl and her father to Paris with the herd, what?﻿ ﻿… I promise you that such filth and mud will be thrown on them and on their precious name that no one will care to bear it for centuries to come.”
Martin-Roget of a truth had much ado to control his own excitement. As the proconsul unfolded his infamous plan, he had at once seen as in a vision the realisation of all his hopes.

‘瞧瞧蚕食这座城市的道德瘟疫吧。你们托付给我治理的这座城市里，我竭尽全力想要涤除的成千上万恶徒中，这伙歹徒不过是冰山一角！’巴黎那些人根本不知道该拿这帮流氓无赖怎么办，”他发出刺耳刺心的冷笑继续说道，“他们要么把这帮人统统押上断头台，要么流放到卡宴去，而且无论如何他们都不得不装模作样搞一场公开审判。但如此一来，他们就不得不承认我采取严苛铁腕是完全合情合理的；日后，我想他们再也不敢对我横加干涉了。”
“如果正如你所言，”马丹-罗热催促道，“国民公会要对这帮人进行审判，那你总得提供出庭作证的证人吧。”
“我当然会提供，”卡里耶冷酷地反唇相讥道，“我当然会。我刚才不是说了吗，我要把全城最臭名昭著的歹徒一网打尽？我敢向你打包票，这种人城里多得是。近来我的‘马拉连’可没空搭理他们。萨沃奈战役后要处置的叛党实在太多，咱们的监狱连关押普通罪犯的空地都腾不出来。不过要随手逮上一两百个简直易如反掌，市政厅或区议会的头头脑脑，抑或是城里有头有脸的体面富商，早就巴不得除掉这帮祸害了，定会乐于出庭指证那些当场被逮个正着的现行犯。谁没受过夜间在街头游荡的那帮恶棍的骚扰之苦？最近我整天被关于那些夜猫子的投诉烦得要命——男男女女，还有……”
他猝然止住话头。他察觉到马丹-罗热正用一双狂热亢奋的眼睛死死盯着自己。于是他向后仰靠在椅背上，仰起脖子，放肆地狂笑起来。
“凭魔鬼和他的所有爪牙起誓，公民！”他刚喘过气来便叫嚷道，“看来你领会起我的意图来，就跟猪一头扎进垃圾堆一样利索。能让你这般完美地美梦成真，区区一万法郎岂不是太便宜你了？咱们何不把凯尔诺冈家的小妞和她老子夹在这群牲口里一道押送去巴黎，如何？……我向你打包票，届时泼在他们身上以及他们那金字招牌上的脏水与污泥，足以让后世几百年里都没人再好意思顶着那个姓氏。”
马丹-罗热此时着实费了好大劲才勉强按捺住内心的狂喜。随着总督将这桩毒计全盘托出，他仿佛立刻在眼前看到了自己平生所有夙愿得偿的图景。

===Original===

What more awful humiliation, what more dire disgrace could be devised for proud Kernogan and his daughter than being herded together with the vilest scum that could be gathered together among the flotsam and jetsam of the population of a seaport town? What more perfect retaliation could there be for the ignominious death of Jean Adet the miller?
Martin-Roget leaned forward in his chair. The hideous figure of Carrier was no longer hideous to him. He saw in that misshapen, gawky form the very embodiment of the god of vengeance, the wielder of the flail of retributive justice which was about to strike the guilty at last.
“You are right, citizen Carrier,” he said, and his voice was thick and hoarse with excitement. He rested his elbow on his knee and his chin in his hand. He hammered his nails against his teeth. “That was exactly in my mind while you spoke.”
“I am always right,” retorted Carrier loftily. “No one knows better than I do how to deal with traitors.”
“And how is the whole thing to be accomplished? The wench is in my sister’s house at present﻿ ﻿… the father is in the Rat Mort.﻿ ﻿…”
“And the Rat Mort is an excellent place.﻿ ﻿… I know of none better. It is one of the worst-famed houses in the whole of Nantes﻿ ﻿… the meeting-place of all the vagabonds, the thieves and the cutthroats of the city.”
“Yes! I know that to my cost. My sister’s house is next door to it. At night the street is not safe for decent females to be abroad: and though there is a platoon of Marats on guard at Le Bouffay close by, they do nothing to free the neighbourhood of that pest.”
“Bah!” retorted Carrier with cynical indifference, “they have more important quarry to net. Rebels and traitors swarm in Nantes, what? Commandant Fleury has had no time hitherto to waste on mere cutthroats, although I had thoughts before now of razing the place to the ground. Citizen Lamberty has his lodgings on the other side and he does nothing but complain of the brawls that go on there o’ nights. Sure it is that while a stone of the Rat Mort remains standing all the nighthawks of Nantes will congregate around it and brew mischief there which is no good to me and no good to the Republic.”
“Yes! I know all about the Rat Mort. I found a night’s shelter there four years ago when﻿ ﻿…”
“When the ci-devant duc de Kernogan was busy hanging your father﻿—the miller﻿—for a crime which he never committed.

马丹-罗热在椅子上向前倾过身去。卡里耶那丑陋的面容在他眼中忽然不再狰狞可怖。在这具畸形笨拙的躯体上，他分明看到了复仇之神的化身，手握惩恶扬善的连枷，终于即将狠狠砸向罪人。
“你说得对，卡里耶公民，”他说道，因兴奋过度而声音变得嘶哑低沉。他把胳膊肘搁在膝盖上，手掌托着下巴，指甲神经质地在牙齿上轻轻敲击。“你刚才说话时，我脑子里正转着这个念头。”
“我向来是对的，”卡里耶倨傲地反唇相讥，“处置叛徒，没人比我更有手段。”
“那这整件事该如何操办？那小娘们眼下在我姐姐家……她老子关在‘死耗子’酒馆……”
“‘死耗子’可是个绝妙的好去处……世上再没有比那更好的地方了。那是全南特名声最臭的黑窝之一……全城无赖、窃贼和亡命之徒聚首的巢穴。”
“是啊！这一点我深有体会。我姐姐家就挨着它。一到入夜，正经妇道人家根本不敢在那条街上露面；尽管近在咫尺的布费城楼就驻扎着一个排的‘马拉连’，他们却对肃清邻近的祸患不闻不问。”

“没错！关于‘死耗子’的底细我全清楚。四年前我曾在那里借宿过一晚，当时……”
“当时前凯尔诺冈公爵正忙着把你那位磨坊主父亲，为了他根本不曾犯下的罪名活活吊死。

===Original===

Well then, citizen Martin-Roget,” continued Carrier with one of his hideous leers, “since you know the Rat Mort so well what say you to your fair and stately Yvonne de Kernogan and her father being captured there in the company of the lowest scum of the population of Nantes?”
“You mean﻿ ﻿… ?” murmured Martin-Roget, who had become livid with excitement.
“I mean that my Marats have orders to raid some of the haunts of our Nantese cutthroats, and that they may as well begin tonight and with the Rat Mort. They will make a descent on the house and a thorough perquisition, and every person﻿—man, woman and child﻿—found on the premises will be arrested and sent with a batch of malefactors to Paris, there to be tried as felons and criminals and deported to Cayenne where they will, I trust, rot as convicts in that pestilential climate. Think you,” concluded the odious creature with a sneer, “that when put face to face with the alternative, your Kernogan wench will still refuse to become the wife of a fine patriot like yourself?”
“I don’t know,” murmured Martin-Roget. “I﻿ ﻿… I﻿ ﻿…”
“But I do know,” broke in Carrier roughly, “that ten thousand francs is far too little to pay for so brilliant a realisation of all one’s hopes. Ten thousand francs? ’Tis an hundred thousand you should give to show your gratitude.”
Martin-Roget rose and stretched his large, heavy figure to its full height. He was at great pains to conceal the utter contempt which he felt for the abominable wretch before whom he was forced to cringe.
“You shall have ten thousand francs, citizen Carrier,” he said slowly; “it is all that I possess in the world now﻿—the last remaining fragment of a sum of twenty-five thousand francs which I earned and scraped together for the past four years. You have had five thousand francs already. And you shall have the other ten. I do not grudge it. If twenty years of my life were any use to you, I would give you that, in exchange for the help you are giving me in what means far more than life to me.”
The proconsul laughed and shrugged his shoulders﻿—of a truth he thought citizen Martin-Roget an awful fool.
“Very well then,” he said, “we will call the matter settled. I confess that it amuses me, although remember that I have warned you. With all these aristos, I believe in the potency of my barges rather than in your elaborate schemes. Still!

“你的意思是……？”马丹-罗热喃喃问道，激动的面色近乎铁青。
“我的意思是，我的‘马拉连’已经接到命令，要清剿南特这帮亡命徒的几处黑窝，他们不妨今晚就从‘死耗子’开刀。他们将突袭那间宅邸，掘地三尺严密搜查，凡是在里面抓获的每一个人——无论男女老幼——一律当场逮捕，编入罪犯押解队遣送巴黎，按重罪犯与不法之徒过堂审讯，再统统发配流放到卡宴去，我想在那恶劣的气候折磨下，他们准会在苦役中彻底烂掉。你想想看，”这个可恶的恶魔冷笑着总结道，“当面临这样的绝境抉择时，你那位凯尔诺冈家的小妞还会拒绝嫁给你这样一位优秀的爱国者吗？”
“我不知道，”马丹-罗热喃喃道，“我……我……”
“可我心里清楚得很，”卡里耶粗暴地打断他，“能让你如此辉煌地如愿以偿，区区一万法郎实在太便宜你了。一万法郎？你就算拿出十万法郎来谢恩都不为过。”
马丹-罗热站起身，将庞大沉重的身躯挺得笔直。他竭力掩饰着内心对眼前这个逼得自己不得不卑躬屈膝的丑陋恶棍的刻骨鄙夷。
“一万法郎分文不少给你，卡里耶公民，”他一字一顿地说道，“那是我在世上仅存的所有家当了——是我过去四年来辛辛苦苦积攒下的两万五千法郎里仅剩的最后一点骨血。你之前已经拿了五千法郎，剩下的这一万定如数奉上。我绝不吝惜。哪怕拿我二十年的阳寿去换，只要对你有用，我也甘愿奉送，用来酬谢你在对这件比我性命还要重大的事上给予的鼎力相助。”
总督放声大笑，耸了耸肩——说实在的，他打心眼里觉得马丹-罗热公民是个不折不扣的大傻瓜。
“那就这么一言为定，”他说道，“咱们就当这桩买卖敲定了。坦白讲，这事倒颇合我的兴致，不过记着我的警告。对付这帮贵族佬，我更相信我那些沉溺驳船的威力，而不是你这套机关算尽的把戏。不过嘛！

===Original===

it shall never be said that Jean Baptiste Carrier has left a friend in the lurch.”
“I am grateful for your help, citizen Carrier,” said Martin-Roget coldly. Then he added slowly, as if reviewing the situation in his own mind: “Tonight, you say?”
“Yes. Tonight. My Marats under the command of citizen Fleury will make a descent upon the Rat Mort. Those shall be my orders. The place will be swept clean of every man, woman and child who is inside. If your two Kernogans are there﻿ ﻿… well!” he said with a cynical laugh and a shrug of his shoulders, “they can be sent up to Paris with the rest of the herd.”
“The dinner bell has gone long ago,” here interposed young Lalouët drily, “the soup will be stone-cold and the chef red-hot with anger.”
“You are right, citizen Lalouët,” said Carrier as he leaned back in his chair once more and stretched out his long legs at his ease. “We have wasted far too much time already over the affairs of a couple of aristos, who ought to have been at the bottom of the Loire a week ago. The audience is ended,” he added airily, and he made a gesture of overweening condescension, for all the world like the one wherewith the Grand Monarque was wont to dismiss his courtiers.
Chauvelin rose too and quietly turned to the door. He had not spoken a word for the past half-hour, ever since in fact he had put in a conciliatory word on behalf of his impetuous colleague. Whether he had taken an active interest in the conversation or not it were impossible to say. But now, just as he was ready to go, and young Lalouët prepared to close the doors of the audience chamber, something seemed suddenly to occur to him and he called somewhat peremptorily to the young man.
“One moment, citizen,” he said.
“What is it now?” queried the youth insolently, and from his fine eyes there shot a glance of contempt on the meagre figure of the once powerful Terrorist.
“About the Kernogan wench,” continued Chauvelin. “She will have to be conveyed some time before night to the tavern next door. There may be agencies at work on her behalf﻿ ﻿…”
“Agencies?” broke in the boy gruffly. “What agencies?”
“Oh!” said Chauvelin vaguely, “we all know that aristos have powerful friends these days. It will not be over safe to take the girl across after dark from one house to another﻿ ﻿… the alley is badly lighted: the wench will not go willingly.

===Chinese===

绝不能让世人戳脊梁骨，说让·巴蒂斯特·卡里耶在紧要关头撇下朋友不管。”
“对你的关照我感激不尽，卡里耶公民，”马丹-罗热冷淡地说道。随后他放慢语调补充道，仿佛在脑海中重新盘算着全盘局势：“你说是定在今晚？”
“没错。就在今晚。我的‘马拉连’将在弗勒里公民的率领下突击‘死耗子’。这道命令我自会签发。里面的每一个男女老幼都将被一网打尽扫荡一空。要是你那两个凯尔诺冈家的人也在里面……嘿！”他冷笑着耸了耸肩说道，“他们大可夹在这群牲口里一道押送去巴黎。”
“开饭的钟声早就响过了，”年轻的拉卢埃在此冷冰冰地插话道，“汤早凉透了，主厨也早就气得火冒三丈了。”
“你说得对，拉卢埃公民，”卡里耶再次仰靠在椅子上，惬意地伸长了双腿，“为了那两个早该在一周前沉入卢瓦尔河底的贵族佬，咱们已经白白浪费了太多宝贵时间。召见结束，”他漫不经心地补充道，摆出一副目空一切的恩赐手势，活脱脱就是当年太阳王屏退侍臣时的傲慢做派。
肖维兰也站起身来，静静地走向房门。过去半小时里他未置一词，确切地说，自从替那位急躁的同僚说了句圆场话之后，他就一直保持缄默。至于他对这场对话究竟是冷眼旁观还是暗自留心，外人根本无从知晓。然而就在他正准备离去、年轻的拉卢埃也准备合上接见室大门的一刹那，他似乎猛然想起了什么，颇为专横地叫住了那少年。
“且慢，公民，”他说道。
“又有何贵干？”少年蛮横无理地反问道，俊秀的双眸中掠过一丝对这位昔日权势煊赫的恐怖分子干瘦身躯的轻蔑之色。
“关于凯尔诺冈家的那个小妞，”肖维兰继续说道，“在入夜之前，必须把她转移到隔壁的小酒馆里去。暗中或许有人正企图营救她……”
“营救势力？”少年粗鲁地打断道，“什么势力？”
“嗨！”肖维兰含糊其辞地应道，“咱们都清楚，如今贵族佬背后常有手眼通天的同党撑腰。天黑之后把那姑娘从一间屋子押送到另一间屋子并不十分安全……小巷里昏暗无光，那小娘们又绝不会甘心就范。

===Original===

She might scream and create a disturbance and draw﻿ ﻿… er﻿ ﻿… those same unknown agencies to her rescue. I think a body of Marats should be told off to convey her to the Rat Mort.﻿ ﻿…”
Young Lalouët shrugged his shoulders.
“That’s your affair,” he said curtly. “Eh, Carrier?” And he glanced over his shoulder at the proconsul, who at once assented.
Martin-Roget﻿—struck by his colleague’s argument﻿—would have interposed, but Carrier broke in with one of his uncontrolled outbursts of fury.
“Ah ça,” he exclaimed, “enough of this now. Citizen Lalouët is right and I have done enough for you already. If you want the Kernogan wench to be at the Rat Mort, you must see to getting her there yourself. She is next door, what? I won’t have anything to do with it and I won’t have my Marats implicated in the affair either. Name of a dog! have I not told you that I am beset with spies? It would of a truth be a climax if I was denounced as having dragged aristos to a house of ill-fame and then had them arrested there as malefactors! Now out with you! I have had enough of this! If your rabble is at the Rat Mort tonight, they shall be arrested with all the other cutthroats. That is my last word. The rest is your affair. Lalouët! the door!”
And without another word, and without listening to further protests from Martin-Roget or Chauvelin, Jacques Lalouët closed the doors of the audience chamber in their face.

===Chinese===

她可能会尖叫喧闹，引来……唔……那些潜伏在暗处的同党前来接应。依我看，应当抽调一个小队的‘马拉连’把她押送到‘死耗子’去……”
年轻的拉卢埃耸了耸肩。
“那是你们自个儿的事，”他冷硬地说道，“你说呢，卡里耶？”他回头瞥了一眼总督，后者当即表示赞同。
马丹-罗热深觉同僚所言极有道理，正欲开口附议，卡里耶却再次暴跳如雷，狂怒地爆发开来。
“啊，行了！”他大声斥责道，“都给我住嘴。拉卢埃公民说得对，我为你们办的事已经够多了。你要是想把凯尔诺冈家的小妞弄到‘死耗子’去，就得自己想办法把她送过去。她不就住在隔壁吗？我绝不插手这桩破事，也绝不容许我的‘马拉连’卷入这趟浑水。见鬼！我刚才不是告诉过你我正被密探盯梢吗？要是我被人告发，说我把贵族佬强行拖进风月黑窝再以恶徒罪名就地抓捕，那可真成了天大的笑话！现在统统给我滚出去！我受够了！要是你那些余孽今晚在‘死耗子’，他们就会跟其他所有凶徒一起被一网打尽。这是我的最终裁定。剩下的全由你们自理。拉卢埃！关门！”
不等马丹-罗热或肖维兰再作争辩，雅克·拉卢埃便二话不说，当着他们的面猛地把接见室的大门严丝合缝地重重合上了。

#### VII / 第七节
===Original===

Outside on the landing, Martin-Roget swore a violent, all comprehensive oath.
“To think that we are under the heel of that skunk!” he said.
“And that in the pursuit of our own ends we have need of his help!” added Chauvelin with a sigh.
“If it were not for that﻿ ﻿… And even now,” continued Martin-Roget moodily, “I doubt what I can do. Yvonne de Kernogan will not follow me willingly either to the Rat Mort or elsewhere, and if I am not to have her conveyed by the guard﻿ ﻿…”
He paused and swore again. His companion’s silence appeared to irritate him.
“What do you advise me to do, citizen Chauvelin?” he asked.
“For the moment,” replied Chauvelin imperturbably, “I should advise you to join me in a walk along the quay as far as Le Bouffay. I have work to see to inside the building and the northwesterly wind is sure to be of good counsel.”
An angry retort hovered on Martin-Roget’s lips, but after a second or two he succeeded in holding his irascible temper in check. He gave a quick sigh of impatience.
“Very well,” he said curtly. “Let us to Le Bouffay by all means. I have much to think on, and as you say the northwesterly wind may blow away the cobwebs which for the nonce do o’ercloud my brain.”
And the two men wrapped their mantles closely round their shoulders, for the air was keen. Then they descended the staircase of the hotel and went out into the street.

===Chinese===

来到楼梯平台上，马丹-罗热狠狠地咬牙切齿咒骂了一通。
“一想到咱们居然得在这臭无赖的脚底下讨生活！”他说道。
“而且为了达成咱们各自的目的，还偏偏不得不仰仗他的相助！”肖维兰叹了口气补充道。
“若不是为了这个……哪怕到现在，”马丹-罗热阴郁地继续说道，“我心里也没底该怎么下手。伊冯娜·凯尔诺冈绝不可能自愿跟着我去‘死耗子’或者别的地方，要是不能调派卫兵押送她……”
他顿了顿，又骂了一句。同伴的沉默似乎让他愈发烦躁。
“你建议我该怎么办，肖维兰公民？”他问道。
“眼下嘛，”肖维兰心平气和地回答，“我建议你随我沿着码头一路散步走到布费去。我在那栋楼里还有公事要处理，而西北风准能给人带来好主意。”
一句暴怒的反驳几乎脱口而出，但过了一两秒，马丹-罗热还是强行压住了急躁的脾气。他不耐烦地轻轻叹了一口气。
“好吧，”他冷冰冰地说道，“那就去布费走一遭。我有太多事情需要琢磨，正如你所言，西北风或许能吹散此刻笼罩在我心头的乱麻。”
寒风凛冽刺骨，两人将斗篷紧紧裹在肩头。随后，他们走下公馆的楼梯，迈步走进了大街。

## II Le Bouffay / 第二章 布费城楼
===Original===



===Chinese===

第二章 布费城楼

#### I / 第一节
===Original===

In the centre of the Place the guillotine stood idle﻿—the paint had worn off her sides﻿—she looked weatherbeaten and forlorn﻿—stern and forbidding still, but in a kind of sullen loneliness, with the ugly stains of crimson on her, turned to rust and grime.
The Place itself was deserted, in strange contrast to the bustle and the movement which characterised it in the days when the death of men, women and children was a daily spectacle here for the crowd. Then a constant stream of traffic, of carts and of tumbrils, of soldiers and gaffers encumbered it in every corner, now a few tumbledown booths set up against the frontage of the grim edifice﻿—once the stronghold of the Dukes of Brittany, now little else but a huge prison﻿—a few vendors and still fewer purchasers of the scanty wares displayed under their ragged awnings, one or two idlers loafing against the mud-stained walls, one or two urchins playing in the gutters were the only signs of life. Martin-Roget with his colleague Chauvelin turned into the Place from the quay﻿—they walked rapidly and kept their mantles closely wrapped under their chin, for the afternoon had turned bitterly cold. It was then close upon five o’clock﻿—a dark, moonless, starless night had set in with only a suspicion of frost in the damp air; but a blustering northwesterly wind blowing down the river and tearing round the narrow streets and the open Place, caused passersby to muffle themselves, shivering, yet tighter in their cloaks.
Martin-Roget was talking volubly and excitedly, his tall, broad figure towering above the slender form of his companion. From time to time he tossed his mantle aside with an impatient, febrile gesture and then paused in the middle of the Place, with one hand on the other man’s shoulder, marking a point in his discourse or emphasising his argument with short staccato sentences and brief, emphatic words. Chauvelin﻿—placid and impenetrable as usual﻿—listened much and talked little. He was ready to stand still or to walk along just as his colleague’s mood demanded; in the darkness, and with the collar of a large mantle pulled tightly up to his ears, it was impossible to guess by any sign in his face what was going on in his mind.

===Chinese===

在广场正中央，断头台静静地伫立着，无人问津——两侧的油漆早已剥落殆尽——显得饱经风霜且形单影只——虽然依旧严酷森然，却带着一种阴沉的孤寂，台身斑驳丑陋的猩红血迹如今已化作斑斑锈迹与陈年污垢。
广场上一片荒凉冷清，与昔日此地人头攒动、熙熙攘攘的景象形成了诡异的鲜明对照；在那些日子里，男女老幼的殒命曾是每日供看客围观的常态。那时，车流不息，囚车与运货大车往来穿梭，士兵与老汉挤满了每个角落；而如今，唯有几间倚着那座阴森建筑门面搭建的破旧货摊——那里曾是布列塔尼公爵的坚固要塞，如今却不过是一座巨型监狱——摊篷破烂不堪，底下陈列着少得可怜的零星货物，摊贩寥寥无几，主顾更是屈指可数，一两个闲汉靠在满是泥污的墙根下游手好闲，一两个顽童在排水沟边嬉闹，构成了这里仅有的生气。马丹-罗热与同僚肖维兰从码头拐进了广场——两人脚步匆匆，斗篷紧紧裹在颔下，因为午后天气已变得奇寒彻骨。此时已近下午五点——四周已是一片漆黑无月、不见星光的夜色，潮湿的空气中隐隐夹带着一丝薄霜；呼啸的西北风顺着河道猛刮下来，在狭窄的街道和开阔的广场上肆虐席卷，逼得过往行人冻得瑟瑟发抖，把身上的斗篷裹得更紧了。
马丹-罗热滔滔不绝、情绪亢奋地高谈阔论，他那高大魁梧的身形在身旁同僚瘦小的身影前显得鹤立鸡群。他不时带着焦躁狂热的举动一把撩开斗篷，旋即在广场中央骤然驻足，一手重重按在对方肩头，用短促有力的断句与斩钉截铁的词句来申明自己的观点或强调论据。肖维兰则一如既往地神色泰然、高深莫测，多听少言。他任由同僚的心绪摆布，或驻足停留，或迈步前行；在浓重的暮色中，厚大斗篷的衣领紧紧竖起遮住了耳朵，单凭脸上的任何神色，根本无法窥见他心底正转着何等念头。

===Original===

They were a strange contrast these two men﻿—temperamentally as well as physically﻿—even though they had so much in common and were both the direct products of that same social upheaval which was shaking the archaic dominion of France to its very foundations. Martin-Roget, tall, broad-shouldered, bull-necked, the typical self-educated peasant, with square jaw and flat head, with wide bony hands and spatulated fingers: and Chauvelin﻿—the aristocrat turned demagogue, thin and frail-looking, bland of manner and suave of speech, with delicate hands and pale, almost ascetic face.
The one represented all that was most brutish and sensual in this fight of one caste against the other, the thirst for the other’s blood, the human beast that has been brought to bay through wrongs perpetrated against it by others and has turned upon its oppressors, lashing out right and left with blind and lustful fury at the crowd of tyrants that had kept him in subjection for so long. Whilst Chauvelin was the personification of the spiritual side of this bloody Revolution﻿—the spirit of cool and calculating reprisals that would demand an eye for an eye and see that it got two. The idealist who dreams of the righteousness of his own cause and the destruction of its enemies, but who leaves to others the accomplishment of all the carnage and the bloodshed which his idealism has demanded, and which his reason has appraised as necessary for the triumph of which he dreams. Chauvelin was the man of thought and Martin-Roget the man of action. With the one, revenge and reprisals were selfish desires, the avenging of wrongs done to himself or to his caste, hatred for those who had injured him or his kindred. The other had no personal feelings of hatred: he had no personal wrongs to avenge: his enemies were the enemies of his party, the erstwhile tyrants who in the past had oppressed an entire people. Every man, woman or child who was not satisfied with the present Reign of Terror, who plotted or planned for its overthrow, who was not ready to see husband, father, wife or child sacrificed for the ultimate triumph of the Revolution was in Chauvelin’s sight a noxious creature, fit only to be trodden under heel and ground into subjection or annihilation as a danger to the State.

===Chinese===

这两个男人在性情与体格上形成了奇异的强烈反差，尽管他们拥有极多共通之处，且同是那场将法兰西陈旧统治撼动至根基的社会动荡所直接孕育的产物。马丹-罗热生得高大魁梧、虎背熊腰，生就一副典型的自学成才农夫模样，下颌方正、扁平脑袋，一双粗大的骨感手掌上长着铲形的粗粝手指；而肖维兰则是贵族出身摇身一变的煽动家，形销骨立、弱不禁风，举止温和、言辞圆滑，手掌纤细修长，面容苍白且近乎苦行僧般冷峻。
在这场阶级对抗的殊死搏杀中，前者代表了最野蛮、最肉欲的一面：对敌方鲜血的饥渴，被昔日强加于己的屈辱逼入绝境而悍然反扑的困兽，带着盲目嗜血的狂怒向长期奴役压迫他的昔日暴君们左右开弓、大肆屠戮。而肖维兰则是这场血腥大革命精神层面的化身——冷静而深谋远虑的报复之灵，不仅主张以眼还眼，更要变本加厉加倍讨还。他是一个狂热的理想主义者，梦想着自身事业的至高正义以及彻底歼灭政敌，却把理想所要求的、理智认定为了梦中胜利所必须付出的一切屠杀与流血牺牲，统统留给他人去操刀代劳。肖维兰是深思熟虑的谋士，马丹-罗热则是冲锋陷阵的干将。对后者而言，复仇与清算不过是私欲的宣泄，是洗雪施加于自身或本阶级的屈辱，是针对伤害过他或其血亲之人的刻骨私仇。而前者心中绝无任何私怨宿仇：他没有任何个人冤仇需要报雪；他的仇敌全是大党的公敌，是昔日骑在全体人民头上作威作福的旧暴君。在肖维兰眼中，凡是对当下的恐怖统治稍有微词者，凡是暗中密谋图谋颠覆者，凡是不愿为了革命终极胜利而甘愿牺牲丈夫、父亲、妻子或骨肉者，无一不是祸国殃民的害虫，作为危害国家的毒瘤，理应被狠狠践踏在脚下，碾碎臣服，直至彻底消灭。

===Original===

Martin-Roget was the personification of sansculottism, of rough manners and foul speech﻿—he chafed against the conventions which forced him to wear decent clothes and boots on his feet﻿—he would gladly have seen everyone go about the streets half-naked, unwashed, a living sign of that downward levelling of castes which he and his friends stood for, and for which they had fought and striven and committed every crime which human passions let loose could invent. Chauvelin, on the other hand, was one of those who wore fine linen and buckled shoes and whose hands were delicately washed and perfumed whilst they signed decrees which sent hundreds of women and children to a violent and cruel death.
The one trod in the paths of Danton: the other followed in the footsteps of Robespierre.

===Chinese===

马丹-罗热是无套裤汉精神、粗鄙举止与污言秽语的典型化身——他极度反感那些逼迫他穿上体面衣衫和皮靴的陈规戒律——他倒巴不得街上人人半裸着身子、蓬头垢面地招摇过市，好成为他与同党所主张的阶级均等化活生生的标志，而正是为了这一目标，他们才并肩奋战、苦苦追寻，犯下了人类放纵情欲所能构想出的每一桩罪行。相反，肖维兰则是那种身着精致亚麻华服、足蹬带扣皮鞋的人物；他在签署将成百上千妇孺送上惨烈绝境的处决令时，一双手甚至洗得干干净净、散发着幽微的香气。
一个亦步亦趋跟随着丹东的脚步，另一个则紧紧追随着罗伯斯庇尔的足迹。

#### II / 第二节
===Original===

Together the two men mounted the outside staircase which leads up past the lodge of the concierge and through the clerk’s office to the interior of the stronghold. Outside the monumental doors they had to wait a moment or two while the clerk examined their permits to enter.
“Will you come into my office with me?” asked Chauvelin of his companion; “I have a word or two to add to my report for the Paris courier tonight. I won’t be long.”
“You are still in touch with the Committee of Public Safety then?” asked Martin-Roget.
“Always,” replied the other curtly.
Martin-Roget threw a quick, suspicious glance on his companion. Darkness and the broad brim of his sugar-loaf hat effectually concealed even the outlines of Chauvelin’s face, and Martin-Roget fell to musing over one or two things which Carrier had blurted out awhile ago. The whole of France was overrun with spies these days﻿—everyone was under suspicion, everyone had to be on his guard. Every word was overheard, every glance seen, every sign noted.
What was this man Chauvelin doing here in Nantes? What reports did he send up to Paris by special courier? He, the miserable failure who had ceased to count was nevertheless in constant touch with that awful Committee of Public Safety which was wont to strike at all times and unexpectedly in the dark. Martin-Roget shivered beneath his mantle. For the first time since his schemes of vengeance had wholly absorbed his mind he regretted the freedom and safety which he had enjoyed in England, and he marvelled if the miserable game which he was playing would be worth the winning in the end. Nevertheless he had followed Chauvelin without comment. The man appeared to exercise a fascination over him﻿—a kind of subtle power, which emanated from his small shrunken figure, from his pale keen eyes and his well-modulated, suave mode of speech.

===Chinese===

两人一同踏上了室外石阶，这条石阶经过门房的值班室，穿过书记员公署，通向堡垒内侧。在那扇宏伟庄严的大门前，他们不得不驻足片刻，等候书记员查验两人的入内许可证。
“要随我去我的公事房坐坐吗？”肖维兰询问同伴道，“我今晚要给发往巴黎的特派信使添上几句密报。用不了多久。”
“这么说，你如今依旧与救国委员会保持着联系？”马丹-罗热问道。
“自始至终，”对方简短地答道。
马丹-罗热朝身旁的同伴投去警惕而怀疑的一瞥。暮色苍茫，加之那顶高冠礼帽的宽大帽檐，将肖维兰的面部轮廓遮挡得严严实实；马丹-罗热不由自主地琢磨起方才卡里耶随口脱口而出的几句话。如今整个法兰西四处密探横行——人人都处在被怀疑的阴影下，人人都必须提心吊胆、严加防范。每一句话都会被暗中偷听，每一个眼神都会被看在眼里，每一个细微动静都会被记录在案。
这个叫肖维兰的人跑到南特究竟有何企图？他通过专程信使呈送巴黎的密报究竟写了些什么？他分明是个早已失势、无足轻重的可悲弃卒，却为何依然同那个惯于在暗处随时随地猝然发难的恐怖救国委员会保持着密切往来？马丹-罗热在厚厚的斗篷下禁不住打了个寒战。自从全副心思被复仇密谋彻底占据以来，他破天荒第一次开始怀念起在英格兰所享有的自由与安宁，他甚至暗自纳罕，自己眼下玩的这出可悲勾当到底值不值得最终赢下。尽管如此，他还是默默无言地跟在肖维兰身后。这个男人身上似乎散发着一种奇特的诱惑力——一种源自他瘦小干瘪的身躯、苍白锐利的目光以及抑扬顿挫、温文尔雅言辞间的微妙掌控力。

#### III / 第三节
===Original===

The clerk had handed the two men their permits back. They were allowed to pass through the gates.
In the hall some half-dozen men were nominally on guard﻿—nominally, because discipline was not over strict these days, and the men sat or lolled about the place; two of them were intent on a game of dominoes, another was watching them, whilst the other three were settling some sort of quarrel among themselves which necessitated vigorous and emphatic gestures and the copious use of expletives. One man, who appeared to be in command, divided his time impartially between the domino-players and those who were quarrelling.
The vast place was insufficiently lighted by a chandelier which hung from the ceiling and a couple of small oil-lamps placed in the circular niches in the wall opposite the front door.
No one took any notice of Martin-Roget or of Chauvelin as they crossed the hall, and presently the latter pushed open a door on the left of the main gates and held it open for his colleague to pass through.
“You are sure that I shall not be disturbing you?” queried Martin-Roget.
“Quite sure,” replied the other curtly. “And there is something which I must say to you﻿ ﻿… where I know that I shall not be overheard.”
Then he followed Martin-Roget into the room and closed the door behind him. The room was scantily furnished with a square deal table in the centre, two or three chairs, a broken-down bureau leaning against one wall and an iron stove wherein a meagre fire sent a stream of malodorous smoke through sundry cracks in its chimney-pipe. From the ceiling there hung an oil-lamp the light of which was thrown down upon the table, by a large green shade made of cardboard.
Chauvelin drew a chair to the bureau and sat down; he pointed to another and Martin-Roget took a seat beside the table. He felt restless and excited﻿—his nerves all on the jar: his colleague’s calm, sardonic glance acted as a further irritant to his temper.
“What is it that you wished to say to me, citizen Chauvelin?” he asked at last.
“Just a word, citizen,” replied the other in his quiet urbane manner. “I have accompanied you faithfully on your journey to England: I have placed my feeble powers at your disposal: awhile ago I stood between you and the proconsul’s wrath. This, I think, has earned me the right of asking what you intend to do.”

===Chinese===

书记员将通行证交还给了两人，准许他们穿过大门入内。
在前厅里，约莫有半打卫兵在名义上当值守卫——之所以说是名义上的，是因为这年头的纪律实在算不上严整，卫兵们有的散坐着，有的东倒西歪地倚靠在各处；其中两人正聚精会神地下着多米诺骨牌，另一人在旁观战，剩下的三个人则在彼此争执着什么，手舞足蹈、慷慨激昂，嘴里还连珠炮似地夹杂着污言秽语。一个似乎是领头头目的男子，则颇为公正地把心思平分在下骨牌和吵架的两拨人之间。
头顶天花板上悬挂着一盏吊灯，加上正对大门的墙壁圆形壁龛里放置的两盏小油灯，微弱的光线勉强照亮了这间空旷的大厅。
当马丹-罗热与肖维兰穿过前厅时，根本无人对他们投以一瞥；很快，肖维兰推开了大门左侧的一扇门，撑着门扇让同僚先进去。
“你确定我不会打扰到你办公？”马丹-罗热问道。
“完全不会，”对方干脆地答道，“而且我正好有几句话必须对你讲……在没人能偷听到的地方讲。”
说罢，他尾随马丹-罗热走进房间，反手带上了房门。房间陈设极为简陋，正中摆着一张松木方桌，两三把椅子，一张靠墙立着的破旧写字台，还有一座生着微弱炭火的铁炉子，烟囱管上的几道裂缝正不断往外冒着难闻的刺鼻浓烟。天花板上垂下一盏油灯，上面罩着一个厚大的绿硬纸灯罩，将光线集中投射在桌面上。
肖维兰拉过一把椅子在写字台前坐下；他抬手示意了一下另一把椅子，马丹-罗热便在桌旁坐了下来。他感到烦躁难安、心绪浮躁——神经始终处于高度亢奋紧绷的状态；而同僚那冷静而讥诮的目光，更是进一步挑动着他易怒的情绪。
“你到底有什么话想对我说，肖维兰公民？”他终于按捺不住问道。
“不过是闲话几句，公民，”对方用惯常那种平和文雅的腔调答道，“英格兰之行我一路忠心耿耿相随，尽我微薄之力听凭调遣；方才我又在总督的雷霆暴怒前替你挡了一灾。我想，凭这些，我总该有资格过问一下你接下来究竟打算如何行事吧。”

===Original===

“I don’t know about the right,” retorted Martin-Roget gruffly, “but I don’t mind telling you. As you remarked awhile ago the Northwest wind is wont to be of good counsel. I have thought the matter over whilst I walked with you along the quay and I have decided to act on Carrier’s suggestion. Our eminent proconsul said just now that it was the duty of every true patriot to marry an aristo, an he be free and Chance puts a comely wench in his way. I mean,” he added with a cynical laugh, “to act on that advice and marry Yvonne de Kernogan﻿ ﻿… if I can.”
“She has refused you up to now?”
“Yes﻿ ﻿… up to now.”
“You have threatened her﻿—and her father?”
“Yes﻿—both. Not only with death but with shame.”
“And still she refuses?”
“Apparently,” said Martin-Roget with ever-growing irritation.
“It is often difficult,” rejoined Chauvelin meditatively, “to compel these aristos. They are obstinate.﻿ ﻿…”
“Oh! don’t forget that I am in a position now to bring additional pressure on the wench. That lout Carrier has splendid ideas﻿—a brute, what? but clever and full of resource. That suggestion of his about the Rat Mort is splendid.﻿ ﻿…”
“You mean to try and act on it?”
“Of course I do,” said Martin-Roget roughly. “I am going over presently to my sister’s house to see the Kernogan wench again, and to have another talk with her. Then if she still refuses, if she still chooses to scorn the honourable position which I offer her, I shall act on Carrier’s suggestion. It will be at the Rat Mort tonight that she and I will have our final interview, and there when I dangle the prospect of Cayenne and the convict’s brand before her, she may not prove so obdurate as she has been up to now.”
“H’m! That is as may be,” was Chauvelin’s dry comment. “Personally I am inclined to agree with Carrier. Death, swift and sure﻿—the Loire or the guillotine﻿—is the best that has yet been invented for traitors and aristos. But we won’t discuss that again. I know your feelings in the matter and in a measure I respect them. But if you will allow me I would like to be present at your interview with the soi-disant Lady Anthony Dewhurst. I won’t disturb you and I won’t say a word﻿ ﻿… but there is something I would like to make sure of.﻿ ﻿…”
“What is that?”
“Whether the wench has any hopes﻿ ﻿…” said Chauvelin slowly, “whether she has received a message or has any premonition﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

“谈不谈得上资格且两说，”马丹-罗热没好气地回敬道，“不过我倒不介意向你全盘托出。正如你刚才所说，西北风往往能给人带来好主意。方才同你沿着码头走来时，我把整件事反复权衡了一番，决定采纳卡里耶的提议。咱们那位显赫的总督大人刚才不是说了吗，只要自身未娶且机缘巧合撞上标致的小妞，娶个贵族女子乃是每一个真正爱国者的神圣天职。我打算，”他冷笑了一声补充道，“遵照这个忠告行事，迎娶伊冯娜·凯尔诺冈……只要能办到的话。”
“迄今为止她始终拒绝？”
“是的……直到此刻依然如此。”
“你拿她父亲和她的生死要挟过她了？”
“是的——两个人都威胁过了。不仅以死相逼，更以奇耻大辱相威胁。”
“可她依旧不从？”
“显然如此，”马丹-罗热愈发恼怒地说道。
“强扭这些贵族佬的心意往往难如登天，”肖维兰若有所思地附和道，“他们向来顽固不化……”
“哈！可别忘了，我现在有的是手段对那个小贱人施加双重压力。卡里耶那个粗汉脑子灵光得很——虽是个畜生，对吧？但确实老谋深算、诡计多端。他关于‘死耗子’酒馆的提议简直妙不可言……”
“你真打算付诸实施？”
“那还用说，”马丹-罗热粗声粗气地说道，“我待会儿就去我姐姐家再见见凯尔诺冈家那个小妞，跟她做个最后的了断。如果她依旧顽抗不从，如果她偏要唾弃我施舍给她的体面名分，那我就按卡里耶的建议办。今晚在‘死耗子’，她和我将进行最后的交锋；到了那儿，等我把发配卡宴和烙印苦役的凄惨下场摆在她面前，我就不信她还能像以往那般冥顽不灵。”
“唔！这也难说得很，”肖维兰干巴巴地评价道，“依我看，我倒更赞同卡里耶的处置。死亡迅捷而彻底——无论是卢瓦尔河的浪涛还是断头台的钢刀——向来是对付叛徒与贵族佬最万无一失的良方。不过咱们不必再为此争辩了。我明白你在这桩事上的心思，在某种程度上我也予以尊重。但如果你肯通融，我倒想旁听你与那位所谓的安东尼·迪尤赫斯特勋爵夫人的会面。我绝不打扰你们，也绝不多插半句话……但我心里有些事情必须亲自求证……”
“求证什么？”
“求证那小妞是否还在眼巴巴指望着有人搭救，

===Original===

whether in short she thinks that outside agencies are at work on her behalf.”
“Tshaw!” exclaimed Martin-Roget impatiently, “you are still harping on that Scarlet Pimpernel idea.”
“I am,” retorted the other drily.
“As you please. But understand, citizen Chauvelin, that I will not allow you to interfere with my plans, whilst you go off on one of those wild-goose chases which have already twice brought you into disrepute.”
“I will not interfere with your plans, citizen,” rejoined Chauvelin with unwonted gentleness, “but let me in my turn impress one thing upon you, and that is that unless you are as wary as the serpent, as cunning as the fox, all your precious plans will be upset by that interfering Englishman whom you choose to disregard.”
“What do you mean?”
“I mean that I know him﻿—to my cost﻿—and you do not. But you will, an I am not gravely mistaken, make acquaintance with him ere your great adventure with these Kernogan people is successfully at an end. Believe me, citizen Martin-Roget,” he added impressively, “you would have been far wiser to accept Carrier’s suggestion and let him fling that rabble into the Loire for you.”
“Pshaw! you are not childish enough to imagine, citizen Chauvelin, that your Englishman can spirit away that wench from under my sister’s eyes? Do you know what my sister suffered at the hands of the Kernogans? Do you think that she is like to forget my father’s ignominious death any more than I am? And she mourns a lover as well as a father﻿—she mourns her youth, her happiness, the mother whom she worshipped. Think you a better gaoler could be found anywhere? And there are friends of mine﻿—lads of our own village, men who hate the Kernogans as bitterly as I do myself﻿—who are only too ready to lend Louise a hand in case of violence. And after that﻿—suppose your magnificent Scarlet Pimpernel succeeded in hoodwinking my sister and in evading the vigilance of a score of determined village lads, who would sooner die one by one than see the Kernogan escape﻿—suppose all that, I say, there would still be the guard at every city gate to challenge. No! no! it couldn’t be done, citizen Chauvelin,” he added with a complacent laugh. “Your Englishman would need the help of a legion of angels, what? to get the wench out of Nantes this time.”
Chauvelin made no comment on his colleague’s impassioned harangue.

===Chinese===

简而言之，求证她是否确信暗中正有外力在为营救她奔走。”
“啐！”马丹-罗热不耐烦地啐道，“你还在死抓着那个‘红花侠’的念头不放。”
“正是，”对方冷硬地回敬道。
“随你的便。但你可得听清楚了，肖维兰公民，我绝不容许你插手我的计划，由着你去捕风捉影追逐幻影——你之前已经因此栽过两次大跟头、声名狼藉了。”
“我绝不会妨碍你的计划，公民，”肖维兰以异乎寻常的平和口吻回应道，“但请容我也郑重告诫你一句：除非你能像毒蛇般警惕、像狐狸般狡黠，否则你那些如意算盘准会被你全然不放在眼里的那个爱管闲事的英国佬搅个稀巴烂。”
“你这话什么意思？”
“我的意思是，我吃过他的大亏，深知他的厉害——而你却一无所知。但若我料想得不错，在这场针对凯尔诺冈一家的报复大业大功告成之前，你少不了要跟他打交道。相信我，马丹-罗热公民，”他语重心长地补充道，“你当初要是采纳卡里耶的提议，由着他替你把那帮贱民扔进卢瓦尔河，那可要英明得多。”
“呸！你总不至于幼稚到以为，肖维兰公民，你的那个英国佬能当着我姐姐的眼皮底下把那个小娘们凭空变走吧？你知道我姐姐当年在凯尔诺冈家手里遭了多大的罪吗？你以为她会比我更容易忘却我父亲被无辜吊死的惨剧吗？她不仅痛失老父，更痛失了心爱的情郎——她埋葬了自己的青春、幸福，以及她无比敬爱的慈母。你想想看，普天之下还能找到比她更尽职尽责的狱卒吗？而且还有我的一帮弟兄——同村的小伙子们，他们对凯尔诺冈家的刻骨仇恨丝毫不在我之下——一旦遇上硬闯，他们随时都乐意帮路易丝一把。退一万步讲——就算你那位神乎其神的红花侠真能蒙蔽我姐姐的双眼，避开二十来个宁死也不肯放跑凯尔诺冈的拼命小伙子的严密看守——就算这一切全让他办成了，城门各处的守军也会严加盘查。绝无可能！肖维兰公民，”他自鸣得意地大笑着补充道，“这一回，你的英国佬就算请来一整队天使助阵，也休想把那小妞活着弄出南特城。”
面对同僚激昂澎湃的长篇大论，肖维兰未置一词。

===Original===

Memory had taken him back to that one day in September in Boulogne when he too had set one prisoner to guard a precious hostage: it brought back to his mind a vision of a strangely picturesque figure as it appeared to him in the window-embrasure of the old castle-hall:1 it brought back to his ears the echo of that quaint, irresponsible laughter, of that lazy, drawling speech, of all that had acted as an irritant on his nerves ere he found himself baffled, foiled, eating out his heart with vain reproach at his own folly.
“I see you are unconvinced, citizen Martin-Roget,” he said quietly, “and I know that it is the fashion nowadays among young politicians to sneer at Chauvelin﻿—the living embodiment of failure. But let me just add this. When you and I talked matters over together at the Bottom Inn, in the wilds of Somersetshire, I warned you that not only was your identity known to the man who calls himself the Scarlet Pimpernel, but also that he knew every one of your plans with regard to the Kernogan wench and her father. You laughed at me then﻿ ﻿… do you remember?﻿ ﻿… you shrugged your shoulders and jeered at what you call my far-fetched ideas﻿ ﻿… just as you do now. Well! will you let me remind you of what happened within four-and-twenty hours of that warning which you chose to disregard?﻿ ﻿… Yvonne de Kernogan was married to Lord Anthony Dewhurst and﻿ ﻿…”
“I know all that, man,” broke in Martin-Roget impatiently. “It was all a mere coincidence﻿ ﻿… the marriage must have been planned long before that﻿ ﻿… your Scarlet Pimpernel could not possibly have had anything to do with it.”
“Perhaps not,” rejoined Chauvelin drily. “But mark what has happened since. Just now when we crossed the Place I saw in the distance a figure flitting past﻿—the gorgeous figure of an exquisite who of a surety is a stranger in Nantes: and carried upon the wings of the northwesterly wind there came to me the sound of a voice which, of late, I have only heard in my dreams. On my soul, citizen Martin-Roget,” he added with earnest emphasis, “I assure you that the Scarlet Pimpernel is in Nantes at the present moment, that he is scheming, plotting, planning to rescue the Kernogan wench out of your clutches.

===Chinese===

回忆将他带回到了九月里在布洛涅的那一天，当时他也曾安排一名囚徒去看守一位珍贵的人质；那幅古堡大厅窗台深处浮现出的奇异而引人注目的身影，再次清晰地浮现在他的脑海中：1那声古怪而玩世不恭的笑声，那慵懒拖沓的腔调，以及在他赫然发现自己惨遭愚弄挫败、只能为自己的愚蠢而徒然噬脐莫及之前，曾深深刺激过他神经的一切，再次回荡在他的耳畔。
“我看出你根本不信，马丹-罗热公民，”他平静地说道，“而且我也清楚，如今年轻政客间正流行嘲笑肖维兰——这个一败涂地的活化身。但请容我再补充一句。当初你我在萨默塞特郡荒原上的谷底客栈促膝长谈时，我曾警告过你，那个自称红花侠的男人不仅早已洞悉了你的真实身份，而且对你针对凯尔诺冈父女的每一个全盘谋划都了如指掌。你当时对我嗤之以鼻……可还记得？……你耸耸肩膀，嘲笑我是在异想天开……正如你此刻的做派一样。好吧！且让我提醒你，就在你对那番警告置若罔闻后不到二十四小时，究竟发生了何事？……伊冯娜·凯尔诺冈便嫁给了安东尼·迪尤赫斯特勋爵，而且……”
“那些事我全清楚，老兄，”马丹-罗热不耐烦地打断道，“那纯属巧合……那桩婚事必定是早有预谋……你的红花侠断不可能同它有半点干系。”
“或许吧，”肖维兰冷冷地回敬道，“但请留心自那之后发生的一切。刚才咱们穿过广场时，我分明瞧见远处晃过一道飞掠而逝的人影——那是一个衣着华贵的花花公子，且绝对是南特城从未谋面的生面孔；伴着呼啸的西北风，一阵近来唯有在梦魇中才会响起的嗓音隐隐传进了我的耳朵。凭我的灵魂起誓，马丹-罗热公民，”他语调极为郑重地补充道，“我敢向你打包票，红花侠此刻就在南特城内，他正在暗中谋划、设局布局，企图把你手中的凯尔诺冈小妞夺走。

===Original===

He will not leave her in your power, on this I would stake my life; she is the wife of one of his dearest friends: he will not abandon her, not while he keeps that resourceful head of his on his shoulders. Unless you are desperately careful he will outwit you; of that I am as convinced as that I am alive.”
“Bah! you have been dreaming, citizen Chauvelin,” rejoined Martin-Roget with a laugh and shrugging his broad shoulders; “your mysterious Englishman in Nantes? Why man! the navigation of the Loire has been totally prohibited these last fourteen days﻿—no carriage, van or vehicle of any kind is allowed to enter the city﻿—no man, woman or child to pass the barriers without special permit signed either by the proconsul himself or by Fleury the captain of the Marats. Why! even I, when I brought the Kernogans in overland from Le Croisic, I was detained two hours outside Nantes while my papers were sent in to Carrier for inspection. You know that, you were with me.”
“I know it,” replied Chauvelin drily, “and yet﻿ ﻿…”
He paused, with one claw-like finger held erect to demand attention. The door of the small room in which they sat gave on the big hall where the half-dozen Marats were stationed, the single window at right angles to the door looked out upon the Place below. It was from there that suddenly there came the sound of a loud peal of laughter﻿—quaint and merry﻿—somewhat inane and affected, and at the sound Chauvelin’s pale face took on the hue of ashes and even Martin-Roget felt a strange sensation of cold creeping down his spine.
For a few seconds the two men remained quite still, as if a spell had been cast over them through that lighthearted peal of rippling laughter. Then equally suddenly the younger man shook himself free of the spell; with a few long strides he was already at the door and out in the vast hall; Chauvelin following closely on his heels.

===Chinese===

他绝不会任由她落在你的掌心里，这一点我敢拿性命担保；她是她生死之交的结发妻子；只要他脖子上那颗机智过人的脑袋还在，他就绝不会对她坐视不管。除非你打起十二分精神严加提防，否则他定会将你玩弄于股掌之间；对此我深信不疑，犹如深信自己还活着一样。”
“哈！你这是青天白日做梦呢，肖维兰公民，”马丹-罗热大笑着耸了耸宽阔的肩膀反驳道，“你那个神神秘秘的英国佬在南特？老兄，卢瓦尔河的航运在过去十四天里早已被彻底封禁——任何马车、货车或车马一概不得擅入城门——任何人等若无总督亲自签署或‘马拉连’上尉弗勒里签发的特别通行证，休想迈过关卡半步。连我带着凯尔诺冈父女从勒克鲁瓦西克走陆路进城时，都被扣在南特城外两个时辰，直到把我的路条送进城由卡里耶亲自过目。这事你是清楚的，当时你就在我身边。”
“我清楚，”肖维兰冷硬地答道，“然而……”
他猛然止住话头，竖起一根干枯如鹰爪般的手指示意肃静。他们所处的小房间房门正通向驻扎着六七个“马拉连”的大厅，而与房门成直角的一扇独窗则俯瞰着下方的广场。正是从那窗外，骤然传来了一阵响亮的笑声——古怪、快活，又透着几分漫不经心的傻气与造作；听到这声笑，肖维兰苍白的面庞瞬间变得面如死灰，就连马丹-罗热也感到一股奇特的寒意顺着脊梁骨直往上蹿。
一连数秒，两个男人僵立原地、一动不动，仿佛全被那阵银铃般轻快跳跃的笑声施了定身咒。然而转瞬之间，年轻人猛然挣脱了束缚；他几个大步跨到了门前，猛地冲进了空旷的大厅；肖维兰紧随其后。

#### IV / 第四节
===Original===

The clock in the tower of the edifice was even then striking five. The Marats in the hall looked up with lazy indifference at the two men who had come rushing out in such an abrupt and excited manner.
“Any stranger been through here?” queried Chauvelin peremptorily of the sergeant in command.
“No,” replied the latter curtly. “How could they, without a permit?”
He shrugged his shoulders and the men resumed their game and their argument. Martin-Roget would have parleyed with them but Chauvelin had already crossed the hall and was striding past the clerk’s office and the lodge of the concierge out toward the open. Martin-Roget, after a moment’s hesitation, followed him.
The Place was wrapped in gloom. From the platform of the guillotine an oil-lamp hoisted on a post threw a small circle of light around. Small pieces of tallow candle, set in pewter sconces, glimmered feebly under the awnings of the booths, and there was a streetlamp affixed to the wall of the old château immediately below the parapet of the staircase, and others at the angles of the Rue de la Monnaye and the narrow Ruelle des Jacobins.
Chauvelin’s keen eyes tried to pierce the surrounding darkness. He leaned over the parapet and peered into the remote angles of the building and round the booths below him.
There were a few people on the Place, some walking rapidly across from one end to the other, intent on business, others pausing in order to make purchases at the booths. Up and down the steps of the guillotine a group of street urchins were playing hide-and-seek. Round the angles of the narrow streets the vague figures of passersby flitted to and fro, now easily discernible in the light of the street lanterns, anon swallowed up again in the darkness beyond. Whilst immediately below the parapet two or three men of the Company Marat were lounging against the walls. Their red bonnets showed up clearly in the flickering light of the street lamps, as did their bare shins and the polished points of their sabots. But of an elegant, picturesque figure such as Chauvelin had described awhile ago there was not a sign.
Martin-Roget leaned over the parapet and called peremptorily:
“Hey there! citizens of the Company Marat!”
One of the red-capped men looked up leisurely.
“Your desire, citizen?”

===Chinese===

古堡钟楼上的大钟恰在此刻敲响了五下。大厅里的“马拉连”卫兵们带着懒洋洋的漠然神色抬起头，打量着这两个猝不及防、神色慌张冲出门来的男人。
“方才有生人从这儿走过吗？”肖维兰神色严厉地厉声盘问领头的军士。
“没有，”军士硬邦邦地回答，“没通行证怎么进得来？”
他耸了耸肩，卫兵们随即又自顾自地下起骨牌、争论起来。马丹-罗热本想同他们理论几句，肖维兰却早已穿过大厅，疾步越过书记员公署与门房值班室，径直朝门外大步走去。略一迟疑，马丹-罗热也快步跟了上去。
广场笼罩在一片深沉的暮色之中。在断头台的高台上，一根立柱上悬挂着的油灯在四周投下一小圈昏黄的光晕。货摊破旧的遮篷下，插在锡烛台上的零星牛油烛头泛着微弱的烛光；在石阶栏杆正下方的古堡旧墙上钉着一盏街灯，而在莫奈街与狭窄的雅各宾小巷拐角处也各有一两盏路灯。
肖维兰锐利的双眼竭力想要刺破周遭浓重的黑夜。他俯身倚在石栏杆上，凝神窥视着城堡深邃的墙角以及下方鳞次栉比的货摊四周。
广场上零零星星有几名行人，有的步履匆匆从一端穿向另一端，忙着赶路；有的在货摊前驻足采买。在断头台的台阶上下，一帮野孩子正兴高采烈地玩着捉迷藏。狭窄街巷的转角处，过路人影影绰绰的模糊身姿来回晃动，时而在街灯光线下清晰可见，转瞬又没入前方的深沉夜色中。而在栏杆的正下方，两三个“马拉连”的队员正懒散地倚墙而立。在街灯忽明忽暗的闪烁中，他们头顶的红便帽分外醒目，同样显眼的还有他们赤条条的光腿以及脚下擦得锃亮的木鞋尖。然而，肖维兰刚才所描绘的那种风度翩翩、引人注目的华贵身影，却连半点踪迹也无。
马丹-罗热探身趴在石栏杆上，专横地高声喊道：
“喂！那边的‘马拉连’公民！”
其中一个戴红便帽的汉子漫不经心地抬起头来。
“有何吩咐，公民？”

===Original===

he queried with insolent deliberation, for they were mighty men, this bodyguard of the great proconsul, his spies and tools in the awesome work of frightfulness which he carried on so ruthlessly.
“Is that you Paul Friche?” queried Martin-Roget in response.
“At your service, citizen,” came the glib reply, delivered not without mock deference.
“Then come up here. I wish to speak with you.”
“I can’t leave my post, nor can my mates,” retorted the man who had answered to the name of Paul Friche. “Come down, citizen, an you desire to speak with us.”
Martin-Roget swore lustily.
“The insolence of that rabble﻿ ﻿…” he murmured.
“Hush! I’ll go,” interposed Chauvelin quickly. “Do you know that man Friche? Is he trustworthy?”
“Yes, I know him. As for being trustworthy﻿ ﻿…” added Martin-Roget with a shrug of the shoulders. “He is a corporal in the Marats and high in favour with commandant Fleury.”
Every second was of value, and Chauvelin was not the man to waste time in useless parleyings. He ran down the stairs at the foot of which one of the red-capped gentry deigned to speak with him.
“Have you seen any strangers across the Place just now?” he queried in a whisper.
“Yes,” replied the man Friche. “Two!”
Then he spat upon the ground and added spitefully: “Aristos, what? In fine clothes﻿—like yourself, citizen﻿ ﻿…”
“Which way did they go?”
“Down the Ruelle des Jacobins.”
“When?”
“Two minutes ago.”
“Why did you not follow them?﻿ ﻿… Aristos and﻿ ﻿…”
“I would have followed,” retorted Paul Friche with studied insolence; “ ’twas you called me away from my duty.”
“After them then!” urged Chauvelin peremptorily. “They cannot have gone far. They are English spies, and remember, citizen, that there’s a reward for their apprehension.”
The man grunted an eager assent. The word “reward” had fired his zeal. In a trice he had called to his mates and the three Marats soon sped across the Place and down the Ruelle des Jacobins where the surrounding gloom quickly swallowed them up.
Chauvelin watched them till they were out of sight, then he rejoined his colleague on the landing at the top of the stairs.

===Chinese===

他慢条斯理、傲慢无礼地盘问道，毕竟这帮作为大总督贴身护卫的人各个手握生杀大权，是卡里耶肆无忌惮推行血腥恐怖统治时的得力耳目与爪牙。
“是你吗，保罗·弗利什？”马丹-罗热高声回应道。
“听凭差遣，公民，”对方油嘴滑舌地回答，话里带着几分嘲弄般的恭顺。
“那就上来。我有话对你说。”
“我不能擅离职守，我的弟兄们也不能，”那个自称保罗·弗利什的汉子反唇相讥，“要是你想同我们搭话，你自己下来吧，公民。”
马丹-罗热恶狠狠地咒骂了一句。
“这帮贱民居然如此猖狂……”他咬牙切齿地咕哝道。
“嘘！我去，”肖维兰急忙拦下他，“你认得这个叫弗利什的人？他可靠吗？”
“认得。至于靠不靠得住嘛……”马丹-罗热耸了耸肩膀补充道，“他是‘马拉连’的下士，深得弗勒里指挥官的器重。”
分秒必争，肖维兰绝不是那种把时间浪费在无谓唇舌上的人。他快步奔下石阶，在台阶底部，一位戴红便帽的爷才屈尊降贵地同他搭起话来。
“方才你们可曾看见有生人穿过广场？”他压低嗓门问道。
“看见了，”弗利什答道，“两个！”
随后他在地上啐了一口唾沫，恶狠狠地补充道：“贵族佬呗，还能是谁？穿着一身锦衣华服——就跟你身上这身差不多，公民……”
“他们往哪边去了？”
“拐进雅各宾小巷了。”
“什么时候的事？”
“两分钟前。”
“你们为什么不去追捕？……明知是贵族佬还……”
“我本打算去追的，”保罗·弗利什故意放肆地回敬道，“可偏偏是你们把老子从当差岗位上叫开的。”
“那就快追啊！”肖维兰厉声催促道，“他们走不远。他们是英国间谍，记着，公民，缉拿他们可是有重赏的。”
汉子喉咙里发出急切的应承声。“赏金”二字瞬间点燃了他的干劲。眨眼工夫，他便招呼起身旁的同伴，三名“马拉连”队员旋即飞奔穿过广场，拐进了雅各宾小巷，身影转瞬被周遭深浓的黑夜彻底吞没。
肖维兰目送他们直至身影消失，随后折身返回了石阶顶端的休息平台上，与同僚汇合。

===Original===

For a second or two longer the click of the men’s sabots upon the stones resounded on the adjoining streets and across the Place, and suddenly that same quaint, merry, somewhat inane laugh woke the echoes of the grim buildings around and caused many a head to turn inquiringly, marvelling who it could be that had the heart to laugh these days in the streets of Nantes.

===Chinese===

又过了一两秒，那几人脚下木鞋踏在石板路面上的哒哒声仍在邻近的街巷和广场上空回荡，冷不丁地，那阵古怪、快活、透着几分漫不经心傻气的笑声再次震响了周遭阴森的楼宇，引得不少行人惊异地回过头来，暗自纳罕在这年头的南特街头，究竟是何方神圣竟有这等闲情雅致放声大笑。

#### V / 第五节
===Original===

Five minutes or so later the three Marats could vaguely be seen recrossing the Place and making their way back to Le Bouffay, where Martin-Roget and Chauvelin still stood on the top of the stairs excited and expectant. At sight of the men Chauvelin ran down the steps to meet them.
“Well?” he queried in an eager whisper.
“We never saw them,” replied Paul Friche gruffly, “though we could hear them clearly enough, talking, laughing and walking very rapidly toward the quay. Then suddenly the earth or the river swallowed them up. We saw and heard nothing more.”
Chauvelin swore and a curious hissing sound escaped his thin lips.
“Don’t be too disappointed, citizen,” added the man with a coarse laugh, “my mate picked this up at the corner of the Ruelle, when, I fancy, we were pressing the aristos pretty closely.”
He held out a small bundle of papers tied together with a piece of red ribbon: the bundle had evidently rolled in the mud, for the papers were covered with grime. Chauvelin’s thin, claw-like fingers had at once closed over them.
“You must give me back those papers, citizen,” said the man, “they are my booty. I can only give them up to citizen-captain Fleury.”
“I’ll give them to the citizen-captain myself,” retorted Chauvelin. “For the moment you had best not leave your post of duty,” he added more peremptorily, seeing that the man made as he would follow him.
“I take orders from no one except﻿ ﻿…” protested the man gruffly.
“You will take them from me now,” broke in Chauvelin with a sudden assumption of command and authority which sat with weird strangeness upon his thin shrunken figure. “Go back to your post at once, ere I lodge a complaint against you for neglect of duty, with the citizen proconsul.”
He turned on his heel and, without paying further heed to the man and his mutterings, he remounted the stone stairs.
“No success, I suppose?” queried Martin-Roget.
“None,” replied Chauvelin curtly.
He had the packet of papers tightly clasped in his hand. He was debating in his mind whether he would speak of them to his colleague or not.
“What did Friche say?” asked the latter impatiently.
“Oh! very little. He and his mates caught sight of the strangers and followed them as far as the quays. But they were walking very fast and suddenly the Marats lost their trace in the darkness.

===Chinese===

约莫五分钟后，隐约可见那三名“马拉连”队员重新穿过广场，折回布费城楼前；马丹-罗热与肖维兰依然焦急而期盼地伫立在石阶顶端。一见那几人走近，肖维兰飞奔下楼梯前去接应。
“如何？”他急切地压低嗓门问道。
“连人影都没见着，”保罗·弗利什没好气地答道，“不过我们听得真真切切，他们一边说话一边大笑，脚底生风朝着码头飞奔。随后突然间，地面或者河流就像把他们吞噬了一样。我们既看不见也听不着了。”
肖维兰暗骂了一句，薄薄的嘴唇间逸出一声古怪的咝咝声。
“别太灰心，公民，”那汉子粗声粗气地大笑着补充道，“我同伴在巷子拐角捡到了这个，我想，当时咱们准是把那些贵族佬咬得死死的。”
他递过一小捆用红丝带系着的文书纸张：那卷文书显然在泥地里打过滚，纸面满是污泥。肖维兰干枯如鹰爪般的细瘦手指一把将其紧紧扣在手中。
“你得把文书还给我，公民，”汉子说道，“那是老子的战利品。我只能亲手交给弗勒里公民上尉。”
“我自会亲自转交给公民上尉，”肖维兰反驳道，“眼下你最好别擅离职守，”见那汉子大有跟上来的架势，他神色严厉地补充道。
“除了长官，老子谁的命令行事都不听……”汉子粗暴地抗议道。
“你现在就得听我的号令，”肖维兰猝然摆出一种居高临下的威严与号令姿态，这与他干瘪瘦小的身形显得出奇相悖，“立刻回你的岗位上去，免得我在公民总督面前告你一状擅离职守。”
他猛然转身，再不理睬那汉子嘴里的低声咒骂，快步踏上了石阶。
“没追上，对吧？”马丹-罗热迎上前问道。
“一无所获，”肖维兰冷硬地答道。
他把那一叠文书死死攥在手心里。脑海中正飞速盘算着到底要不要向同僚透露这些纸张的底细。
“弗利什怎么说的？”后者急不可耐地追问道。
“嗨！没说什么要紧的。他和同伴看见了那几个陌生人的影子，一路尾随追到了码头。但那些人走得奇快无比，突然间‘马拉连’就在夜色里失去了踪迹。

===Original===

It seemed, according to Paul Friche, as if the earth or the night had swallowed them up.”
“And was that all?”
“Yes. That was all.”
“I wonder,” added Martin-Roget with a light laugh and a careless shrug of his wide shoulders, “I wonder if you and I, citizen Chauvelin﻿—and Paul Friche too for that matter﻿—have been the victims of our nerves.”
“I wonder,” assented Chauvelin drily. And﻿—quite quietly﻿—he slipped the packet of papers in the pocket of his coat.
“Then we may as well adjourn. There is nothing else you wish to say to me about that enigmatic Scarlet Pimpernel of yours?”
“No﻿—nothing.”
“And you still would like to hear what the Kernogan wench will say and see how she will look when I put my final proposal before her?”
“If you will allow me.”
“Then come,” said Martin-Roget. “My sister’s house is close by.”

===Chinese===

据保罗·弗利什所言，他们简直就像被大地或黑夜一口吞没了一样。”
“就这些？”
“是的，就这些。”
“我真怀疑，”马丹-罗热轻松地笑了一声，满不在乎地耸了耸宽阔的肩膀补充道，“我真怀疑你和我，肖维兰公民——说实话保罗·弗利什也是一样——是不是全成了神经疑神疑鬼的牺牲品。”
“我也怀疑呢，”肖维兰干硬地附和道。接着——不动声色地——他把那一包文书悄悄塞进了大衣口袋。
“那咱们不妨换个地方。关于你那个神神秘秘的红花侠，你还有别的话要对我讲吗？”
“没有了——没什么了。”
“那你还打算亲耳听听凯尔诺冈家那个小妞会作何答复，亲眼瞧瞧我当面提出最终条件时她是什么神情吗？”
“只要你准许。”
“那就走吧，”马丹-罗热说道，“我姐姐家就在近旁。”

## III The Fowlers / 第三章 捕鸟人
===Original===



===Chinese===

第三章 捕鸟人

#### I / 第一节
===Original===

In order to reach the Carrefour de la Poissonnerie the two men had to skirt the whole edifice of Le Bouffay, walk a little along the quay and turn up the narrow alley opposite the bridge. They walked on in silence, each absorbed in his own thoughts.
The house occupied by the citizeness Adet lay back a little from the others in the street. It was one of an irregular row of mean, squalid, tumbledown houses, some of them little more than lean-to sheds built into the walls of Le Bouffay. Most of them had overhanging roofs which stretched out like awnings more than halfway across the road, and even at midday shut out any little ray of sunshine which might have a tendency to peep into the street below.
In this year II of the Republic the Carrefour de la Poissonnerie was unpaved, dark and evil-smelling. For two thirds of the year it was ankle-deep in mud: the rest of the time the mud was baked into cakes and emitted clouds of sticky dust under the shuffling feet of the passersby. At night it was dimly lighted by one or two broken-down lanterns which were hung on transverse chains overhead from house to house. These lanterns only made a very small circle of light immediately below them: the rest of the street was left in darkness, save for the faint glimmer which filtrated through an occasional ill-fitting doorway or through the chinks of some insecurely fastened shutter.
The Carrefour de la Poissonnerie was practically deserted in the daytime; only a few children﻿—miserable little atoms of humanity showing their meagre, emaciated bodies through the scanty rags which failed to cover their nakedness﻿—played weird, mirthless games in the mud and filth of the street. But at night it became strangely peopled with vague and furtive forms that were wont to glide swiftly by, beneath the hanging lanterns, in order to lose themselves again in the welcome obscurity beyond: men and women﻿—ill-clothed and unshod, with hands buried in pockets or beneath scanty shawls﻿—their feet, ofttimes bare, making no sound as they went squishing through the mud. A perpetual silence used to reign in this kingdom of squalor and of darkness, where nighthawks alone fluttered their wings; only from time to time a joyless greeting of boon-companions, or the hoarse cough of some wretched consumptive would wake the dormant echoes that lingered in the gloom.

===Chinese===

为了前往鱼市路口，两人不得不绕过整座布费城楼，沿着码头走上一小段，再拐进桥对面的狭窄小巷。他们默默前行，各自沉浸在自己的思绪里。
阿代女公民居住的那所宅子，比街上其他房屋稍往后退缩了些许。那是一排参差不齐、破烂不堪的低矮陋屋，其中有些不过是依附在布费要塞外墙上搭建的单坡斜顶披屋罢了。这些屋子大都顶着向外探出的深挑屋檐，宛如凉棚般遮盖了大半条马路，即便在正午时分，也把任何一丝试图窥探下方街道的阳光挡得严严实实。
在共和国这共和二年里，鱼市路口未铺路面，昏暗且散发着恶臭。一年有大半时间，这里的泥泞没过脚踝；其余时候，烂泥晒成了干硬的硬块，在过往行人拖沓的脚步下扬起阵阵令人发腻的黏糊尘埃。入夜后，悬挂在房屋之间横拉铁链上的几盏破烂提灯散发着微弱昏暗的光线。这些提灯只能在正下方照出一小圈微光；街道的其余部分则完全陷于黑暗之中，唯有偶尔从关不拢的门缝或未关严的百叶窗隙间，透出几分微弱的光亮。
白天，鱼市路口几乎见不到人影；唯有几个瘦骨嶙峋的孩童——这些可怜卑微的苦命人儿透过遮不住赤裸身躯的破衣烂衫露出瘦骨嶙峋的肢体——在街头的污泥脏水里玩着荒诞而毫无欢笑的游戏。然而一到夜晚，这里便诡异地挤满了影影绰绰、鬼鬼祟祟的暗影，惯常从悬吊的提灯下飞快溜过，以便再次没入前方的深沉夜色中：男男女女——衣衫褴褛、赤脚跣足，双手插在口袋或缩在单薄的披肩下——光脚踩在烂泥中发出噗哧噗哧的声响，却不带丝毫脚步声。在这片唯有夜枭扑腾羽翼的污秽与黑暗王国中，向来笼罩着永恒的死寂；唯有时不时传来酒肉朋友间毫无欢愉的寒暄，或是某个可怜痨病鬼粗重嘶哑的咳嗽声，才会惊醒潜伏在阴暗中的沉睡回声。

#### II / 第二节
===Original===

Martin-Roget knew his way about the murky street well enough. He went up to the house which lay a little back from the others. It appeared even more squalid than the rest, not a sound came from within﻿—hardly a light﻿—only a narrow glimmer found its way through the chink of a shutter on the floor above. To right and left of it the houses were tall, with walls that reeked of damp and of filth: from one of these﻿—the one on the left﻿—an iron sign dangled and creaked dismally as it swung in the wind. Just above the sign there was a window with partially closed shutters: through it came the sound of two husky voices raised in heated argument.
In the open space in front of Louise Adet’s house vague forms standing about or lounging against the walls of the neighbouring houses were vaguely discernible in the gloom. Martin-Roget and Chauvelin as they approached were challenged by a raucous voice which came to them out of the inky blackness around.
“Halt! who goes there?”
“Friends!” replied Martin-Roget promptly. “Is citizeness Adet within?”
“Yes! she is!” retorted the man bluntly; “excuse me, friend Adet﻿—I did not know you in this confounded darkness.”
“No harm done,” said Martin-Roget. “And it is I who am grateful to you all for your vigilance.”
“Oh!” said the other with a laugh, “there’s not much fear of your bird getting out of its cage. Have no fear, friend Adet! That Kernogan rabble is well looked after.”
The small group dispersed in the darkness and Martin-Roget rapped against the door of his sister’s house with his knuckles.
“That is the Rat Mort,” he said, indicating the building on his left with a nod of the head. “A very unpleasant neighbourhood for my sister, and she has oft complained of it﻿—but name of a dog! won’t it prove useful this night?”
Chauvelin had as usual followed his colleague in silence, but his keen eyes had not failed to note the presence of the village lads of whom Martin-Roget had spoken. There are no eyes so watchful as those of hate, nor is there aught so incorruptible. Every one of these men here had an old wrong to avenge, an old score to settle with those ci-devant Kernogans who had once been their masters and who were so completely in their power now. Louise Adet had gathered round her a far more efficient bodyguard than even the proconsul could hope to have.

===Chinese===

马丹-罗热对这条阴暗幽深的街道熟门熟路。他径直走向那所比邻舍略微后退的宅子。那屋子看上去比别处更显破败凄凉，屋里听不见半点声响——几乎没有灯光——唯有一道微弱的光亮透过楼上百叶窗的缝隙幽幽射出。左右两侧的房舍高耸，墙壁上弥漫着潮湿与污秽的腥臭气味：其中左侧那栋房舍前悬挂着一面铁招牌，在寒风中摇晃并发出凄厉刺耳的吱嘎声。就在招牌的正上方有一扇半掩着百叶窗的窗户，里面正隐约传出两个嘶哑粗重的嗓门激辩不休的话音。
在路易丝·阿代家门前的空地上，影影绰绰的人影伫立在旁，或懒散地倚靠在邻舍的墙根下，在夜色中依稀可辨。当马丹-罗热与肖维兰走上前时，周遭漆黑的夜色中猝然传来一声粗暴嘶哑的喝问。
“站住！口令？”
“自家人！”马丹-罗热随即应声答道，“阿代女公民在屋里吗？”
“在！她在！”那汉子粗声粗气地回答，“失敬了，阿代老兄——在这该死的黑夜里，我险些没认出你来。”
“不妨事，”马丹-罗热说道，“全仗诸位兄弟警惕戒备，我谢你们还来不及呢。”
“哈！”对方大笑着说道，“你那只鸟儿休想从笼子里飞出去。放一百个心吧，阿代老兄！凯尔诺冈那帮贱骨头被我们盯得插翅难飞。”
聚拢的小团伙随即在夜色中散去，马丹-罗热用指节敲了敲姐姐家的房门。
“那就是‘死耗子’酒馆，”他朝着左侧的那栋建筑点了点头示意道，“对我姐姐来说这邻舍可真够倒胃口的，她以前没少向我抱怨——但见鬼！今晚它可就要派上天大的用场了不是？”
肖维兰一如既往地默默跟在同僚身后，但他那双敏锐的眼睛却早已将马丹-罗热所提及的同村青年尽收眼底。世上再没有比仇恨之眼更警觉的眼睛，也再没有比复仇之火更坚定不移的忠诚了。眼前的每一个汉子都有一桩深仇大恨要报雪，都有一笔陈年宿怨要与那些昔日曾骑在他们头上作威作福、如今却完全沦为阶下囚的前凯尔诺冈家清算。路易丝·阿代在身边聚集起的这批忠实看守，其得力程度甚至连那位总督大人都难望其项背。

===Original===

A moment or two later the door was opened, softly and cautiously, and Martin-Roget asked: “Is that you, Louise?” for of a truth the darkness was almost deeper within than without, and he could not see who it was that was standing by the door.
“Yes! it is,” replied a weary and querulous voice. “Enter quickly. The wind is cruel, and I can’t keep myself warm. Who is with you, Pierre?”
“A friend,” said Martin-Roget drily. “We want to see the aristo.”
The woman without further comment closed the door behind the newcomers. The place now was as dark as pitch, but she seemed to know her way about like a cat, for her shuffling footsteps were heard moving about unerringly. A moment or two later she opened another door opposite the front entrance, revealing an inner room﻿—a sort of kitchen﻿—which was lighted by a small lamp.
“You can go straight up,” she called curtly to the two men.
The narrow, winding staircase was divided from this kitchen by a wooden partition. Martin-Roget, closely followed by Chauvelin, went up the stairs. On the top of these there was a tiny landing with a door on either side of it. Martin-Roget without any ceremony pushed open the door on his right with his foot.
A tallow candle fixed in a bottle and placed in the centre of a table in the middle of the room flickered in the draught as the door flew open. It was bare of everything save a table and a chair, and a bundle of straw in one corner. The tiny window at right angles to the door was innocent of glass, and the northwesterly wind came in an icy stream through the aperture. On the table, in addition to the candle, there was a broken pitcher half-filled with water, and a small chunk of brown bread blotched with stains of mould.
On the chair beside the table and immediately facing the door sat Yvonne Lady Dewhurst. On the wall above her head a hand unused to calligraphy had traced in clumsy characters the words: “*Liberté! Fraternité! Egalité!*” and below that “*ou la Mort.*”

===Chinese===

片刻之后，门被轻轻而谨慎地拉开了一条缝，马丹-罗热问道：“是你吗，路易丝？”因为屋里着实比屋外还要昏黑，他根本看不清站在门边的是谁。
“是的！是我，”一个疲惫而满是怨气的嗓音回答道，“快进屋来。风刮得像刀子一样，我冻得浑身发冷。皮埃尔，跟你一起的是谁？”
“一位朋友，”马丹-罗热冷淡地说道，“我们想见见那个贵族小妞。”
那妇人未再多言，反手关上了大门。屋里此刻黑得伸手不见五指，但她却像猫一样熟门熟路，只听见她那拖沓的脚步声分毫不差地在黑暗中挪动。片刻后，她推开了正对正门的一扇内门，露出了一间内室——一间充当厨房的小屋，里面点着一盏微弱的小油灯。
“你们直接上去吧，”她冷冰冰地冲那两个男人喊道。
狭窄盘旋的楼梯与这间厨房仅隔着一层木板隔断。马丹-罗热在前，肖维兰紧随其后，两人拾级而上。楼梯顶端是一个极狭小的楼梯平台，左右两侧各有一扇门。马丹-罗热毫不客气，飞起一脚便踹开了右手边的那扇门。
随着房门猛然洞开，插在桌心一个瓶子里的牛油蜡烛在穿堂风中剧烈晃动起来。屋里除了正中的一张桌子、一把椅子，以及角落里的一堆麦秸秆外，别无长物。与房门成直角的一扇狭小窗户根本没有安装玻璃，凛冽刺骨的西北风顺着敞开的窗口化作一道寒流倒灌进屋。桌上除了一截烛头外，还放着一个盛着半壶残破瓦罐里的水，以及一小块布满霉斑的黑面包。
在桌旁正对房门的椅子上，坐着安东尼·迪尤赫斯特勋爵夫人伊冯娜。在她头顶上方的墙壁上，一只不惯操笔的笨拙之手用歪歪扭扭的字迹涂写着几个大字：“自由！博爱！平等！”下面赫然写着“否则以死相拼”。

#### III / 第三节
===Original===

The men entered the narrow room and Chauvelin carefully closed the door behind him. He at once withdrew into a remote corner of the room and stood there quite still, wrapped in his mantle, a small, silent, mysterious figure on which Yvonne fixed dark, inquiring eyes.
Martin-Roget, restless and excited, paced up and down the small space like a wild animal in a cage. From time to time exclamations of impatience escaped him and he struck one fist repeatedly against his open palm. Yvonne followed his movements with a quiet, uninterested glance, but Chauvelin paid no heed whatever to him.
He was watching Yvonne ceaselessly, and closely.
Three days’ incarceration in this windswept attic, the lack of decent food and of warmth, the want of sleep and the horror of her present position all following upon the soul-agony which she had endured when she was forcibly torn away from her dear milor, had left their mark on Yvonne Dewhurst’s fresh young face. The look of gravity which had always sat so quaintly on her piquant features had now changed to one of deep and abiding sorrow; her large dark eyes were circled and sunk; they had in them the unnatural glow of fever, as well as the settled look of horror and of pathetic resignation. Her soft brown hair had lost its lustre; her cheeks were drawn and absolutely colourless.
Martin-Roget paused in his restless walk. For a moment he stood silent and absorbed, contemplating by the flickering light of the candle all the havoc which his brutality had wrought upon Yvonne’s dainty face.
But Yvonne after a while ceased to look at him﻿—she appeared to be unconscious of the gaze of these two men, each of whom was at this moment only thinking of the evil which he meant to inflict upon her﻿—each of whom only thought of her as a helpless bird whom he had at last ensnared and whom he could crush to death as soon as he felt so inclined.
She kept her lips tightly closed and her head averted. She was gazing across at the unglazed window into the obscurity beyond, marvelling in what direction lay the sea and the shores of England.
Martin-Roget crossed his arms over his broad chest and clutched his elbows with his hands with an obvious effort to keep control over his movements and his temper in check. The quiet, almost indifferent attitude of the girl was exasperating to his overstrung nerves.

===Chinese===

两个男人走进了狭窄的阁楼，肖维兰小心翼翼地随手带上了房门。他随即退到了房间偏僻的角落里，紧裹着斗篷静静伫立，身形矮小、沉默而神秘，伊冯娜一双深邃而探询的明眸正落在他身上。
马丹-罗热焦躁不安、情绪亢奋，在这狭小的空间里像关在铁笼中的困兽般来回踱步。不耐烦的咒骂不时从他口中冒出，他一次又一次用拳头狠命砸向张开的手掌。伊冯娜用平静而漠不关心的眼神注视着他的一举一动，而肖维兰却对他全然视若无睹。
他正目不转睛、全神贯注地死死凝视着伊冯娜。
在这寒风凛冽的阁楼中被幽禁了整整三天，忍饥挨饿、受冻缺眠，加之身陷绝境的恐惧，以及被硬生生从她挚爱的爵爷身边强行掳走时所遭受的撕心裂肺之痛，都在伊冯娜·迪尤赫斯特娇嫩年轻的容颜上刻下了难以磨灭的憔悴印记。那抹往常总在她秀丽脸庞上显得格外俏皮庄重的神情，如今已化作了一抹深沉而挥之不去的哀愁；一双乌黑明亮的大眼睛陷落下去，周围泛着一圈黑晕；眼眸深处不仅闪烁着不正常的狂热光芒，更凝固着深沉的绝望与凄楚的顺从。她那柔顺的栗色长发失去了昔日的光泽；双颊凹陷，惨白得毫无血色。
马丹-罗热停下了焦躁的踱步。他伫立片刻，若有所思地沉默不语，借着摇曳不定的烛光打量着自己的残酷暴行在伊冯娜娇美的面容上留下的斑斑创痕。
然而片刻之后，伊冯娜便不再看他——她仿佛浑然不觉眼前这两个男人的注视，此刻他们心中所转悠的全是如何将更严酷的折磨施加于她——他们每一个都把她视作终于落入罗网的无助雏鸟，只要兴致一来便可随时将其捏碎毙命。
她紧咬着苍白的嘴唇，别过脸去。她隔着没有装玻璃的窗口凝望着外面的沉沉夜色，暗自揣度着大海究竟在何方，英格兰的海岸又究竟在哪个方向。
马丹-罗热双臂抱胸，双手紧紧扣住胳膊肘，显然在拼命克制着自己的举止与脾气。眼前少女那平静得近乎漠然的孤傲神态，深深刺痛并激怒了他本已高度紧绷的神经。

===Original===

“Look here, my girl,” he said at last, roughly and peremptorily, “I had an interview with the proconsul this afternoon. He chides me for my leniency toward you. Three days he thinks is far too long to keep traitors eating the bread of honest citizens and taking up valuable space in our city. Yesterday I made a proposal to you. Have you thought on it?”
Yvonne made no reply. She was still gazing out into nothingness and just at that moment she was very far away from the narrow, squalid room and the company of these two inhuman brutes. She was thinking of her dear milor and of that lovely home at Combwich wherein she had spent three such unforgettable days. She was remembering how beautiful had been the colour of the bare twigs in the chestnut coppice when the wintry sun danced through and in between them and drew fantastic patterns of living gold upon the carpet of dead leaves; and she remembered too how exquisite were the tints of russet and blue on the distant hills, and how quaintly the thrushes had called: “Kiss me quick!” She saw again those trembling leaves of a delicious faintly crimson hue which still hung upon the branches of the scarlet oak, and the early flowering heath which clothed the moors with a gorgeous mantle of rosy amethyst.
Martin-Roget’s harsh voice brought her abruptly back to the hideous reality of the moment.
“Your obstinacy will avail you nothing,” he said, speaking quietly, even though a note of intense irritation was distinctly perceptible in his voice. “The proconsul has given me a further delay wherein to deal leniently with you and with your father if I am so minded. You know what I have proposed to you: Life with me as my wife﻿—in which case your father will be free to return to England or to go to the devil as he pleases﻿—or the death of a malefactor for you both in the company of all the thieves and evildoers who are mouldering in the prisons of Nantes at this moment. Another delay wherein to choose between an honourable life and a shameful death. The proconsul waits. But tonight he must have his answer.”
Then Yvonne turned her head slowly and looked calmly on her enemy.
“The tyrant who murders innocent men, women and children,” she said, “can have his answer now. I choose death which is inevitable in preference to a life of shame.”

===Chinese===

“听着，姑娘，”他终于粗暴而专横地开口道，“今天下午我刚晋见过总督。他严厉训斥我对你太过心慈手软。在他看来，留着叛徒白白消耗诚实市民的面包、霸占我们城里的宝贵空地达三天之久，简直荒唐透顶。昨天我对你提过条件，你考虑得如何了？”
伊冯娜默不作声。她依然凝望着虚无缥缈的黑夜，此时此刻，她的心神早已远远飞离了这间狭窄破败的阁楼，远离了这两个毫无人性的恶魔。她心中牵挂着她亲爱的爵爷，思念着在库姆威治那座美丽的庄园里，她曾度过的那段永生难忘的三日时光。她依稀记得，当冬日的阳光透过栗树矮林间穿梭跳跃、在落叶织就的地毯上洒下一片片灵动的碎金图案时，光秃秃的枝桠是何等秀美动人；她也记得，远山上的赭红与黛蓝是何等绮丽妖娆，画眉鸟又是如何俏皮地婉转啼鸣：“快来吻我！”她再次看见了依然悬挂在红栎树枝头、微微颤动的淡绯红树叶，以及盛开在荒原上、给大地披上一件瑰丽紫晶华服的初绽石楠。
马丹-罗热那刺耳的嗓音猝然将她拉回了残酷恐怖的现实。
“你的冥顽不灵对你毫无益处，”他压着嗓子说道，尽管话里的暴怒已清晰可辨，“总督大人又多宽限了我一些时日，只要我乐意，我随时可以对你和你的老子网开一面。你清楚我对你开出的条件：做我的妻子，保全性命——如此一来，你的父亲便可自由返回英格兰，或者爱上哪儿去见鬼都行；否则，你们父女俩就将同此刻在南特牢狱中等死的窃贼与凶徒混杂在一起，以死囚的身份暴尸荒野。这是给你们的最后宽限，由你在体面的生路与屈辱的死路之间做出抉择。总督在等回音，而今晚他必须拿到确切的答复。”
伊冯娜这才缓缓转过头来，从容镇定地凝视着自己的仇敌。
“那个滥杀无辜妇孺的残暴刽子手，”她平静地说道，“现在就可以得到他的答复了。比起苟且偷生的奇耻大辱，我宁可选择必然降临的死亡。”

===Original===

“You seem,” he retorted, “to have lost sight of the fact that the law gives me the right to take by force that which you so obstinately refuse.”
“Have I not said,” she replied, “that death is my choice? Life with you would be a life of shame.”
“I can get a priest to marry us without your consent: and your religion forbids you to take your own life,” he said with a sneer.
To this she made no reply, but he knew that he had his answer. Smothering a curse, he resumed after a while:
“So you prefer to drag your father to death with you? Yet he has begged you to consider your decision and to listen to reason. He has given his consent to our marriage.”
“Let me see my father,” she retorted firmly, “and hear him say that with his own lips.
“Ah!” she added quickly, for at her words Martin-Roget had turned his head away and shrugged his shoulders with well-assumed indifference, “you cannot and dare not let me see him. For three days now you have kept us apart and no doubt fed us both up with your lies. My father is duc de Kernogan, Marquis de Trentemoult,” she added proudly, “he would far rather die side by side with his daughter than see her wedded to a criminal.”
“And you, my girl,” rejoined Martin-Roget coldly, “would you see your father branded as a malefactor, linked to a thief and sent to perish in the Loire?”
“My father,” she retorted, “will die as he has lived, a brave and honourable gentleman. The brand of a malefactor cannot cling to his name.
